Hopf Monads: A Survey with New Examples and Applications
Aryan Ghobadi

Abstract
We survey the theory of Hopf monads on monoidal categories, and present new examples and applications. As applications, we utilise this machinery to present a new theory of cross products, as well as analogues of the Fundamental Theorem of Hopf algebras and Radford’s biproduct Theorem for Hopf algebroids. Additionally, we describe new examples of Hopf monads which arise from Galois and Ore extensions of bialgebras. We also classify Lawvere theories whose corresponding monads on the category of sets and functions become Hopf, as well as Hopf monads on the poset of natural numbers.



2020
Mathematics Subject Classification
: Primary 16T99, 18C15, 18M05; Secondary: 03G30, 18D15

Keywords
: monads, bimonads, Hopf algebras, monoidal categories, braided categories, Hopf algebroid, bialgebroid

1
Introduction

Hopf monads were originally introduced to generalise the lifting properties of ordinary Hopf algebras in the category
\mathsf{Vec}
of vector spaces, to arbitrary monoidal categories. In
[
51
]
, Hopf monads were defined as monads which lift the monoidal structure of a monoidal category to their category of modules (or Eilenberg-Moore categories). These monads are now referred to as
bimonads
or
comonoidal monads
, whereas monads which also lift the closed structure (inner-homs) of a closed monoidal category are called Hopf monads
[
12
]
. While the purpose of the latter definition was rooted in lifting the closed structure, the ‘Hopf condition’ presented in
[
12
]
makes sense for bimonads on arbitrary monoidal categories. Additionally, this theory has garnered interest because many of the various categorical generalisations of Hopf algebras including Hopf algebroids, Hopf Polyads and Hopf categories, among others
[
8
,
9
]
can all be viewed within this framework. Hence, any results proved at the level of Hopf monads can have fruitful applications for all these other structures.

Initially, Hopf monads were defined for rigid monoidal categories
[
14
]
and various Hopf algebraic results were extended to this setting and applied in the study of tensor categories
[
13
]
and topological field theories
[
64
]
. This new point of view has also produced novel results for its simplest family of examples, namely braided Hopf algebras in rigid monoidal categories, such as the notions of Drinfeld double and quasitriangular structures
[
15
,
16
]
. While some of the classical Hopf algebraic results, including the Fundamental Theorem of Hopf algebras and Radford’s biproduct Theorem, were also generalised to the setting of Hopf monads on arbitrary monoidal categories
[
12
]
, other results such as the theory of integrals have not received the same treatment. This is partly due to the lack of explicit examples when the category is not rigid. In the same vein, even some of the results which have been generalised to this setting have not been translated into the language of interesting examples of Hopf monads, such as Hopf algebroids.

Here, we present a survey-style review of the theory of Hopf monads and bimonads with a particular focus on constructing new examples of these objects. While collecting the various results in the theory of Hopf monads, which are spread between
[
14
,
12
,
16
,
60
]
among other texts, we also compare them with their classical analogues in the theory of ordinary Hopf algebras and apply some of them in the setting of Hopf algebroids. We also aim to provide simpler sketches of the proofs of these results, which provide the reader with a better picture of the proof and avoid unnecessary details.

The book
[
9
]
along with several surveys including
[
65
]
formulate numerous generalisations of Hopf algebraic structures as examples of Hopf monads, but do not review the various results on Hopf monads themselves. In addition to providing a survey of these results, we take a different approach to examples. Throughout Section
4
, we pick several base monoidal categories and then construct examples of Hopf monads on these bases, which do not already appear as Hopf algebra-like structures in the literature. The reader can also refer to Chapter II of
[
64
]
as it contains a review of the theory of Hopf monads on rigid monoidal categories. In this survey however, we primarily focus on results and examples in the non-rigid setting.

Organisation:
In Section
2
, we review the basic categorical machinery which we will use and review some key features of some well-known monoidal categories. In Section
3
, we recall the notion of bimonads and the various definitions of Hopf monads and review their fundamental properties. In Section
5
, we review the different ways in which we can combine these structures to obtain new Hopf monads, while in Section
7
, we review some well-known results in the theory of Hopf algebras which have been generalised to the setting of Hopf monads. In Section
6
we recall the correspondence between Hopf monads and cocommutative central coalgebras. The novel portions of our work mainly appear in Section
4
where we present several new examples of Hopf monads by looking at poset categories, algebraic theories and Galois extensions of bialgebras. The various new applications of Hopf monads to the theory of Hopf algebroids are spread within Sections
5
,
6
and
7
as examples. Finally, we mention some aspects of Hopf monads which we have not discussed here in Section
8
and present an account of where Hopf adjunctions appear in topos theory in Appendix
A
.

Novel Constructions:
Our simplest family of examples appear in Section
4.2
, where we classify Hopf monads on the poset
(\mathbb{N}_{0},\leq)
which is viewed as a category with its monoidal structure given by
+
. We show that bimonads on this category correspond to infinite submonoids of
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)
, while Hopf monads on this category correspond to submonoids which are generated by a single positive number.

Monads on
\mathsf{Set}
are said to be generalisation of algebraic theories, in particular, because finitary monads on
\mathsf{Set}
correspond exactly to algebraic or Lawvere theories. In Section
4.3
, we show that any finitary Hopf monad must correspond to the theory of
G
-sets for a group
G
(a Hopf algebra in
\mathsf{Set}
).

In Section
4.4
, we consider extensions of bialgebras
f:B\rightarrow H
and investigate when the induced adjunction given by restriction and extension of scalars
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}\leftrightarrows{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
gives rise to a Hopf monad. We show that the (left)
pre-Hopf
condition presented in
[
12
]
corresponds to
H
being a Galois extension
[
59
]
of
B
over a coalgebra determined by
f
. Furthermore, we present a generalised Galois condition (
51
) for
f
which determines when the adjunction is (left) Hopf. We then show that any suitable Ore extension of bialgebras satisfies this condition.

In
[
27
]
, we presented a construction for Hopf monads corresponding to pivotal pairs
(P,Q)
in suitable closed monoidal categories. In Section
4.5
, we review this construction and show that we still obtain a Hopf monad under more general assumptions where the base category is not necessarily closed, Theorem
4.15
. We already noted in
[
27
]
, that this construction can be viewed as an example of Shimizu’s Tannaka-Krein reconstruction for Hopf monads
[
60
]
. In Section
7.4
, we review the latter theory and use our example in Section
4.5
to clarify different aspects of this complicated machinery.

In Section
4.6
, we review how Hopf algebroids and bialgebroids over a base algebra
A
can be viewed as Hopf monads and bimonads over the category of
A
-bimodules. While this point of view is well-known, various useful results for Hopf monads have not been translated into the language of Hopf algebroids. For instance, in Example
5.3
we translate the theory of cross products of Hopf monads to the setting of Hopf algebroids and present an analogous construction to the cross product of Hopf algebras in this setting. We present three more such results in Examples
6.5
,
7.6
and
7.4
, which include the construction of a lax braiding on the induced coalgebra of Hopf algebroids and analogues of the Fundamental Theorem of Hopf algebras and Radford’s biproduct Theorem for these structures.

Acknowledgements:
The author would like to thank Shahn Majid for numerous helpful conversations on all things Hopf-related. We would also like to show gratitude to Ivan Tomas̆ić for useful discussions on Section
4.3
and Appendix
A
, as well as Shu Sasaki and Tomasz Brzeziński for their examination of the author’s dissertation
[
28
]
, which contained a large portion of this work in its first two chapters. The author is also grateful to the EPSRC for grant number EP/W522508/1 which supported part of this work.

2
Categorical Background

In this section we recall some of the categorical concepts which we will use in future chapters and set our notation. Our main references for basic category theory will be
[
39
,
54
]
.

2.1
Monoidal Categories

In this section we briefly recall the theory of monoidal categories and set our notation. We refer the reader to Section 1 of
[
64
]
for additional details.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a quadruple
(\otimes,\mathtt{1},\alpha,l,r)
is called a monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathtt{1}
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
,
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a bifunctor and
\alpha:(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})
,
l:\mathtt{1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
r:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathtt{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
natural isomorphisms satisfying coherence axioms as presented in Section 1.2.1 of
[
64
]
. Given such a structure
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathtt{1})
is referred to as a
monoidal category
and
\mathtt{1}
is called its
monoidal unit
. The monoidal structure is said to be
strict
if
\alpha,l
and
r
are all identity morphisms. Given any monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathtt{1})
we obtain another monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes^{\mathrm{op}},\mathtt{1})
where the bifunctor
\otimes^{\mathrm{op}}
is defined by
X\otimes^{\mathrm{op}}Y=Y\otimes X
. This construction is distinct to the opposite category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
which has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
but morphisms in reversed directions.

Throughout this work, every monoidal category will either be strict or the coherence isomorphisms
\alpha,l,r
, while not the identity morphisms, will be trivial e.g. in the case of the category of sets,
\mathsf{Set}
, the function
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:(X\times Y)\times Z\rightarrow X\times(Y\times Z)
sends every element
((x,y),z)
to
(x,(y,z))
. Hence, the effect of these natural isomorphisms will be negligible and we will not discuss them further.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
between monoidal categories
(\mathcal{C},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}},\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{D},\otimes_{\mathcal{D}},\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{D}})
is said to be
(strong) monoidal
if there exist a pair
(F_{2},F_{0})
where
F_{2}(-,-):F(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}F(-)\rightarrow F(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)
is a natural (isomorphism) transformation and
F_{0}:\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow F(\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}})
a (isomorphism) morphism satisfying

\displaystyle F_{2}(X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}Y,Z)(F_{2}(X,Y)\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{id}_{F(Z)})=

\displaystyle F_{2}(X,Y\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}Z)(\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}F_{2}(Y,Z))

(1)

\displaystyle F_{2}(X,\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}})(\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}F_{0})=\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}

\displaystyle=F_{2}(\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}},X)(F_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{id}_{F(X)})

(2)

for any three objects
X,Y,Z
in
\mathcal{C}
. The functor
F
is said to be
(strong) comonoidal
if there exist a pair
(F_{2},F_{0})
with arrows going in the opposite direction. From here onwards, we will omit the subscript denoting the ambient category e.g. replace
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}
by
\otimes
, since the choice of monoidal structure will be clear from context. Note that a strong monoidal structure
(F_{2},F_{0})
on a functor
F
is equivalent to a strong comonoidal structure
(F_{0}^{-1},F_{2}^{-1})
on
F
. A strong monoidal functor with
F_{2}=\mathrm{id}_{-\otimes-}
and
F_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathtt{1}}
is called
strict monoidal
. The reader should also note that in many sources the term monoidal functor refers to a strong monoidal functor, but we choose to match the terminology used in the main Hopf monad literature
[
14
,
12
]
.

The composition
GF
of two functors
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and
G:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
with (co)monoidal structures
(F_{2},F_{0})
and
(G_{2},G_{0})
obtains a natural (co)monoidal structure by
(GF_{2})G_{2}(F,F)
and
(GF_{0})G_{0}
(resp.
G_{2}(F,F)GF_{2}
and
G_{0}(GF_{0})
). A natural transformation
\theta:F\Rightarrow G
between two (co)monoidal functors
(F,F_{2},F_{0})
and
(G,G_{2},G_{0})
is said to be
(co)monoidal
if
\theta F_{2}=G_{2}(\theta\otimes\theta)
and
\theta_{\mathtt{1}}F_{0}=G_{0}
(resp.
(\theta\otimes\theta)F_{2}=G_{2}\theta
and
G_{0}\theta=F_{0}
) hold.

Given an object
X
in a monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathtt{1})
, we say an object
{}^{\vee}{X}
is a
left dual
of
X
, if there exist morphisms
\mathrm{ev}_{X}:{}^{\vee}{X}\otimes X\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{X}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow X\otimes{}^{\vee}{X}
such that

(\mathrm{ev}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}},\quad(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{X})(\mathrm{coev}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{X}

In such a case, we call
X
a
right dual
for
{}^{\vee}{X}
. Furthermore, a right dual of an object
X
is denoted by
X^{\vee}
, with evaluation and coevaluation maps denoted by
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}:X\otimes X^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
and
\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow X^{\vee}\otimes X
, respectively. We will refer to evaluation and coevaluation maps as such, as
duality morphisms
. We say an object
X
is
dualizable
if it has both a left dual and a right dual. Note that if a left (or right) dual object to
X
exists, then it is unique upto isomorphisms. Let
Y
and
Z
both be left dual objects to
X
, with duality morphisms
(\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{coev})
and
(\mathrm{ev}^{\prime},\mathrm{coev}^{\prime})
, then
(\mathrm{ev}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Z})(\mathrm{id}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{coev}^{\prime})
and
(\mathrm{ev}^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y})(\mathrm{id}_{Z}\otimes\mathrm{coev})
form an isomorphism between
Y
and
Z
.

The monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be left (right)
rigid
or
autonomous
if all objects have left (right) duals. If a category is both left and right rigid, we simply call it
rigid
. In the literature, when a category is said to be left (or right rigid), it is assumed that we have
chosen
a left dual and a pair of duality morphisms for every object
X
and
{}^{\vee}{X}
denotes this specific choice of left dual. Given these choices, we have a contravariant functor
{}^{\vee}{(-)}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
which sends objects
X
to their left duals
{}^{\vee}{X}
and morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
to morphisms
(\mathrm{ev}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{Y}}\otimes f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{Y}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{X})
. Similarly,
(-)^{\vee}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
defines a contravariant functor on a right rigid category.

We call a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}

left (right) closed
if for every object
X
there exists an endofunctor
[X,-]^{l}
(resp.
[X,-]^{r}
) on
\mathcal{C}
which is right adjoint to
-\otimes X
(resp.
X\otimes-
). We will denote the unit and counit of these adjunctions by
\mathrm{coev}^{-}_{X}
(resp.
\underline{\mathrm{coev}}^{-}_{X}
) and
\mathrm{ev}^{-}_{X}
(resp.
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}^{-}_{X}
). By definition
[-,-]^{l},[-,-]^{r}:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
become bifunctors and we refer to them as
inner-homs
. If a category is left and right closed we call it
closed
. Observe that if
X
has a left (right) dual
{}^{\vee}{X}
(resp.
X^{\vee}
), the functor
-\otimes{}^{\vee}{X}
(resp.
X^{\vee}\otimes-
) becomes right adjoint to
-\otimes X
(resp.
X\otimes-
) and therefore every left (right) rigid category is left (right) closed. Furthermore, if
X
has a left (right) dual,
{}^{\vee}{X}\cong[X,\mathtt{1}]^{l}
(resp.
X^{\vee}\cong[X,\mathtt{1}]^{r}
). We have adopted the notation of
[
12
]
here, and what we refer to as a left closed structure is referred to as a right closed structure in some of our other references such as
[
56
]
.

It is well-known that strong monoidal functors preserve dual objects i.e.
F({}^{\vee}{X})\cong{}^{\vee}{F(X)}
since
F_{0}^{-1}F(\mathrm{ev})F_{2}({}^{\vee}{X},X)
and
F_{2}^{-1}(X,{}^{\vee}{X})F(\mathrm{coev})F_{0}
act as the evaluation and coevaluation morphisms making
F({}^{\vee}{X})
a left dual object to
F(X)
.

Given a monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
between a pair of left closed monoidal categories,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we obtain a canonical family of morphisms
\Phi^{l}_{X,Y}:=F(\mathrm{ev}_{X}^{Y})F_{2}([X,Y]^{l}_{\mathcal{C}},X):F[X,Y]_{\mathcal{C}}^{l}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}FX\rightarrow FY
for every pair of objects
X,Y
in
\mathcal{C}
. Consequently we obtain a family of morphisms

F_{X,Y}^{l}:=[F(X),\Phi^{l}_{X,Y}]^{l}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{coev}_{F(X)}^{F[X,Y]^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}}:F[X,Y]^{l}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow[F(X),F(Y)]^{l}_{\mathcal{D}}

as the corresponding morphism to
\Phi^{l}_{X,Y}
under the adjunction
-\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}F(X)\dashv[F(X),-]^{l}_{\mathcal{D}}
. We say that the monoidal functor
F
is
left closed
if the morphisms
F^{l}_{X,Y}
are isomorphisms for all pair of objects
X,Y
in
\mathcal{C}
. A symmetric definition can be made for
right closed
functors. We refer the reader to Section 3.2 of
[
12
]
for additional details.

2.2
Braided Monoidal Categories and Hopf Algebras

In this section we briefly recall the definitions of braided monoidal categories and braided bialgebras and Hopf algebras in them, which appeared in the work of Majid
[
41
]
under the name of braided groups. We refer the reader to Section II.6 of
[
64
]
for additional details and a modern treatment.

A monoidal category is said to be (lax)
braided
if there exists a natural isomorphism (natural transformation)
\Psi_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow Y\otimes X
satisfying what we refer to as the braiding axioms:

\displaystyle\Phi_{X,Y\otimes Z}=(\mathrm{id}_{Y}\otimes\Phi_{X,Z})(\Phi_{X,Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Z}),\quad\Phi_{X\otimes Y,Z}=(\Phi_{X,Z}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y})(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\Phi_{Y,Z})

(3)

A braided monoidal category is said to be symmetric if
\Phi_{Y,X}\Phi_{X,Y}=\mathrm{id}_{X\otimes Y}
for every pair of objects
X,Y
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Note that in a braided monoidal category, an object is left dualisable if and only if it is right dualisable. If
{}^{\vee}{X}
denotes the left dual of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, with duality morphisms
\mathrm{coev}
and
\mathrm{ev}
then
\mathrm{ev}\Psi_{X,{}^{\vee}{X}}
and
\Psi^{-1}_{X,{}^{\vee}{X}}\mathrm{coev}
make
{}^{\vee}{X}
a right dual of
X
. The converse argument also holds, making
X^{\vee}
a left dual of
X
. More generally,
\Psi_{-,X}:-\otimes X\rightarrow X\otimes-
is an isomorphism of functors and thereby a braided monoidal category is left closed if and only if its is right closed.

An
algebra
or
monoid
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a triple
(M,\mu,\eta)
, where
M
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mu:M\otimes M\rightarrow M
and
\eta:1\rightarrow M
are morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
which satisfy
\mu(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\eta)=\mathrm{id}_{M}=\mu(\eta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})
and
\mu(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\mu)=\mu(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})
. A
coalgebra
or
comonoid
in
\mathcal{C}
can be defined by simply reversing the arrows in the definition of a monoid.

A
braided bialgebra
in a braided monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
consists of an object
B
in
\mathcal{C}
, and quadruple of morphisms
(m,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon)
, such that
(B,m,\eta)
form a monoid in
\mathcal{C}
,
(B,\Delta,\epsilon)
form a comonoid in
\mathcal{C}
and the following axioms hold

\displaystyle(m\otimes m)(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi_{B,B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\Delta\otimes\Delta)=\Delta

\displaystyle m

(4)

\displaystyle\Delta\eta=\eta\otimes\eta,\quad\epsilon m=(\epsilon\otimes\epsilon),\quad\epsilon\eta=\mathrm{id}_{\mathtt{1}}

(5)

A braided bialgebra is called a
braided Hopf algebra
if there exists a morphism
S:B\rightarrow B
called its
antipode
such that
m(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes S)\Delta=\eta\epsilon=m(S\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\Delta
. If
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
is a symmetric category then we simply omit the “braided” prefix when referring to bialgebras and Hopf algebras in
\mathcal{C}
.

Given any monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, we can define the category of monoids in
\mathcal{C}
which has monoids
(M,m,\eta)
in
\mathcal{C}
as objects and morphisms
f:(M,m,\eta)\rightarrow(M^{\prime},m^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are morphisms
f:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
which commute with the structural morphisms i.e. satisfy
m^{\prime}(f\otimes f)=fm
and
f\eta=\eta^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is endowed with a braiding
\Psi
, then the category of monoids in
\mathcal{C}
obtains a monoidal structure defined by

(M,m,\eta)\otimes(M^{\prime},m^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=\big(M\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}M^{\prime},(m\otimes m^{\prime})(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\Psi_{M,M^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M^{\prime}}),\eta\otimes\eta^{\prime}\big)

We can define the category of comonoids in
\mathcal{C}
and its monoidal structure, when
\mathcal{C}
is braided, in a symmetric way. From this point of view, picking out a bialgebra in
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to picking a comonoid (monoid) in the monoidal category of monoids (comonoids) in
\mathcal{C}
.

2.3
Dual of a Monoidal Functor and Center

In this section we recall the definition of the dual of a monoidal functor and the center of a monoidal category with
[
40
]
as our main reference. We also present some basic results regarding the dual construction which do not appear together elsewhere.

If
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a strong monoidal functor between monoidal categories, the
dual
of the functor
U
is defined as the category whose objects are pairs
(X,\tau)
with
X
being an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
\tau:X\otimes U(-)\rightarrow U(-)\otimes X
a natural isomorphism satisfying

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{U(M)}\otimes\tau_{N})(\tau_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U(N)})=

\displaystyle(U_{2}(M,N)^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\tau_{M\otimes N}(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes U_{2}(M,N))

(6)

\displaystyle(U_{0}^{-1}\otimes

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{X})\tau_{\mathtt{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes U_{0})=\mathrm{id}_{X}

(7)

with morphisms
f:(X,\tau)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
being morphisms
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
which satisfy
\tau^{\prime}(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes f)\tau
. We denote the dual by
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
. The dual of the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, is called the
center
of
\mathcal{C}
and denoted by
Z(\mathcal{C})
. This construction is often referred to as the Drinfeld-Majid center.

The
lax left
dual of
U
, denoted by
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
is defined exactly as
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
but where the natural transformations
\tau
are not assumed to be isomorphisms. In a symmetric manner, we can define the
lax right
dual of
U
, whose objects are pairs
(X,\tau)
where
\tau:U(-)\otimes X\rightarrow X\otimes U(-)
is a natural transformation. The lax left (right) dual of the identity functor on
\mathcal{C}
is called the lax left (right) center of
\mathcal{C}
and is denoted by
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
(
Z_{r,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
). In
[
57
]
,
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
is referred to as the
weak left centralizer
of
U
and denoted by
\mathcal{W}_{l}(U)
.

It is easy to check that the dual
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
carries a monoidal structure via

(X,\tau)\otimes(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}):=(X\otimes X^{\prime},(\tau\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{\prime}})(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\tau^{\prime}))

(8)

with the pair
(\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{id}_{U(-)})
acting as the monoidal unit. Furthermore, the forgetful functor
\overline{U}:\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
defined by
\overline{U}(X,\tau)=X
becomes strict monoidal. Thereby, the dual construction actually sends a strong monoidal functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
with codomain
\mathcal{C}
to another such functor
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

The notion of lax left (right) dual of a functor
U
becomes equivalent to the dual of
U
when
\mathcal{D}
is right (left) rigid:

Theorem 2.1
.

If
\mathcal{D}
is a right rigid category then
(_{\mathcal{D}}U_{\mathcal{C}})^{\circ}=(_{\mathcal{D}}U_{\mathcal{C}})^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
.

Proof.

Let
M
be an object of
\mathcal{D}
and
M^{\vee}
denote its right dual. Since
U
is strong monoidal then following our discussion in Section
2.1
,
U(M^{\vee})
becomes a right dual of
U(M)
via
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}^{\prime}=U_{0}^{-1}U(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{M})U_{2}(M,M^{\vee})
and
\underline{\mathrm{coev}}^{\prime}=U_{2}^{-1}(M^{\vee},M)U(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{M})U_{0}
. Hence, for any object
(X,\tau)
in
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
, we can define
\tau^{-1}_{M}
by
(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X\otimes M})\tau_{M^{\vee}}(\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes X}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}^{\prime})
. It follows from
\tau
being natural and satisfying (
6
) and (
7
) that
\tau_{M}
and
\tau_{M}^{-1}
are inverses. Consequently, for any
(X,\tau)
in
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
, the transformation
\tau
is an isomorphism and
(_{\mathcal{D}}U_{\mathcal{C}})^{\circ}=(_{\mathcal{D}}U_{\mathcal{C}})^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}

∎

Now we observe that dualities between objects in the base category lift to the monoidal dual.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
X
be a left dualizable object of
\mathcal{C}
and
{}^{\vee}{X}
denote its left dual. If
(X,\tau)
is an object of
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
, then there exists a natural braiding on
\rho
such that
({}^{\vee}{X},\rho)
becomes an object of
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
and the left dual of
(X,\tau)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{ev}_{X}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{X}
denote the relevant duality morphisms between
X
and
{}^{\vee}{X}
. We define
\rho_{M}:=(\mathrm{ev}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}}\otimes\tau^{-1}_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{coev}_{X})
. It follows by definition that
\mathrm{ev}_{X}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{X}
become morphisms in
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
.
∎

In
[
57
]
, a stronger statement is proved:

Theorem 2.3

(Proposition 3.1
[
57
]
)
.

If
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a strong monoidal functor,
\mathcal{C}
is left closed and
\mathcal{D}
right rigid then
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
has a left closed structure which
U
preserves.

The center of a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is of particular interest since it has a braided structure
\Psi
defined by
\Psi_{(X,\tau),(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}=\tau_{X^{\prime}}
. A
central bialgebra (Hopf algebra)
in
\mathcal{C}
refers to an object
(B,\tau)
in
Z(\mathcal{C})
with a braided bialgebra (Hopf algebra) structure.

At this point we reflect on the fact that, under suitable conditions, the dual of a monoidal functor lifts colimits from the base category.

Theorem 2.4
.

If
-\otimes-
in
\mathcal{C}
preserves colimits in both entries, the forgetful functor
\overline{U}:\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
creates colimits which exist in
\mathcal{C}
. The same statement holds if we consider
\overline{U}:\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

Proof.

Consider a diagram
\mathbb{D}:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
so that the diagram
\overline{U}\mathbb{D}:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
has a colimit
A
in
\mathcal{C}
with a family of universal morphisms
\pi_{j}:\overline{U}\mathbb{D}(j)\rightarrow A
for objects
j
in
\mathcal{J}
. Since
\mathbb{D}:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
is a functor, for any object
X\in\mathcal{D}
we have a family of morphisms
\sigma^{j}_{X}:\mathbb{D}(j)\otimes U(X)\rightarrow U(X)\otimes\mathbb{D}(j)
which are natural with respect to
\mathcal{J}
and thereby form a natural transformation
\sigma:\mathbb{D}\otimes U(X)\Rightarrow U(X)\otimes\mathbb{D}
. Because the bifunctor
-\otimes-
in
\mathcal{C}
preserves colimits in both components, the diagrams
\overline{U}\mathbb{D}\otimes U(X)
and
U(X)\otimes\overline{U}\mathbb{D}
admit colimits
A\otimes U(X)
and
U(X)\otimes A
, respectively. By the universal property of
A\otimes U(X)
, there exists a unique morphism
\sigma^{A}_{X}
such that
\sigma^{A}_{X}(\pi_{j}\otimes U(X))=(U(X)\otimes\pi_{j})\sigma^{j}_{X}
. If we show that
(A,\sigma_{A})
is indeed an object of
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
, then it follows that
\pi:\mathbb{D}\Rightarrow(A,\sigma_{A})
becomes a cocone of the diagram
\mathbb{D}
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{D}
. To show that
(U(f)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\sigma^{A}_{X}=\sigma^{A}_{Y}(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes U(f))
, we observe that

\displaystyle\sigma^{A}_{Y}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes U(f))(\pi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U(X)})=\sigma^{A}_{Y}(\pi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U(Y)})(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}(j)}\otimes U(f))

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{U(Y)}\otimes\pi_{j})\sigma^{j}_{Y}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}(j)}\otimes U(f))=(\mathrm{id}_{U(Y)}\otimes\pi_{j})(U(f)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}(j)})\sigma^{j}_{X}

\displaystyle=(U(f)\otimes A)(\mathrm{id}_{U(X)}\otimes\pi_{j})\sigma^{j}_{X}=(U(f)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\sigma^{A}_{X}(\pi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U(X)})

holds and use the universal property of
(\pi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U(X)})
.

Hence,
(A,\sigma_{A})
is an object of
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
and a cocone of the diagram
\mathbb{D}
via
\pi_{j}
. Consider another cocone
\kappa:\mathbb{D}\Rightarrow(B,\sigma_{B})
. Since
A
is a colimit of
\overline{U}\mathbb{D}
, there exists a unique morphism
t:A\rightarrow B
such that
\overline{U}(\kappa)=t\overline{U}(\pi)
. What remains to be shown is whether
t
is a morphism in
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{U}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
. This also follows from the universality of
A\otimes U(X)
and the calculation below

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{U}\otimes t)\sigma^{A}(\overline{U}(\pi)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{U}\otimes t\overline{U}(\pi))\sigma^{j}=(\mathrm{id}_{U}\otimes\overline{U}(\kappa))\sigma^{j}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{B}(\overline{U}(\kappa)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})=\sigma^{B}(t\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})(\overline{U}(\pi)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})

Hence
(\mathrm{id}_{U}\otimes t)\sigma^{A}=\sigma^{B}(t\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})
and thereby,
(A,\sigma_{A})
is a colimit of the original diagram
\mathbb{D}
.

If
\sigma^{j}
were invertible, it follows from the universal property of
U(X)\otimes A
that there exists a unique morphism
(\sigma^{A})^{-1}
such that
(\sigma^{A})^{-1}(\mathrm{id}_{U}\otimes\pi)=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})(\sigma^{j})^{-1}
. It follows from the universal properties of
U(X)\otimes A
and
A\otimes U(X)
that
\sigma^{A}_{X}
and
(\sigma^{A}_{X})^{-1}
are inverses.
∎

The reader should note that the proof of Theorem
2.4
can be replicated in a symmetric manner for any limits in
\mathcal{C}
which are preserved in both entries of
-\otimes-
.

2.4
Key Examples of Monoidal Categories

In this section we review some key examples of monoidal categories and their properties.

Example 2.5
.

Given any category
\mathcal{C}
, we obtain a new category denoted by
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
, which has endofunctors
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
as objects and natural transformations between them as morphisms. The category of endofunctors has a canonical monoidal structure via composition of functors i.e.
F\otimes G=FG
for endofunctors
F,G
and the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
acting as the monoidal unit. In this monoidal category, a functor
F
being left (right) dual to
G
, is exactly equivalent to
F
being left (right) adjoint to
G
. Additionally, the right adjoint functor
[F,-]^{l}
to
-\otimes F:\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
, if it exists, is usually denoted by
\mathrm{Ran}_{F}
and
[F,G]^{l}
is called the
right Kan extension
of
G
along
F
, see Proposition 6.1.5 of
[
54
]
. We also have a trivial description of the center of any endofunctor category:

Proposition 2.6
.

Given any non-empty category
\mathcal{C}
, the center of the monoidal category
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
is equivalent to the trivial category.

Proof.

For any object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exists an endofunctor
F_{X}
which sends all objects to
X
and all morphisms to
\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Let
(G,\tau)
belong to
Z(\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}))
. It follows that for every object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
we obtain an isomorphism
\tau_{F_{X}}:GF_{X}\rightarrow F_{X}G=F_{X}
. Hence, we have a family of isomorphisms
\tau_{F_{X}}:G(X)\cong X
for objects
X
in
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, natural transformations
F_{f}:F_{X}\rightarrow F_{Y}
are in correspondence with morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
. Since
\tau
is a natural then for any morphism
f
the equality
f\tau_{F_{X}}=\tau_{F_{Y}}G(f)
holds and
\tau_{F_{-}}:G\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
becomes a natural isomorphism of functors. Hence, every pair
(G,\tau)
is isomorphic to
(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{id})
via
\tau_{F_{-}}
and the skeleton of
Z(\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}))
is the trivial category.
∎

Example 2.7
.

Any category with finite products obtains a natural symmetric monoidal structure with the product acting as
\otimes
and the final object acting as the monoidal unit. Such monoidal structures are called
cartesian
. In particular, the category of sets and functions
\mathsf{Set}
has a monoidal structure via the product of sets
\times
and the set with one element
\mathtt{1}=\{\star\}
acting as the monoidal unit. The symmetric structure is given by the flip map
\Psi_{X,Y}=\mathfrak{flip}
which sends a pair
(x,y)\in X\times Y
to
(y,x)\in Y\times X
. The only dualizable object in
\mathsf{Set}
is
\mathtt{1}
since
\mathrm{coev}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow X\times{}^{\vee}{X}
would have to be surjective on its projections to
{}^{\vee}{X}
and
X
but its image contains exactly one element, so
X\cong{}^{\vee}{X}\cong\mathtt{1}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

There exists a monoidal equivalence
Z(\mathsf{Set})\simeq\mathsf{Set}
.

Proof.

Let
(X,\tau)
be an object of
Z(\mathsf{Set})
. For any set
Y
and element
y\in Y
, we have a unique morphism
f_{y}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow Y
sending the element
\star
to
y
. By the naturality of
\tau
and (
7
) it follows that
\tau_{Y}(\mathrm{id}_{X}\times f_{y})=(f_{y}\times\mathrm{id}_{X})\mathrm{id}_{\mathtt{1}\times X}
and thereby
\tau_{Y}(x,y)=(y,x)
for any
x\in X
. Hence,
\tau
must act as the flip map and the only objects in
Z(\mathsf{Set})
are of the form
(X,\mathfrak{flip})
. ∎

Since the category
(\mathsf{Set},\times,\mathtt{1})
is symmetric monoidal, we can describe a Hopf algebra object in
\mathsf{Set}
: Let
(X,\Delta,\epsilon)
be a comonoid in
(\mathsf{Set},\times,\mathtt{1})
. Since
\epsilon:X\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
is the unique map sending all elements of
X
to the unique element in
\mathtt{1}
, then it follows from
(\epsilon\times\mathrm{id}_{X})\Delta=\mathrm{id}_{X}=(\mathrm{id}_{X}\times\epsilon)\Delta
that
\Delta(x)=(x,x)\in X\times X
for all
x\in X
. Consequently, any monoid
(X,m,\eta)
becomes a bialgebra
(X,m,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon)
with this trivial comonoid structure. The existence of an antipode for such a bialgebra is precisely equivalent to the existence of inverses for all elements. Hence, Hopf algebras in
\mathsf{Set}
are precisely groups.

Example 2.9
.

The category of vectorspaces
\mathsf{Vec}
over a base field
\mathbb{K}
is endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure via the tensor product of vectorspaces
\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
and the one dimensional vectorspace
\mathbb{K}
acting as the unit. The symmetric structure
\Psi_{V,W}:V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W\rightarrow W\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}V
is the flip map sending
v\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}w\in V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W
to
v\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}w
. The famous hom-tensor adjunction illustrates that this monoidal structure is closed with inner-homs
[V,W]
given by spaces of
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,W)
. It is well-known that a vectorspace
V
is dualizable in
\mathsf{Vec}
if and only if
V
is finite dimensional. This is because
\mathrm{coev}:\mathbb{K}\rightarrow V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}{}^{\vee}{V}
is uniquely determined by the choice of
\mathrm{coev}(1)
which must be of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}v^{*}_{i}
for some number
n
. It then follows by the definition of the duality morphisms that
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
must form a finite basis for
V
. A similar argument to Proposition
2.8
with maps
f_{v}:\mathbb{K}\rightarrow V
corresponding to
v\in V
proves that
Z(\mathsf{Vec})\simeq\mathsf{Vec}
.

Monoids in
\mathsf{Vec}
are simply
\mathbb{K}
-algebras. Comonoids
(C,\Delta,\epsilon)
in
\mathsf{Vec}
are called
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebras, or coalgebras for simplicity. We will use
Sweedler’s notation
for the
coproduct

\Delta:C\rightarrow C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}C
and write
\Delta(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}c_{(2)}
where the right hand side depicts the finite sum of elements of the form
c_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}c_{2}\in C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}C
corresponding to
c
. We recover the theory of ordinary bialgebras and Hopf algebras when looking at bialgebras and Hopf algebras in
\mathsf{Vec}
with this symmetric monoidal structure and refer the reader to
[
46
]
for more details on these.

Example 2.10
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. The category of
A
-bimodules has a natural monoidal structure by tensoring bimodules over the algebra
A
, denoted by
\otimes_{A}
, and the algebra
A
, regarded as an
A
-bimodule, acting as the unit object. It is well-known that a bimodule has a left (right) dual in the monoidal category
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
if and only if it is finitely generated and projective,
fgp
for short, as a right (left)
A
-module. In particular,
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is closed with

\displaystyle[M,N]^{l}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N),\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle[afb](m)=af(bm),\quad f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N)

\displaystyle[M,N]^{r}:=\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,N),\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle[agb](m)=g(ma)b,\quad g\in\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,N)

where
a,b\in A
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N)
and
\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}(M,N)}
denote the vectorspaces of right and left
A
-module morphisms from
M
to
N
, respectively. Explicitly, the units and counits of the adjunctions for the left and right closed structures are given by

\textstyle{\varrho^{M}_{N}:N\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N\otimes M),}
\textstyle{\varepsilon^{M}_{N}:\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N)\otimes M\longrightarrow N}
\textstyle{\hskip 14.22636ptn\longmapsto f_{n}:(m\mapsto n\otimes m)}
\textstyle{\hskip 36.98866ptf\otimes m\longmapsto f(m)}

\textstyle{\Theta^{M}_{N}:N\longrightarrow\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,M\otimes N),}
\textstyle{\hskip-7.11317pt\Pi^{M}_{N}:M\otimes\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,N)\longrightarrow N}
\textstyle{\hskip 14.22636ptn\longmapsto g_{n}:(m\mapsto m\otimes n)}
\textstyle{\hskip 31.2982ptm\otimes g\longmapsto g(m)}

for any pair of
A
-bimodules
M
and
N
. Consequently, for a right or left fgp bimodule
M
, we identify
{}^{\vee}{M}
by
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,A)
and
M^{\vee}
by
\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}(M,A)}
.
Note that the category
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is generally not braided. For a discussion of when
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
admits a braiding we refer the reader to
[
2
]
. Several characterisations of the center of
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
are also provided in
[
1
]
.

Since the category of bimodules does not admit a braiding in general, we can not discuss Hopf algebra objects in this category. However, the role of Hopf algebras and bialgebras in this setting is taken by Hopf algebroids and bialgebroids which we will discuss further in Section
4.6
.

2.5
Monads

In this section we recall the theory of monads, with Chapter VI of
[
39
]
as our main reference.

A
monad
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a monoid in its monoidal category of endofunctors
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
. Explicitly, a monad consists of a triple
(T,\mu,\eta)
, where
T
is an endofunctor on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mu:TT\rightarrow T
and
\eta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow T
are natural transformations satisfying
\mu T\mu=\mu\mu_{T}
,
\mu T\eta=\mathrm{id}_{T}=\mu\eta_{T}
. Morphisms of monads
\theta:(T,\mu,\eta)\rightarrow(T,\mu^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
can be defined accordingly as natural transformations
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
satisfying
\theta\eta=\eta^{\prime}
and
\theta\mu=\mu^{\prime}(T^{\prime}\theta)\theta_{T}
.

Any monad gives rise to an adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{T}
is the
Eilenberg-Moore category
associated to
T
. The category
\mathcal{C}^{T}
has pairs
(X,r)
, where
X
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
and
r:TX\rightarrow X
a
T
-
action
satisfying
r\mu_{X}=rTr
and
r\eta=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, as its objects and morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
which commute with the
T
-actions as its morphisms. The
free
functor
F_{T}
acts as
F_{T}(X)=(TX,\mu_{X})
and the
forgetful
functor
U_{T}
acts by
U_{T}(X,r)=X
. We will call every pair
(X,r)
a
T
-
module
and will sometimes refer to the Eilenberg-Moore category as the category of
T
-modules. In the converse direction, any adjunction
F\dashv G:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a monad
(GF,\eta,G\epsilon_{F})
where
\eta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow GF
and
\epsilon:FG\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
denote the unit and counit of the adjunction, respectively. We also obtain a natural functor
K:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
defined by
K(d)=(Gd,G\epsilon_{d})
. This functor is called the
comparison functor
and satisfies
U_{T}K=G
and
KF=F_{T}
. We say the functor
G
is
monadic
if
K
is an equivalence of categories.

Now we recall Beck’s Theorem. The proof of this theorem and several equivalent formulations of it can be found in Section VI. 7 of
[
39
]
.

Theorem 2.11

(Beck’s Theorem)
.

Given an adjunction
F\dashv G:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
,
G
is monadic if and only if the functor
G
creates coequalizers for parallel pairs
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
for which
Gf,Gg
has a split coequalizer.

Split coequalizers should not to be confused with reflexive coequalizers. A pair
Gf,Gg
has a
split
coequalizer if there exists an object
C
in
\mathcal{C}
along with morphisms
s:C\rightarrow GY
,
h:GY\rightarrow GX
and
t:GY\rightarrow C

such that
t
is a coequalizer and
ts=\mathrm{id}_{C}
,
(Gg)h=\mathrm{id}_{GY}
and
(Gf)h=st
hold. A coequalizer of a parallel pair
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
is called
reflexive
if there exists a morphism
h
such that
hf=hg=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. The importance of reflexive coequalizers within the theory of monads was described in a theorem of Linton
[
38
]
, which concerns the existence of colimits in the category of
T
-modules. We will refer to reflexive coequalizers and coreflexive equalisers by RCs and CEs, respectively.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category, then a monoid structures
(A,m,\eta)
on an object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a monad structures on the endofunctor
A\otimes-
with
\mu=m\otimes-
. The Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathcal{C}^{T}
in this case becomes the category of left
A
-modules i.e. objects
X
with an action
r:A\otimes X\rightarrow X
. The free functor
F_{T}
sends an object
X
to the free module generated by it
(A\otimes X,m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})
and the forgetful functor
U_{T}
sends a module
(X,r)
to the object
X
.

The notation for monads is slightly confusing when compared to algebras. We usually denote the category of (left) modules over an algebra
A
by
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}
and the category of (left) comodules over a coalgebra
C
by
{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}
, while in the monadic case, the category of modules over a monad
T
is usually written as
\mathcal{C}^{T}
and category of comodules of a comonad
S
as
\mathcal{C}_{S}
. Moreover, in many texts including
[
39
]

T
-modules are referred to as
T
-algebras
to stay consistent with the point of view that monads generalise algebraic theories and
\mathcal{C}^{T}
plays the role of the category of algebras over a theory (see Section
4.3
). Here we use the term
T
-modules to stay closer to representation theoretic language and the example
T=A\otimes-
.

Finally, we recall the following well-known fact.

Theorem 2.12
.

Given a pair of adjoint endofunctors
F\dashv G:\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
, monad structures on
F
correspond to comonad structures on
G
.

This can be seen easily from the fact that
F
and
G
are duals in the monoidal category of
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
and this duality can be used to take an algebra structure on one object to a coalgebra structure on the other, see Proposition 2.4 of
[
44
]
. Explicitly, if
\eta
and
\epsilon
denote the unit and counit of
F\dashv G
and
(F,\mu,\nu)
is a monad structure, then
(GG\epsilon)(GG\mu_{G})(G\eta_{FG})\eta_{G}
and
\epsilon\nu_{G}
provide a comonad structure on
G
.

2.6
Ends and Coends

In this section we briefly recall the notion of ends and coends, with Chapter IX of
[
39
]
serving as our main reference.

For categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
and bifunctors
F,G:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, a
dinatural transformation

d:F\rightarrow G
consists of a family of morphisms
d_{X}:F(X,X)\rightarrow G(X,X)
such that for any morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, we have an equality of morphisms

G(\mathrm{id}_{X},f)d_{X}F(f,\mathrm{id}_{X})=G(f,\mathrm{id}_{Y})d_{Y}F(\mathrm{id}_{Y},f):F(Y,X)\rightarrow G(X,Y)

(17)

An
end
for a bifunctor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a pair
(E,e)
consisting of an object
E
of
\mathcal{D}
and a dinatural transformation
e:E\rightarrow F
, where we regard
E
as a constant functor
E:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
sending all objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}
to
E
and all morphisms to
\mathrm{id}_{E}
, where
e
is universal in the sense that for any other such pair
(E^{\prime},e^{\prime})
, there exists a unique morphism
f:E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
satisfying
ef=e^{\prime}
. Note that for an end
(E,e)
of
F
, the equations (
17
) reduce to
F(\mathrm{id}_{X},f)e_{X}=F(f,\mathrm{id}_{Y})e_{Y}
. Dually, we can define a
coend
of
F
as a pair
(C,d)
where
d:F\rightarrow C
is a universal dinatural transformation satisfying
d_{X}F(f,\mathrm{id}_{X})=d_{Y}F(\mathrm{id}_{Y},f)
. The end and coend of
F
will be denoted by
\int_{X\in\mathcal{C}}F(X,X)
and
\int^{X\in\mathcal{C}}F(X,X)
, respectively.

Given any bifunctor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, we obtain a diagram in
\mathcal{D}
containing morphisms
F(\mathrm{id}_{Y},f)
and
F(f,\mathrm{id}_{X})
corresponding to
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}
. An end
\int_{X\in\mathcal{C}}F(X,X)
and a coend
\int^{X\in\mathcal{C}}F(X,X)
are precisely a limit and colimit for this diagram, respectively. Hence, (co)ends for a bifunctor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
exists if
\mathcal{D}
is (co)complete.

3
Hopf and Bimonads

In this section, we review the definitions and basic properties of bimonads and Hopf monads from
[
14
,
12
]
. After first reviewing the theory of bimonads, we will look at the definition of Hopf monads on rigid monoidal categories, defined in
[
14
]
. We will then review the notion of Hopf monads on general monoidal categories and closed monoidal categories, which were defined in
[
12
]
.

3.1
Bimonads and Comonoidal Adjunctions

In this section, we first recall the theory of
bimonads
or
comonoidal monads
from
[
14
]
, which first appeared in
[
47
]
under the name of
opmonoidal monads
and independently in
[
51
]
under the name of
Hopf monads
. Since these objects generalise braided bialgebras to arbitrary monoidal categories, we choose to stay consistent with the terminology of
[
14
,
12
]
and refer to them as bimonads.

Definition 3.1
.

A monad
(T,\mu,\eta)
on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be a
bimonad
or an
comonoidal monad
if it also has a compatible comonoidal structure
(T_{2},T_{0})
satisfying

\displaystyle T_{2}(X,Y)\mu_{X\otimes Y}=(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y})

\displaystyle T_{2}(T(X),T(Y))TT_{2}(X,Y)

(18)

\displaystyle T_{2}(X,Y)\eta_{X\otimes Y}=

\displaystyle\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}

(19)

\displaystyle T_{0}\mu_{\mathtt{1}}=

\displaystyle T_{0}(TT_{0})

(20)

\displaystyle T_{0}\eta_{\mathtt{1}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{\mathtt{1}}

(21)

for arbitrary objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
.

The conditions in the above definition are just stating that
\mu
and
\eta
are
comonoidal natural transformations
. A
bimonad morphism
between two bimonads is exactly a morphism between monads which is also a comonoidal natural transformation. A bimonad structure on a monad
T
is precisely the structure needed to lift the monoidal structure on the base category
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
:

Theorem 3.2

(Theorem 7.1
[
51
]
)
.

If
T
is a monad on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, then bimonad structures on
T
are in correspondence with liftings of the monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
onto
\mathcal{C}^{T}
i.e. monoidal structures on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
such that
U_{T}
is strict monoidal.

Sketch of proof.

If
T
has a compatible comonoidal structure
(T_{2},T_{0})
, then we define the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
as

(M,r)\otimes(N,s):=\left(M\otimes N,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.5746pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.5746pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T(M\otimes N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.26065pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T_{2}(M,N)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.19368pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.88414pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.19368pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T(M)\otimes T(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 136.53183pt\raise 5.58334pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r\otimes s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 155.66289pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 155.66289pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\otimes N}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)

(22)

with
\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}=(\mathtt{1},T_{0})
as the unit object. Conditions (
18
) and (
19
) ensure that (
22
) is a well-defined object in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. Conditions (
20
) and (
21
) ensure that
T_{0}
is a well-defined
T
-action on
\mathtt{1}
so that
\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}
becomes an object in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
.

In the converse direction, let us assume that
\mathcal{C}^{T}
has a well-defined monoidal structure
\overline{\otimes}
such that
U_{T}
is strict monoidal, defined by
(M,r)\overline{\otimes}(N,s)=\left(M\otimes N,{r\overline{\otimes}s}\right)
. Then consider the arbitrary free
T
-modules
(T(M),\mu_{M})
and
(T(N),\mu_{N})
and define
T_{2}(N,M)
as the composite

\textstyle{T_{2}(M,N):T(M\otimes N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\eta_{M}\otimes\eta_{N})}
\textstyle{T(T(M)\otimes T(N))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{M}\overline{\otimes}\mu_{N}}
\textstyle{T(M)\otimes T(N)}

(23)

It should be clear that due to the functoriality of
T
and
\mu T\eta=\mathrm{id}_{T}
holding, this process gives the inverse of the one describe above. For further details of this proof, we refer the reader to Theorem 7.1 in
[
51
]
.
\diamond

Now we reflect on the manner in which bimonads generalise braided bialgebras. Recall from Section
2.5
that for any algebra
(B,m,\eta)
in a braided category
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
we obtain a monad
(B\otimes-,m\otimes-,\eta\otimes-)
, which we will denote by
\mathbb{B}
. It is well-known that the category of modules over a bialgebra lifts the monoidal structure of the base category, via its coalgebra structure. From the perspective of Theorem
3.2
, the coalgebra structure of a bialgebra provides a comonoidal structure on the functor
B\otimes-
which turns
\mathbb{B}
into a bimonad. Explicitly, the comonoidal structure is defined by

\mathbb{B}_{2}(M,N)=(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi_{B,M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes N}),\quad\mathbb{B}_{0}=\epsilon

(24)

and the conditions in Definition
3.1
translate exactly to the bialgebra axioms. In this way, we recover precisely the action of a bialgebra on the tensor product of its modules from (
22
).

As we recalled in Section
2.5
, the theory of monads and adjunctions are intertwined and it is natural to expect that adjunctions which giving rise to bimonads should have a corresponding structure. These adjunctions appeared first in Section 2.4 of
[
14
]
:

Definition 3.3
.

An adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
between monoidal categories is called a
comonoidal adjunction
if
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is strong monoidal.

Lemma 3.4

(Theorem 2.6
[
14
]
)
.

If
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
is an adjunction between monoidal categories then TFAE

(I)

U
is strong comonoidal.

(II)

F
and
U
are both comonoidal and the unit and counit of the adjunction are comonoidal natural transformations.

Furthermore, if the above conditions hold then
T=UF
becomes a bimonad.

Sketch of proof.

Given a comonoidal adjunction
F\dashv U
, we obtain a comonoidal structure on
F
as follows

\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}(X,Y)}
\scriptstyle{F(\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y})}
\textstyle{F(X)\otimes F(Y)}
\textstyle{F(UF(X)\otimes UF(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{FU_{2}^{-1}(F(X),F(Y))}
\textstyle{FU(F(X)\otimes F(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{F(X)\otimes F(Y)}}

(25)

and
F_{0}=\epsilon_{\mathtt{1}}F(U_{0}^{-1})
, where
\eta
and
\epsilon
denote the unit and counit of the adjunction. It then follows by definition that
\eta
and
\epsilon
are comonoidal natural transformations.

In the converse direction if
F
and
U
are both comonoidal and the
\eta
and
\epsilon
respect these comonoidal structures, then
U
is strong comonoidal and the inverse of
U_{2}(X,Y)
is given by

\textstyle{U(X)\otimes U(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{2}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{U(X)\otimes U(Y)}}
\textstyle{U(X\otimes Y)}
\textstyle{UF(U(X)\otimes U(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{UF_{2}(U(X),U(Y))}
\textstyle{U(FU(X)\otimes FU(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U(\epsilon_{F(X)}\otimes\epsilon_{F(Y)})}

(26)

Consequently, for a comonoidal adjunction
F\dashv U
the monad
T=UF
obtains a comonoidal structure by the composition of functors and the conditions in Definition
3.1
follow from
\eta
and
\epsilon
respecting this comonoidal structure.
\diamond

In a symmetric manner to Lemma
3.4
, given a bimonad
T
we obtain a comonoidal adjunction, namely the free/forgetful adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
. We have already seen in Theorem
3.2
, that
U_{T}
is strict monoidal and carries a trivial strong comonoidal structure. Note that when the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
is cartesian, any adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
is comonoidal, since
U
preserves all limits and is, thereby, a strong monoidal functor.

We should also note that in the circumstances of Lemma
3.4
, the comparison functor
K:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
obtains a natural strong monoidal structure such that equalities
U_{T}K=U
and
KF=F_{T}
hold as equalities of comonoidal functors. Explicitly
K_{2}(X,Y)=U_{2}
and
K_{0}=U_{0}
. For further details we refer the reader to the proof of Theorem 2.6 in
[
14
]
.

Dual Notion:
The appropriate dualisation of the notion of bimonads is that of
bicomonads
or
monoidal comonads
. While a bimonad consists of a monad and a comonoidal structure, a bicomonad refers to a comonad with a compatible monoidal structure, and these compatibility conditions are obtained by reversing the morphisms in Definition
3.1
. By symmetric arguments, one can prove that the monoidal structure of the base category lifts to the category of comodules over the bicomonad. Given a bialgebra
B
in a braided category
\mathcal{C}
, we also obtain a natural bicomonad structure on the endofunctor
B\otimes-
where the coalgebra structure of
B
appears in the comonad structure and the algebra structure in the monoidal structure. This is a unique case, and in general we do not expect a single endofunctor to admit both a bimonad and a bicomonad structure.

After reviewing the connection between comonoidal monads and their lifting properties for their Eilenberg-Moore categories, there are two natural questions which arise. 1) What happens if we consider a monoidal structure on a monad? 2) Which monads lift the monoidal structure of the base category to their Kleisli categories?

Monoidal Monads:
A monoidal monad is a monad
(T,\mu,\eta)
with a monoidal structure
(T_{2},T_{0})
such that
\mu
and
\eta
become monoidal morphisms. If suitable coequalizers exist, then the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal monad obtains a natural monoidal structure
\otimes_{T}
defined as the following coequalizer:

\textstyle{T(T(X)\otimes T(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(r\otimes s)}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{M\otimes N}TT_{2}(M,N)}
\textstyle{T(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(X,r)\otimes_{T}(Y,s)}

(27)

for a pair of
T
-modules
(X,r)
and
(Y,s)
. In this setting, the free module functor
F_{T}
becomes strong monoidal. Consequently, the Kleisli category which can be identified as the subcategory of free
T
-modules of the monad lifts the monoidal structure of the base category. This monoidal structure generalises the tensor product in the category of
R
-modules for a commutative ring
R
. Lastly, the reader should note that
U_{T}F_{T}
becomes a monoidal comonad on
\mathcal{C}^{D}
. In a symmetric way, any comonoidal comonad on
\mathcal{C}
with suitable equalizers would give rise a comonoidal monad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. We will see a refined form of this relation in Section
6
for Hopf monads.

Remark 3.5
.

The theory of monoidal monads is embedded in the theory of commutative monads within the literature. A functor on a monoidal category can be equipped with two natural transformations called a strength and a costrength, which were introduced in
[
35
]
. If the base category is symmetric monoidal, we can ask for these morphisms to be compatible via the symmetry and a monad equipped with such compatible structures is called
commutative
. In fact, any monoidal monad obtains a strength and a costrength in a natural way, and monoidal monads which respect the symmetric structure are in bijection with commutative monads
[
36
]
.

3.2
Hopf Monads on Rigid Monoidal Categories

In this section we review the first definition of Hopf monads based on
[
14
]
. As we will see, the definition provided in
[
14
]
is only sensible when the base category is rigid and was later generalised to arbitrary monoidal categories in
[
12
]
.

Bialgebras in a braided monoidal category are known to lift the monoidal structure of the base category to their categories of modules. As we saw in the previous section, bimonads generalise this aspect of the theory of bialgebras. In the same direction, a bialgebra admitting an antipode and becoming a Hopf algebra implies that the duality morphisms in the base category lift to its category of modules. More concretely, let
B
be a braided bialgebra in
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
, and
{}^{\vee}{X}
denote the left dual of an object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, with duality morphisms
\mathrm{ev}
and
\mathrm{coev}
. If
B
admits an antipode
S
then for any
B
-action on
X
,
\triangleright:B\otimes X\rightarrow X
, we obtain a natural
B
-action on
{}^{\vee}{X}
by
(\mathrm{ev}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}}\otimes\triangleright(S\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})(\Psi_{B,{}^{\vee}{X}}\otimes\mathrm{coev})
. In particular,
\mathrm{ev}
and
\mathrm{coev}
respect these
B
-actions and become duality morphisms in
{}_{B}\mathcal{C}
. When
B
admits an invertible antipode, we obtain a similar action on right dual objects using
S^{-1}
. The first Hopf condition introduced in
[
14
]
ensures that the dualities in a rigid monoidal category lift to the Eilenberg-Moore category of the bimonad. Let us recall the definition of antipodes for bimonads from Section 3.3 of
[
14
]
.

Definition 3.6
.

A bimonad
T
on a left rigid monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is called a
left Hopf
monad if there exists a natural transformation
s^{l}_{X}:T({}^{\vee}{T(X)})\rightarrow{}^{\vee}{X}
satisfying

\displaystyle T_{0}T(\mathrm{ev}_{X}({}^{\vee}{\eta_{X}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}))=\mathrm{ev}_{T(X)}(s^{l}_{T(X)}T({}^{\vee}{\mu_{X}})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(X)})T_{2}({}^{\vee}{T(X)},X):T({}^{\vee}{T(X)}\otimes X)\rightarrow\mathtt{1}

\displaystyle(\mu_{X}\otimes s^{l}_{X})T_{2}(T(X),{}^{\vee}{T(X)})T(\mathrm{coev}_{T(X)})=(\eta_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}})\mathrm{coev}_{X}T_{0}:T(\mathtt{1})\rightarrow T(X)\otimes{}^{\vee}{X}

Symmetrically, a bimonad
T
on a right rigid monoidal category is called a
right Hopf
monad if there exists a natural transformation
s^{r}_{X}:T(T(X)^{\vee})\rightarrow X^{\vee}
satisfying

\displaystyle T_{0}T(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X}^{\vee}))=\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{T(X)}(\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes s^{r}_{T(X)}T(\mu_{X}^{\vee}))T_{2}(X,T(X)^{\vee}):T(X\otimes T(X)^{\vee})\rightarrow\mathtt{1}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{X^{\vee}}\otimes\eta_{X})\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}T_{0}=(s^{r}_{X}\otimes\mu_{X})T_{2}(T(X)^{\vee},T(X))T(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{T(X)}):T(\mathtt{1})\rightarrow X^{\vee}\otimes T(X)

We say a bimonad
T
on a rigid category is a
Hopf
monad if it is both left Hopf and right Hopf. The natural transformation
s^{l}
(resp.
s^{r}
) is called a left (right)
antipode
for the bimonad
T
.

This notion of antipode generalises the notion of antipode for Hopf algebras in the following way: If
\mathcal{C}
is a rigid braided monoidal category and
B
a Hopf algebra in it, then its corresponding bimonad
\mathbb{B}
, obtains a left and right antipode:

\displaystyle s^{l}_{X}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}}\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{{}^{\vee}{X}\otimes{}^{\vee}{B}}\otimes S)(\Psi_{B,{}^{\vee}{X}\otimes{}^{\vee}{B}})\longleftrightarrow S=(s^{l}_{\mathtt{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi^{-1}_{B,{}^{\vee}{B}}\mathrm{coev}_{B})

\displaystyle s^{r}_{X}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{X^{\vee}}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{B}(S^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B^{\vee}}))(\Psi_{B,X^{\vee}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B^{\vee}})\longleftrightarrow S^{-1}=(s^{r}_{\mathtt{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{B})

where we have used the isomorphisms
{}^{\vee}{(B\otimes X)}\cong{}^{\vee}{X}\otimes{}^{\vee}{B}
and
(B\otimes X)^{\vee}\cong X^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee}
. The conditions in Definition
3.6
simply require
S
to satisfy the usual antipode conditions. Note that for a braided bialgebra
B
in a rigid category, the bimonad
\mathbb{B}
being only right Hopf implies the existence of an
opantipode

S^{\prime}
in the place of
S^{-1}
. An opantipode is simply a map
S^{\prime}:B\rightarrow B
satisfying the basic properties which the inverse of an antipode would satisfy i.e.
m(S^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\Psi^{-1}_{B,B}\Delta=\eta\epsilon=m(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes S^{\prime})\Psi_{B,B}^{-1}\Delta
.

The reader should note that because the definitions of Hopf monads with antipodes require the base category to be left or right rigid, this theory generalises the theory of
dualisable
braided Hopf algebras and
finite-dimensional
Hopf algebras. For any Hopf algebra
B
in a category
\mathcal{C}
, the endofunctor
B\otimes-
does not restrict to an endofunctor on the (left or right) rigid subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
unless
B
itself is (left or right) dualisable. In particular, in the case of
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}
, we need
H
to be finite-dimensional to obtain an endofunctor on
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathrm{fd}}
.

Now we recall how the existence of left and right antipodes relates to the lifting of left and right dualities to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
:

Theorem 3.7

(Theorem 3.8
[
14
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is left (right) rigid, and
T
a bimonad on
\mathcal{C}
then
T
is left (right) Hopf if and only if
\mathcal{C}^{T}
is left (right) rigid.

Sketch of proof.

If
X
is a
T
-module with action
r:T(X)\rightarrow X
, then the left and right antipodes, if they exist, provide the following actions on
{}^{\vee}{X}
and
X^{\vee}
, receptively:

s^{l}_{X}T\left({}^{\vee}{r}\right):T({}^{\vee}{X})\rightarrow{}^{\vee}{X},\quad\quad s^{r}_{X}T(r^{\vee}):T(X^{\vee})\rightarrow X^{\vee}

(28)

Furthermore, with these actions the usual evaluation and coevaluation morphisms lift to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
as
T
-module morphisms. For further details on why the above morphisms are
T
-actions we refer the reader to Theorem 3.8 of
[
14
]
.

In the converse direction, assume an arbitrary object
(A,r)
in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
has a dual
({}^{\vee}{A},\rho_{(A,r)})
with a pair of duality morphisms
\mathrm{ev}
and
\mathrm{coev}
. Since
U
is strict monoidal then
{}^{\vee}{A}
is a left dual of
A
in
\mathcal{C}
. With this notation one can show that
\rho
is natural and the left antipode is recovered as
{}^{\vee}{\eta_{A}}\rho_{F_{T}(A)}:T({}^{\vee}{T(A)})\rightarrow{}^{\vee}{A}
. See the proof of Theorem 3.8 of
[
14
]
for more details.

\diamond

We should note that the actions described in Theorem
3.7
are unique for each choice of duality morphisms. In particular, in Lemma 3.9 of
[
14
]
it is proved that the described action on
{}^{\vee}{X}
is the unique
T
-action on
{}^{\vee}{X}
which makes both
\mathrm{coev}_{X}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{X}
into
T
-module morphisms.

As in the case of comonoidal adjunctions, one can define
Hopf adjunctions
between rigid categories. An adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is said to be (left or right) Hopf, if
U
is strong monoidal and both categories are (left or right) rigid. By Theorem
3.7
, for a (left or right) Hopf monad
T
, the free/forgetful adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
becomes such an adjunction. In the converse direction, if
F\dashv U
is a (left or right) Hopf adjunction, in Theorem 3.14 of
[
14
]
it is shown that
UF
becomes a (left or right) Hopf monad with the antipodes being obtained in a similar fashion to the proof of Theorem
3.7
. In the next section, we will encounter a more general notion of Hopf adjunction.

Given any functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
between rigid monoidal categories, we can form a pair of functors
F^{!}
and
{}^{!}F
by

F^{!}(X)=F({}^{\vee}{X})^{\vee},\hskip 56.9055pt\prescript{!}{}{F(X)}={}^{\vee}{F(X^{\vee})}

(29)

Observe that if
F
is strong monoidal then
{}^{!}F\cong F\cong F^{!}
. Furthermore, it is not hard to see that if
F\dashv U
, then
U^{!}\dashv F^{!}
and
\prescript{!}{}{U}\dashv\prescript{!}{}{F}
. With these observations in mind, consider the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
for a Hopf monad
T
on a rigid monoidal category. By Theorem
3.7
,
\mathcal{C}^{T}
is rigid and thereby there exists an adjunction
U_{T}^{!}\dashv F_{T}^{!}
. Since
U_{T}\cong U_{T}^{!}
then
U_{T}
admits a right adjoint. Furthermore, since
T^{!}=U_{T}^{!}F_{T}^{!}
and
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}\cong U_{T}^{!}\dashv F_{T}^{!}
, we obtain an adjunction
T\dashv T^{!}
. An alternate description of this adjunction comes directly from the antipodes:

Theorem 3.8

(Proposition 3.11
[
14
]
)
.

If
T
is a Hopf monad on a rigid monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, with antipodes
s^{l}
and
s^{r}
then
s^{r}_{{}^{\vee}{T(X)}}T((s^{l}_{X})^{\vee})=\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}=s^{l}_{T(X)^{\vee}}T({}^{\vee}{(s^{r}_{X})})
. In particular,
(s^{l}_{-})^{\vee}
and
s^{r}_{{}^{\vee}{(-)}}
provide the unit and counit for the adjunction
T\dashv T^{!}
.

Corollary 3.9
.

Any Hopf monad
T
on a rigid category admits a right adjoint and hence preserves colimits.

First note that when working over rigid monoidal abelian (or triangulated) categories, Corollary
3.9
allows one to classify Hopf monads using variations of the Eilenberg-Watts Theorem. The reader should also keep in mind the well-known fact that the forgetful functor
U_{T}
of a colimit preserving monad
T
creates (rather than just preserves) colimits. We also observe that a symmetric argument to Theorem
3.8
can be made to prove that
T\dashv\prescript{!}{}{T}
via
\prescript{!}{}{U_{T}}\dashv\prescript{!}{}{F_{T}}
and since adjoints are unique upto isomorphism, then
\prescript{!}{}{T}\cong T^{!}
.

Dual Notion:
Based on the adjunction
T\dashv T^{!}
and Theorem
2.12
the functor
T^{!}
obtains a comonad structure. The right adjoint
T^{!}
also obtains a monoidal structure as in Equation (
25
). In particular,
T^{!}
has a Hopf comonad structure, with a dual notion of antipodes, and its category of comodules is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. By a dual argument, we can send any Hopf comonad
T
to a Hopf monad
{}^{!}T
. Consequently, we see that for a rigid monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
,
T\leftrightarrow T^{!}
provides a correspondence between Hopf monads and Hopf comonads on
\mathcal{C}
. We should also note that bicomonads correspond to
monoidal adjunctions
where the left adjoint functor is strong monoidal .

3.3
Hopf Monads on General Monoidal Categories

In
[
12
]
, a new Hopf condition for bimonads was introduced which can be applied to arbitrary monoidal categories instead of rigid ones. In this section, we briefly review this definition and its properties.

Definition 3.10
.

Given a bimonad
T
on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, one obtains a pair of natural transformations called the left and right
fusion operators
, denoted by
H^{l}
and
H^{r}
, respectively:

\textstyle{H^{l}_{X,Y}:T(X\otimes T(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}(X,T(Y))}
\textstyle{T(X)\otimes TT(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\mu_{Y}}
\textstyle{T(X)\otimes T(Y)}

\textstyle{H^{r}_{X,Y}:T(T(X)\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}(T(X),Y)}
\textstyle{TT(X)\otimes T(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(Y)}}
\textstyle{T(X)\otimes T(Y)}

The bimonad is said to be
left (right) Hopf
, if the left (right) fusion operator is invertible.

Observe that for the bimonad
\mathbb{B}
corresponding to a bialgebra
B
in a braided category, we always have isomorphisms
\mathbb{B}(X\otimes\mathbb{B}(Y))\cong\mathbb{B}(X)\otimes\mathbb{B}(Y)
and
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{B}(X)\otimes Y)\cong\mathbb{B}(X)\otimes\mathbb{B}(Y)
via the braiding. However, these are due to the form of the functor and most importantly these isomorphisms do not provide inverses for the fusion operators. Translating the conditions of Definition
3.10
for the bimonad
\mathbb{B}=B\otimes-
, we see that the left and right fusion operators being invertible imply the invertibility of maps
\mathsf{H}_{1}=(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m)(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})=H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}=(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi_{B,B})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})=H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}}
, respectively. In turn, the invertibility of
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
correspond to
B
admitting an antipode
S
and an opantipode
S^{\prime}
, respectively:

\displaystyle S=(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\mathsf{H}_{1}^{-1}(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\eta)\longleftrightarrow\mathsf{H}_{1}^{-1}=(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m)(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes S\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})

\displaystyle S^{\prime}=(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\mathsf{H}_{2}^{-1}(\eta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\longleftrightarrow\mathsf{H}_{2}^{-1}=(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m)(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes S^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\Psi_{B,B}^{-1}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\Psi_{B,B}^{-1}

As far as the author is aware, this interpretation of the map
\mathsf{H}_{1}
first appeared in
[
61
]
, where it was shown that
\mathsf{H}_{1}
satisfies the
fusion equation
. Due to the above correspondence, the invertibility of
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
becomes equivalent to the invertibility of the left and right fusion operators of
\mathbb{B}
:

\displaystyle(H^{l}_{M,N})^{-1}=(\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes M}\otimes m(S\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi_{B,M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes N})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes B\otimes N})

\displaystyle(H^{r}_{M,N})^{-1}=\big((\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m)(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes S^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\Psi_{B,B}^{-1}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes N}\big)(\Psi_{B\otimes M,B}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})

As in previous sections, we now recall the appropriate notion for a comonoidal adjunction to be Hopf. For a comonoidal adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
, we can define analogous
fusion operators
:

\textstyle{\overline{H}^{l}_{X,Y}:F(X\otimes U(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}(X,U(Y))}
\textstyle{F(X)\otimes FU(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}\otimes\epsilon_{Y}}
\textstyle{F(X)\otimes Y}

\textstyle{\overline{H}^{r}_{X,Y}:F(U(X)\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}(U(X),Y)}
\textstyle{FU(X)\otimes F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{F(Y)}}
\textstyle{X\otimes F(Y)}

where
X
and
Y
are objects of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, and
\epsilon
is the counit of the adjunction. Such an adjunction with an invertible left (right) fusion operator is called a left (right)
Hopf
adjunction. Observe that the fusion operators of the adjunction are families of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
, whereas the fusion operators of the bimonad are morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
.

For any bimonad
T=UF
, we have equalities

H^{l}_{X,Y}=U_{2}(F(X),F(Y))U(\overline{H}^{l}_{X,F(Y)}),\hskip 28.45274ptH^{r}_{X,Y}=U_{2}(F(X),F(Y))U(\overline{H}^{r}_{F(X),Y})

Since
U
is strong monoidal and
U_{2}
is invertible, then
H^{l}
(resp.
H^{r}
) being invertible follows from
\overline{H}^{l}
(resp.
\overline{H}^{r}
) being invertible. Hence, a left (right) Hopf adjunction in this sense gives rise to a left (right) Hopf monad. In the converse direction, a Hopf monad gives rise to such an adjunction as well:

Theorem 3.11

(Theorem 2.15
[
12
]
)
.

A bimonad
T
is a (left or right) Hopf monad, if and only if the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
is a (left or right) Hopf adjunction.

Sketch of proof.

We have already discussed one direction of the proof. For the second part, assume that
T
is either left or right Hopf. We will simply provide the inverses to the fusion operators of
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
. Let
X
and
(M,r)
be objects of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{T}
, respectively, and denote
\overline{H}^{l}_{X,(M,r)}=f
and
\overline{H}^{r}_{(M,r),X}=g
. Depending on whether
H^{r}_{M,X}
and
H^{r}_{M,X}
are invertible, we obtain inverses for
f
and
g
by

\textstyle{f^{-1}\!:T(X)\otimes M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\eta_{M}}
\textstyle{T(X)\otimes T(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(H^{l}_{X,M})^{-1}}
\textstyle{T(X\otimes T(M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes r)}
\textstyle{T(X\otimes M)}

\textstyle{g^{-1}\!:M\otimes T(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{F(Y)}}
\textstyle{T(M)\otimes T(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(H^{r}_{M,X})^{-1}}
\textstyle{T(T(M)\otimes X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(r\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})}
\textstyle{T(M\otimes X)}

Since the above morphisms are in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
, one does not only need to check that the above expressions provide the inverses for the fusion operators, but also that they are
T
-module maps. We refer the reader to Lemmas 2.18 and 2.19 in
[
12
]
for a detailed proof of these facts.

\diamond

In
[
12
]
, the authors also present the notion of left (right)
pre-Hopf
monads which are bimonads with invertible
H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},-}
(resp.
H^{r}_{-,\mathtt{1}}
). Several of the results proved in
[
12
]
only require this weaker condition rather than the full Hopf condition. We will briefly discuss when a bimonad being pre-Hopf is equivalent to it being Hopf in Section
6
. An example of a pre-Hopf monad which is not Hopf is provided in Example 2.8 of
[
12
]
.

3.4
Hopf Monads on Closed Monoidal Categories

In this section, we will look at the case where the base category
\mathcal{C}
is monoidal closed and describe the relation between the fusion operators and the lifting of the closed structure of
\mathcal{C}
to
