\mathcal{C}^{T}
. To do this, we need to recall the theory of liftings from Section 3.5 of
[
12
]
. In
[
12
]
a new set of binary antipodes were also introduced, in addition to the ordinary antipodes of
[
14
]
. Without delving too much into the details of proofs we will present the definitions of these notions.

First, let us recall how an ordinary Hopf algebra
H
with an invertible antipode lifts the closed structure of
\mathsf{Vec}
to its category of modules. Given any pair of
H
-modules
(V,\triangleright)
and
(W,\triangleright^{\prime})
, we obtain two
H
-actions
\triangleright^{l}
and
\triangleright^{r}
on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,W)
defined by
h\triangleright^{l}f=h_{(1)}\triangleright^{\prime}f(S(h_{(2)})\triangleright-)
and
h\triangleright^{r}f=h_{(1)}\triangleright^{\prime}f(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\triangleright-)
for
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,W)
. In this way, the unit and counit of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,-)\dashv-\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}V\cong V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
become
H
-module morphisms so that endofunctors
(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,-),\triangleright^{l})
and
(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(V,-),\triangleright^{r})
on
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
become right adjoint to
-\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(V,\triangleright)
and
(V,\triangleright)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
, respectively. A similar statement can be made for braided Hopf algebras in arbitrary closed braided categories.

Now we recall how adjunctions on a base category can lift to the category of modules over a monad. In full generality, given monads
(T,\mu,\eta)
and
(T^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
on categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, respectively, a
lift
of functor
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
along
(T,T^{\prime})
is a functor
\overline{L}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime T^{\prime}}
such that
U_{T^{\prime}}\overline{L}=LU_{T}
holds. It is a well-known fact that lifts
\overline{L}
correspond to natural transformations
\theta:T^{\prime}L\rightarrow LT
which satisfy
\theta\mu^{\prime}_{L}=G(\mu)\theta_{T}T^{\prime}(\theta)
and
\theta\eta^{\prime}_{L}=L(\eta)
. Such a natural transformation
\theta
is referred to as a
lifting datum
and defines
\overline{L}
by
\overline{L}(M,r)=(L(M),(Lr)\theta_{M})
, where
(M,r)
is an object of
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. In the converse direction, if
\overline{L}
exists and sends any free module
(T(M),\mu_{M})
to a
T^{\prime}
-module
(LT(M),\rho_{M})
, we obtain
\theta=\rho(T^{\prime}L\eta)
.

If in the above scenario,
L
admits a right adjoint
R:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, then liftings
\overline{R}
of
R
which are right adjoint to
\overline{L}
together with a unit and counit which lift the unit and counit of the original adjunction
L\dashv R
, are in bijection with special lifting data
\xi:TR\rightarrow RT^{\prime}
which satisfy additional compatibility conditions (Equations (3a)-(3f)
[
12
]
). In Theorem 3.13 of
[
12
]
, it is proved that such a
\xi
exists if and only if the lifting datum
\theta
of
L
is invertible. If so, then
\xi
is uniquely determined by the expression
\xi=RT^{\prime}(e)R(\theta^{-1}_{R})h_{TR}
, where
h
and
e
denote the unit and counit of
L\dashv R
.

The case which concerns us is when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
T=T^{\prime}
is a bimonad on
\mathcal{C}
. In this situation, any
T
-module
(M,r)
provides a lifting
\overline{L}=-\otimes(M,r)
of
L=-\otimes M
. In particular, the left fusion operator of
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
provides a lifting datum
\overline{H}_{-,(M,r)}:F_{T}(-\otimes M)\longrightarrow F_{T}(-)\otimes M
. If
\mathcal{C}
is left closed and the adjunction in consideration is that of
L=-\otimes M
and
R=[M,-]^{l}
, then the mentioned theory shows that the left fusion operator is invertible if and only if
\overline{L}=-\otimes(M,r)
has a right adjoint
\overline{R}
lifting
[M,-]^{l}
. We denote
\overline{R}
by
[(M,r),-]^{l}
and observe that

[(M,r),(N,t)]^{l}=\left([M,N]^{l},\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.83157pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.83157pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T[M,N]^{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.81792pt\raise 7.06445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.70335pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\xi^{(M,r)}_{N}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.52203pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.52203pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[M,T(N)]^{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.4782pt\raise 7.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{[M,t]^{l}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.65344pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 135.65344pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[M,N]^{l}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)

(38)

where
\xi^{(M,r)}_{N}
itself decomposes as

\textstyle{T[M,N]^{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{(M,r)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{T[M,N]^{l}}}
\textstyle{[M,T(N)]^{l}}
\textstyle{[M,T[M,N]^{l}\otimes M]^{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[M,\left(\overline{H}_{N,(M,r)}\right)^{-1}\right]^{l}}
\textstyle{[M,T([M,N]^{l}\otimes M)]^{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[M,T(\mathrm{ev}^{M}_{N})]^{l}}

Hence, over closed monoidal categories the invertibility of the fusion operators for a bimonad become equivalent to the lifting of the closed structures of the base category to the category of modules over the monad.

Theorem 3.12

(Theorem 3.6
[
12
]
)
.

If
T
is a bimonad on a left (resp. right) closed monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, then
T
is left (resp. right) Hopf if and only if the category
\mathcal{C}^{T}
is left (resp. right) closed and
U_{T}
preserves the closed structure.

Sketch of proof.

The only subtlety which we have not addressed is that
U_{T}
preserving the closed structure of
\mathcal{C}^{T}
, in the sense described in Section
2.1
, is truly equivalent to the adjunctions
-\otimes M\dashv[M,-]^{l}
lifting to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. If the latter holds then it is clear that
\mathcal{C}^{T}
becomes left closed and
U_{T}
preserves this structure with
U_{T}^{l}=\mathrm{id}
. In the other direction,
U_{T}
preserving the closed structure provides an isomorphism of bifunctors
U_{T}^{l}:U_{T}[,]_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}^{l}\rightarrow[U_{T},U_{T}]^{l}
and thereby we can define a left closed structure on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
with
[,]_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}^{l}=[U_{T},U_{T}]^{l}
. For more details on this correspondence, see Lemma 3.15 and more generally Section 3 of
[
12
]
.

\diamond

In the particular case where
\mathcal{C}
is closed, Section 3.3 of
[
12
]
provides an equivalent formulation of the Hopf condition for bimonads. First note that
\xi
is natural in term
(M,r)
as well i.e.
\xi
can be viewed as a natural transformation between bifunctors
\xi:T[U_{T},\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}]^{l}\rightarrow[U_{T},T]^{l}
. Using the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
, we obtain a bijection between
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}(T[U_{T},N]^{l},[U_{T},T(N)]^{l})
and
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(T[T,N]^{l},[\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}},T(N)]^{l})
for any object
N
in
\mathcal{C}
. The corresponding morphism to
\xi_{N}
under this bijection is
[\eta_{-},N]^{l}\xi^{F_{T}(-)}_{N}:T[T(-),N]^{l}\rightarrow[-,T(N)]^{l}
and is denoted by
\overline{s}^{l}_{-,N}
. In this way,
\xi^{(M,r)}_{N}=\overline{s}^{l}_{M,N}T[r,N]^{l}
. The family of morphisms
\overline{s}^{l}
are called the
left binary antipodes
and satisfy certain compatibility conditions (Equations (1a) and (1b)
[
12
]
), which can be written without reference to the fusion operators. A notion of right binary antipode
\overline{s}^{r}_{M,N}:T[T(M),N]^{r}\rightarrow[M,T(N)]^{r}
can also be defined in a symmetric manner.

In the case where
T
corresponds to an ordinary Hopf algebra
H
, for an arbitrary
H
-module
(M,\triangleright)
the binary antipode and
\xi^{(M,\triangleright)}_{N}
take the following forms:

\displaystyle\overline{s}^{l}_{M,N}(h\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}g)=

\displaystyle\big(m\mapsto h_{(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}g(S(h_{(2)})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)\big)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(M,H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N)

\displaystyle\xi^{(M,\triangleright)}_{N}(h\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}f)=

\displaystyle\big(m\mapsto h_{(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}f(S(h_{(2)})\triangleright m)\big)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(M,H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N)

where
h,h^{\prime}\in H
,
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(M,N)
and
g\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
.

Since
\overline{s}^{l}
was determined as the unique map corresponding to
\xi
under the adjunction, it should be clear that for a closed monoidal category, the existence of the binary antipodes is equivalent to the fusion operators being invertible. For a detailed proof of this correspondence we refer the reader to Theorem 3.6 of
[
12
]
. Here we will simply recall how the fusion operators and binary antipodes can be described in terms of each other from Proposition 3.9 of
[
12
]
:

\displaystyle(H_{M,N}^{l})^{-1}=

\displaystyle T(M\otimes\mu_{N})\mathrm{ev}^{T(N)}_{T(M\otimes TT(N))}\left(s^{l}_{T(N),M\otimes TT(N)}T\big(\mathrm{coev}_{M}^{TT(N)}\big)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(N)}\right)

(39)

\displaystyle(H_{M,N}^{r})^{-1}=

\displaystyle T(\mu_{M}\otimes N)\underline{\mathrm{ev}}^{T(M)}_{T(TT(M)\otimes N)}\left(\mathrm{id}_{T(M)}\otimes s^{r}_{T(M),TT(M)\otimes N}T\big(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{N}^{TT(M)}\big)\right)

(40)

\displaystyle s^{l}_{M,N}=

\displaystyle\left[M,\mathrm{ev}^{T(M)}_{N}\right]^{l}\left[\eta_{M},(H^{l}_{M,[T(M),N]^{l}})^{-1}\right]^{l}\mathrm{coev}_{T[T(M),N]^{l}}^{T(M)}

(41)

\displaystyle s^{r}_{M,N}=

\displaystyle\left[M,\underline{\mathrm{ev}}^{T(M)}_{N}\right]^{r}\left[\eta_{M},(H^{r}_{M,[T(M),N]^{l}})^{-1}\right]^{r}\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{T[T(M),N]^{r}}^{T(M)}

(42)

The particular benefit of considering the binary antipodes is that the
T
-actions on the inner-homs are simplified. In particular, the
T
-action on
[M,N]^{l}
for
T
-modules
(M,r)
and
(N,t)
, described in (
38
), becomes
[M,t]^{l}\overline{s}^{l}_{M,N}T[r,N]^{l}
.

Finally, we should comment on why the notions of Hopf monads on rigid categories from Definition
3.6
and those defined for general monoidal categories in Definition
3.10
agree when the base monoidal category is rigid:

Theorem 3.13
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a left (right) rigid category and
T
a bimonad on it, then
T
is a left (right) Hopf monad as in Definition
3.6
if and only if it is a left (right) Hopf monad as in Definition
3.10
.

Sketch of proof.

This statement follows simply by Theorems
3.7
and
3.12
and the fact that any left (right) rigid category is left (right) closed with
[M,N]^{l}=N\otimes{}^{\vee}{M}
(resp.
[M,N]^{r}=M^{\vee}\otimes N
). Alternatively, one can prove Theorem
3.13
by directly showing how the binary and ordinary antipodes are related:

\displaystyle\overline{s}^{l}_{M,N}

\displaystyle=(T(N)\otimes s^{l}_{M})T_{2}(N,{}^{\vee}{T(M)}),

\displaystyle s^{l}_{X}

\displaystyle=(T_{0}\otimes{}^{\vee}{X})\overline{s}^{l}_{X,\mathtt{1}}

(43)

\displaystyle\overline{s}^{r}_{M,N}

\displaystyle=(s^{r}_{M}\otimes T(N))T_{2}(T(M)^{\vee},N),

\displaystyle s^{r}_{X}

\displaystyle=(X^{\vee}\otimes T_{0})\overline{s}^{r}_{X,\mathtt{1}}

(44)

The relation between these antipodes is presented in Remark 3.11 of
[
12
]
with some minor mistakes, which we have corrected here.
\diamond

Cartesian Case:
Adjunctions between cartesian closed categories
L\dashv R:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
where the right adjoint
R
is also cartesian closed had been considered long before the appearance of Hopf monads. It was also well-known that
L
being closed becomes equivalent to the invertibility of the fusion operator (
3.3
) (see Lemma A1.5.8 of
[
32
]
). The latter condition in the cartesian setting is often referred to as the
Frobenius reciprocity law
for
L\dashv R
in the literature.

4
Examples

In this section we present various examples of Hopf monads. In
[
9
]
several structures such as ordinary Hopf algebras, Hopf algebroids and bimonoids in duoidal categories are described as examples of bimonads. Here we review some of these examples and provide some novel examples of our own.

4.1
Central Hopf Algebras as Hopf monads

In this section we review the theory of augmented Hopf monads from
[
12
]
and recall a criteria for telling when a Hopf monad arises from a braided Hopf algebra.

So far we have described how any bialgebra (Hopf algebra)
B
in a braided category
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a bimonad (left Hopf monad) on
\mathcal{C}
. If the category is monoidal but does not admit a braiding, then a bialgebra
(B,\tau)
in the lax center of the category
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
would also produce a bimonad structure on the endofunctor
B\otimes-
in the same way. The underlying monad structure will still be defined by the algebra structure of
B
, while for defining the comonoidal structure of
B\otimes-
we simply replace the use of the braiding of the category
\Psi_{B,-}
by the braiding
\tau_{-}
which
(B,\tau)
comes equipped with. We follow the notation of
[
12
]
and call
(B,\tau)
a
central (Hopf) bialgebra
and denote its corresponding (Hopf) bimonad by
B\otimes_{\tau}-
. Note that any braided bialgebra
B
in a braided category
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
can be viewed as a central bialgebra
(B,\Psi_{B,-})
.

In
[
12
]
the authors provided a characterisation for Hopf monads which arise from central Hopf algebras. An arbitrary bimonad
T
on a monoidal category is called
augmented
if there exists a bimonad morphism
\epsilon:T\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{C}
. Given a central bialgebra
(B,\tau)
, the bimonad
B\otimes_{\tau}-
automatically becomes augmented via the counit map
\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}:T=B\otimes-\rightarrow-
. Without acknowledging this augmentation morphism, the counit is hidden in the comonoidal structure, as a morphism from
T(\mathtt{1})
to
\mathtt{1}
.

Theorem 4.1

(Theorem 5.7
[
12
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category. There is an equivalence of categories between the category of central Hopf algebras in
\mathcal{C}
and that of augmented left Hopf monads on
\mathcal{C}
.

Sketch of proof.

We have already mentioned one direction of the argument. For the converse direction, the difficulty is showing that for an augmented left Hopf monad
T
,
T(\mathtt{1})
becomes a central Hopf algebra and that its corresponding left Hopf monad is isomorphic to
T
. First observe that given an augmentation
\epsilon:T\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
we can reconstruct the following natural transformations:

\displaystyle u^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=(T(\mathtt{1})\otimes\epsilon)T_{2}(\mathtt{1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}):T\rightarrow T(\mathtt{1})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}

(45)

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon\otimes T(\mathtt{1}))T_{2}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathtt{1}):T\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes T(\mathtt{1})

(46)

In Lemma 5.16 of
[
12
]
, it is shown that when
T
is left Hopf, then
u^{\epsilon}
is invertible and its inverse is given by
T(\epsilon)(H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},-})^{-1}(T(\mathtt{1})\otimes\eta)
. In Lemma 5.15
[
12
]
, it is demonstrated that
\tau:=v^{\epsilon}(u^{\epsilon})^{-1}:T(\mathtt{1})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes T(\mathtt{1})
provides a lax braiding for
T(\mathtt{1})
and moreover that
u^{\epsilon}:T\rightarrow T(\mathtt{1})\otimes_{\tau}-
is an isomorphism of bimonads. One can thereby define the central Hopf algebra structure on
T(\mathtt{1})
via this isomorphism.

\diamond

First observe that a symmetric argument can prove a similar statement for augmented right Hopf monads and bialgebras in the lax right center
Z_{r,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
which admit opantipodes. Secondly, we note that the use of Lemma 5.16 from
[
12
]
is essential and without knowing that the left fusion operator is invertible, we can not provide an inverse for
u^{\epsilon}
. In
[
12
]
, augmented bimonads
(T,\epsilon)
for which
u^{\epsilon}
is invertible are called
left regular
. By the same argument as above, we can describe an equivalence between central bialgebras in
\mathcal{C}
and left regular augmented bimonads on
\mathcal{C}
[Theorem 5.17
[
12
]
]. We should also note that for the corresponding bimonad
B\otimes_{\tau}-
of a central bialgebra
(B,\tau)
, we have
u^{\epsilon}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
v^{\epsilon}=\tau
.

Observe that for an arbitrary central bialgebra
(B,\tau)
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, the module category
\mathcal{C}^{B\otimes_{\tau}-}
can not be viewed as the module category over a bialgebra in
\mathcal{C}
itself. Hence, although these bimonads correspond to braided bialgebras in some braided category, namely
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
, they are the first genuine examples of bimonads on a base category
\mathcal{C}
that go beyond the study of bialgebras within
\mathcal{C}
itself. In the next sections we will discuss examples of Hopf monads which can not be augmented, but first we can look at an application of this theory which was described in
[
16
]
.

It is well-known that for any given Hopf algebra
B
in a braided category
(\mathcal{D},\Psi)
, the center of its category of modules
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{B}\mathcal{D})
has an equivalent formulation as the category of
Yetter-Drinfeld
modules
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}
over
B
. The category
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}
consists of triples
(M,\triangleright,\delta)
where
M
is an object of
\mathcal{D}
,
\triangleright:B\otimes M\rightarrow M
is a
B
-action and
\delta:M\rightarrow B\otimes M
a
B
-coaction satisfying the following compatibility condition:

(m\otimes\triangleright)(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{H,H}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}\!)(\Delta\otimes\delta)\!=\!(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}\!)(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{M,H})(\delta\triangleright\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}\!)(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{H,M})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}\!)

(47)

The equivalence between
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{B}\mathcal{D})
and
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}
is given by sending a Yetter-Drinfeld module
(M,\triangleright,\delta)
to
(M,\triangleright)
with the lax braiding
\tau_{(N,r)}=(r\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{M,N})(\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})
. In the converse direction, any
B
-module
(M,\triangleright)
with a lax braiding
\tau
is sent to
(M,\triangleright,\tau_{H}(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\eta))
. The resulting braiding on the category
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}
is given by
(\triangleright_{N}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{M,N})(\delta_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})
for a pair of objects
(M,\triangleright_{M},\delta_{M})
and
(N,\triangleright_{N},\delta_{N})
. Note that when the antipode of
B
is invertible then
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{B}\mathcal{D})=Z({}_{B}\mathcal{D})
. We refer the reader to
[
6
]
for additional details on Yetter-Drinfeld modules in braided categories.

If we start with a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
and pick a braided Hopf algebra
B
in
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
, it is an interesting question whether we can express the category
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}\left(Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})\right)\equiv Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}\left({}_{B}Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})\right)
as the center of a category of modules over a bialgebra-like structure in
\mathcal{C}
itself. This gap is precisely filled by the theory of Hopf monads:

Theorem 4.2
.

[Proposition 2.13
[
16
]
] If
(B,\tau)
is a braided Hopf algebra in the center of
\mathcal{C}
, then there exists a braided monoidal isomorphism
{}^{(B,\tau)}_{(B,\tau)}\mathcal{YD}\left(Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})\right)\cong Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}\left(\mathcal{C}^{B\otimes_{\tau}-}\right)
.

Note that
{}_{(B,\tau)}Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathcal{C}^{B\otimes_{\tau}-}
will generally not be the equivalent, while the above result tells us that their centres are.

4.2
Hopf Monads on
\mathbb{N}_{0}

In this section we will classify Hopf monads on the monoidal category
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+,0)
. The category
\mathbb{N}_{0}
has natural numbers (including
0
) as objects and morphisms are given by the natural order
\leq
on
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Explicitly, for two numbers
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists a unique arrow
\mathrm{min}(m,n)\rightarrow\mathrm{max}(m,n)
. Recall that more generally, any poset
(P,\leq)
can be viewed as a category in the same way. The category
\mathbb{N}_{0}
also obtains a symmetric monoidal structure via addition
+
and
0
acting as the monoidal unit.

It is well-known that monads
(T,\mu,\eta)
on poset categories
(P,\leq)
correspond to
closure operators
on
(P,\leq)
i.e. an order-preserving map
T:P\rightarrow P
such that
p\leq T(p)
and
T(p)=T^{2}(p)
for any
p\in P
(Example 5.1.7
[
54
]
).

For the particular case of
\mathbb{N}_{0}
, closure operators correspond to infinite subsets of
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Given a closure operators
T
, since
n\leq T(n)
, the set
S_{T}:=\{T(n)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
defines an infinite subset of
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Additionally, for any pair of numbers
m
and
k
which satisfy
m\leq m+k\leq T(m)
, we have that
T(m)\leq T(m+k)\leq T(m)
and thereby
T(m+k)=T(m)
. Hence
T(n)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is defined solely by
m_{n}:=\mathrm{min}\{m\in S_{T}\mid n\leq m\}
: By definition
T(n)\leq T(m_{n})=m_{n}
and since
T(n)\in\{m\in S_{T}\mid n\leq m\}
, then
T(n)=m_{n}
. In the converse direction, we obtain a closure operator
T
for any infinite subset
S
of
\mathbb{N}_{0}
defined by
T(n)=\mathrm{min}\{m\in S\mid n\leq m\}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Bimonads on
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+,0)
correspond to non-trivial (not
\{0\}
) submonoids of
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)
.

Proof.

Assume
(T,\mu,\eta)
is defined by an infinite subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
and has a compatible comonoidal structure
(T_{2},T_{0})
. Firstly note that the existence of
T_{0}
implies that
0\leq T(0)\leq 0
, and thereby
T(0)=0\in S
. Secondly,
T_{2}
implies that
T(m+n)\leq T(m)+T(n)
for all pairs
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In particular, for
m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in S
we have that
m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}\leq T(m^{\prime}+n^{\prime})\leq m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}
. Thereby
S
must be closed under addition. This condition is in fact necessary and sufficient since for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have
m+n\leq T(m)+T(n)
and thereby
T(m+n)\leq T(T(m)+T(n))=T(m)+T(n)
. The compatibility conditions of Definition
3.1
do not need to be checked, since there exists a unique morphism between any two objects in a poset category.
∎

Theorem 4.4
.

Hopf monads on
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+,0)
are in bijection with submonoids
\langle n\rangle
of
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)
which are generated by a single non-zero element
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

It is easy to show that any Hopf monad
T
on
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+,0)
is uniquely determined by
T(1)
. The left fusion operator being invertible implies that
T(T(n)+m)=T(n)+T(m)
. For any
n\in S_{T}
,
T(n+1)=\mathrm{min}\{m\in S_{T}\mid n\lneq m\}
. By the Hopf condition
T(n+1)=n+T(1)
. Consequently,
S_{T}=\{0,T(1),2T(1),\ldots\}=\langle T(1)\rangle
.

In the converse direction, assume that
S_{T}
has the mentioned form and for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exist
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
T(m)=kT(1)
and
T(n)=lT(1)
. In this case,
T(kT(1)+n)=k^{\prime}T(1)
where
k^{\prime}
is minimal so that
kT(1)+n\leq k^{\prime}T(1)
. Consequently,
l=k^{\prime}-k
and
T(T(n)+m)=T(n)+T(m)
holds.
∎

Another interpretation of the above results comes from looking at the Eilenberg-Moore category. Any
T
-module
n
for a closure operator must satisfy
T(n)=n
. Therefore
\mathcal{C}^{T}
is precisely the set
S_{T}
defined above. From this perspective Lemma
4.3
is a consequence of Theorem
3.2
. Also note that the Hopf monads from Theorem
4.4
are only augmented if
T(1)=1
and
S_{T}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

4.3
Hopf Monads on
\mathsf{Set}

In this section we will classify finitary Hopf monads on the category
(\mathsf{Set},\times,1)
. It is well-known that
finitary
monads
(T,\mu,\eta)
, meaning monads where
T
preserves filtered-colimits, on
\mathsf{Set}
correspond to
Lawvere theories
or
algebraic theories
. The latter were introduced in
[
37
]
and we will refer to them as
theories
here. We also base our notation on the modern treatment of this topic in
[
30
]
which focuses on the correspondence between theories and monads. A classification of theories whose categories of models are cartesian closed has already appeared in
[
31
]
. Here, we will extend this work and show that any theory whose category of models lifts the cartesian closed structure of
\mathsf{Set}
must correspond to the theory of
G
-sets for a group
G
.

First, we will recall the basics of algebraic theories from Section 2 of
[
30
]
. Let
\aleph_{0}
denote the skeleton of the category of full subcategory of finite sets. Hence,
\aleph_{0}
has the natural numbers as its objects (including
0
instead of
\emptyset
) and morphisms
\alpha:m\rightarrow n
correspond to morphisms
\alpha:\{1,\ldots m\}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots n\}
. A Lawvere theory
\mathbb{T}
consists of a small category with finite products and a strict product-preserving identity-on-objects functor
t:\aleph_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
where every object is a power of
t(1)
. We will abuse notation and denote the images of
t(n)=t(1)\times\cdots\times t(1)
by
n
.

Models over the theory
\mathbb{T}
are limit-preserving functors
\mathbb{T}
into
\mathsf{Set}
. In terms of classical universal algebra language, any model
M:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
corresponds to the choice of a set
M(1)
and maps
M(m)=M(1)^{m}\rightarrow M(1)^{n}=M(n)
corresponding to morphisms in
\mathbb{T}
, which compose in a functorial manner. To ease notation, we will discuss
\mathbb{T}
-models by referring to
M(1)
and the operations acting on
f_{M(1)}=M(f)
instead of the functor
M
. The inclusion of maps
\alpha:\{1,\ldots m\}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots n\}
via
t:\aleph_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
gives rise to elementary operations
\overline{\alpha}:X^{n}\rightarrow X^{m}
, where
\overline{\alpha}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=(x_{\alpha(1)},\ldots,x_{\alpha(m)})
for any
\mathbb{T}
-model on the set
X
. In particular, all the diagonal maps
\Delta^{n}:X\rightarrow X^{n}
sending
x\mapsto(x,\ldots,x)
act on each model.

Throughout this section we assume that
\mathbb{T}
is a
non-degenerate
theory, meaning that not all its models are trivial (have a singleton set as their underlying set).

Every theory
\mathbb{T}
described above gives rise to a finitary monad
T
and vice-versa. In particular, the category of
T
-modules is equivalent to the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models. If
U:\mathbb{T}{\rm-Mod}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{Set}
denotes the forgetful functor sending a model
M
to its underlying set
U(M)=M(1)
, then its left adjoint
F
is defined as

F(X)=\int^{n\in\aleph_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathbb{T}(n,1)\times X^{n}

(48)

and
(T=UF,\mu,\eta)
denotes the corresponding monad to the theory. The equivalences between
\mathbb{T}
-models and
T
-modules commute with the forgetful functors to
\mathsf{Set}
so we can work with either notion interchangeably. Starting with a finitary monad, its Kleisli category can be viewed as a Lawvere theory and recovers
T
by the described procedure, upto isomorphism. See Section 4 of
[
30
]
for more details.

We will call the elements of
\mathbb{T}(n,1)
,
n
-ary operations. Note that
F(X)
becomes a quotient of the coproduct
\amalg_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ \rho\in\mathbb{T}(n,1)}X^{n}
. Hence an arbitrary element in
F(X)
can be written as the image of an element
(\rho;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
where
x_{i}\in X
and
\rho
is an
n
-ary operation and if
p=q\overline{\alpha}
in
\mathbb{T}
for a morphism
\alpha
in
\aleph_{0}
, then the terms
(p;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
(q;x_{\alpha(1)},\ldots,x_{\alpha(m)})
are identified in
F(X)
. If we denote the set with
m
elements by
m
in
\mathsf{Set}
, then its easy to check that
F(m)\cong\mathbb{T}(m,1)
becomes the set of all possible
n
-ary operations: Any element in
\amalg_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ \rho\in\mathbb{T}(n,1)}m^{n}
can be written in the form
(\rho;\overline{\alpha}(1,\ldots n))
for some morphism
\alpha\in\aleph_{0}
, which thereby gets identified with
(\rho\overline{\alpha};1,\ldots m)
in
F(m)
, where
\rho\overline{\alpha}
is an
m
-ary operation. Hence, we will write elements
(\rho;\alpha(1),\ldots,\alpha(n))
of
F(m)
as
m
-ary
\rho\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{T}(m,1)
instead.

It is also well-known that if
A
and
B
are
\mathbb{T}
-models, then
U(A)\times U(B)
obtains a natural
\mathbb{T}
-model structure define by

\rho((a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{n},b_{n}))=\big(\rho(a_{1},\ldots a_{n}),\rho(b_{1},\ldots b_{n})\big)

for an arbitrary
n
-ary operation
\rho
. By Theorem
3.2
, the corresponding monad
T
of the theory
\mathbb{T}
becomes a bimonad. In particular, the comonoidal structure
F_{2}
on
F
is given by:

\big(\rho;(a_{(1)},b_{(1)}),\ldots,(a_{(n)},b_{(n)})\big)\mapsto\big((\rho;a_{1},\ldots a_{n}),(\rho;b_{1},\ldots b_{n})\big)

Now, we look at when the corresponding bimonad of a theory is Hopf. First recall from Proposition
2.8
that
Z(\mathsf{Set})=\mathsf{Set}
, and thereby the only possible augmented Hopf monads on
\mathsf{Set}
arise from Hopf algebras in
\mathsf{Set}
i.e. groups
G
. The corresponding Hopf monad
T
of group
G
, will be of the form
G\times-
and preserves all colimits. In particular, the corresponding algebraic theory
\mathbb{T}
to this monad is the category generated by
\mathbb{T}(1,1)=G
and all other morphisms from
\aleph_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The category of models over the theory are
G
-sets. Here, we will demonstrate that any algebraic theory whose corresponding bimonad is Hopf must be of this form.

Assume that
\mathbb{T}
is a theory whose corresponding monad
T
becomes Hopf. Hence, the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models becomes cartesian closed and for any
T
-algebra
A
, the following morphism (
\overline{H}^{l}_{\mathtt{1},A}
) is bijective:

\textstyle{\Psi_{A}:FU(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(1)\times A}
\textstyle{(\rho;a_{1},\ldots a_{|\rho|})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\big(\rho^{\Delta},\rho_{A}(a_{1},\ldots a_{|\rho|})\big)}

(49)

where
|\rho|
denotes the arity of
\rho
and
\rho^{\Delta}=\rho\Delta^{|\rho|}
is the unary operation associated to
\rho
.

Recall that a unary operation
u\in F(1)
is called a pseudo-constant if
u(x)=u(y)
in all
\mathbb{T}
-models.

Theorem 4.5

(Theorem 1.2
[
31
]
)
.

If the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models is cartesian closed, then
\mathbb{T}
has no pseudo-constants.

Note that if a theory has no pseudo constants, it also has no
constants
i.e. operations of arity 0. if
\lambda\in T(0,1)
, then we would obtain a pseudo-constant operation
\lambda\overline{\alpha}
, where
\alpha
is the unique morphism
\emptyset\rightarrow 1
.

Lemma 4.6
.

For any unary
u
operation in
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}
-model
A
, the map
u_{A}
is injective.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{T}
is non-degenerate we can pick an arbitrary
\mathbb{T}
-model
A
with at least two elements
a\neq b\in U(A)
. If
u
is a unary operation in
\mathbb{T}
and
u_{A}(a)=u_{A}(b)
, then
\Psi_{A}((u;a))=\Psi_{A}((u;b))
. Since
\Psi_{A}
is a bijection, it means that
(u;a)=(u;b)
in
FU(A)
.

Now consider any
\mathbb{T}
-model
B
and arbitrary elements
c,d\in U(B)
. We can define a map
f:U(A)\rightarrow U(B)
such that
f(a)=c
and
f(b)=(d)
. Consequently,
F(f):FU(A)\rightarrow FU(B)
is an algebra morphism and, by definition,
F(f)((u;a))=(u;c)
and
F(f)((u;b))=(u;d)
hold and
(u;c)=(u;d)
in
FU(B)
. Since the
\mathbb{T}
-model structure of
B
is given by an action
UFU(B)\rightarrow U(B)
, then
u_{B}(c)=u_{B}(d)
in
B
. Since,
B
,
c
and
d
were chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
u
is pseudo-constant in all
\mathbb{T}
-models, which contradicts Theorem
4.5
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Any unary operation in
\mathbb{T}
is invertible and its inverse is another unary operation of the theory.

Proof.

Let
u
be a unary operation in
\mathbb{T}
. First note that
\Psi_{F(1)}
is a bijection and consider
\Psi_{F(1)}^{-1}((u,\mathrm{id}))
. There must exist an operation
\rho
of arity
n
and a family of unaries
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in F(1)
, such that
\rho^{\Delta}=u
and
\rho(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\Delta^{n}=\mathrm{id}
, so that
\Psi_{F(1)}((\rho;u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}))=(u,\mathrm{id})
.

Now consider a non-trivial
\mathbb{T}
-model
A
and
a\in A
. By definition
\Psi_{A}((u;a))=(u,u_{A}(a))
. However, we also know that
\Psi_{A}((\rho;b_{1},\ldots b_{n}))=(u,u_{A}(a))
, where
b_{i}:=(u_{i})_{A}u_{A}(a)
. Since
\Psi_{A}
is a bijection, we conclude that
(u;a)=(\rho;b_{1},\ldots b_{n})
in
FU(A)
.

If for all
i
, we have that
b_{i}\neq a
, then we can consider any pair
x\neq y\in B
in any non-trivial
\mathbb{T}
-model
B
and conclude that
u_{B}(x)=u_{B}(y)
: This is because there exist a pair of functions
f_{1},f_{2}:U(A)\rightarrow U(B)
which map
b_{i}
to the same collection of elements but where
f_{1}
maps
a
to
x
and
f_{2}
maps
a
to
y
. Hence, by considering the images of the algebra maps
F(f_{1})
and
F(f_{2})
we see that

(u;x)=(\rho;f_{1}(b_{1}),\ldots,f_{1}(b_{n}))=(\rho;f_{2}(b_{1}),\ldots,f_{2}(b_{n}))=(u;y)\in FU(B)

Since the
\mathbb{T}
-model structure of
B
is defined by a map from
FU(B)
to
U(B)
, then
u_{B}(x)=u_{B}(y)
, which contradicts Lemma
4.6
. Hence, there must be an
1\leq m\leq n
for which
a=(u_{m})_{A}u_{A}(a)
.

Now consider the case where
A=F(1)
and
a=\mathrm{id}
. From the argument above, there must exist an
m
such that
\mathrm{id}=(u_{m})_{F(1)}u_{F(1)}(\mathrm{id})=u_{m}u
. Consequently, we obtain the following equality in
F(1)
:

(u_{m})_{F(1)}(uu_{m})=u_{m}uu_{m}=u_{m}=(u_{m})_{F(1)}(\mathrm{id})

But by Lemma
4.6
,
(u_{m})_{F(1)}
is injective and thereby,
uu_{m}=\mathrm{id}
as well. Hence,
u
is invertible and
u_{m}u=\mathrm{id}=uu_{m}
. Before concluding the proof, we note as a consequence
(\rho;u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})=(u;u_{m})
holds in
FUF(1)
. ∎

It follows immediately from Lemma
4.7
, that the set of unary operations in
\mathbb{T}
form a group and as a consequence any
\mathbb{T}
-model is a
F(1)
-set. Now we show that the theory
\mathbb{T}
is fully generated by unary operations.

Theorem 4.8
.

For any operation
\rho
in
\mathbb{T}
of arity
n
, there exists an
1\leq m\leq n
and a unary operation
u
such that
\rho=u\sigma_{m}
, where
\sigma_{m}(1,\ldots n)=m
.

Proof.

Let
A
be an arbitrary
\mathbb{T}
-model and
\rho
an
n
-ary operation in
A
. Since
\Psi_{A}
is bijective, we have that

(\rho;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=\big(\rho^{\Delta};(\rho^{\Delta}_{A})^{-1}\rho_{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\big)\in FU(A)

If
b:=(\rho^{\Delta}_{A})^{-1}\rho(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\neq a_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then we can consider maps
U(A)\rightarrow U(B)
for arbitrary models
B
, which send
b
to various elements of
A
, but send
a_{i}
to a fixed set of elements. Similar to the argument in the proof of Lemma
4.7
, this implies that
\rho^{\Delta}
must be a pseudo-constant which contradicts Theorem
4.5
. Therefore,
(\rho^{\Delta}_{A})^{-1}\rho(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=a_{i}
for some
i
.

Now let
A=F(n)
and let
a_{i}=\sigma_{i}\in F(n)
where
\sigma_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{i}
. By the above argument, we see that for some
i
the following equality holds

(\rho;\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})=\big(\rho^{\Delta};\sigma_{i}\big)\in FUF(n)

Looking at the image of the two elements under
\Psi_{F(n)}
, we observe that
\rho(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\rho^{\Delta}(x_{i})
.
∎

By Theorem
4.8
and the construction of
F
as a coend, we observe that
F(X)\cong F(1)\times X
for any set
X
. In particular, the monad structure on
T
is given by the group structure on
F(1)
.

Theorem 4.9
.

If
T
is a finitary Hopf monad on
\mathsf{Set}
, then there exists a group
G
such that
T\cong G\times-
. In particular,
T
must be augmented.

4.4
Galois and Ore Extensions of Bialgebras

In this section, we will assume
B
and
H
are a pair of bialgebras and
f:B\rightarrow H
a bialgebra injection and discuss when the induced adjunction
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}\leftrightarrows{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
is left (pre-) Hopf. We will first show that the pre-Hopf condition corresponds to
H
being a Galois extension of
B
in the sense of
[
59
]
and then provide a more general Galois condition (
51
) for the bimonad to be Hopf. Finally, we show that any suitable Ore extension of a bialgebra provides a left Hopf monad in this way.

Any algebra morphism
f
gives rise to a pair of adjoint functors,
H\otimes_{B}-\dashv U:{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}\leftrightarrows{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
where
U
denotes the restriction of scalars via
f
and
H
has a natural
B
-bimodule structure via
f
. If
f
is a bialgebra morphism then
U
becomes naturally strong monoidal since for any pair of
H
-modules
M
and
N
,
B
acts on
U(M\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N)
by
f(b)_{(1)}.m\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}f(b)_{(2)}.n
while the
B
-action on
U(M)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}U(N)
is given by
f(b_{(1)}).m\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}f(b_{(2)}).n
. Consequently, if
f
is a bialgebra morphism then
H\otimes_{B}-\dashv U
is a comonoidal adjunction. Alternatively, one can look directly at the induced monad on
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
which is given by
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
where we consider the natural
B
-bimodule structure induced by
f
. The comonoidal structure
T_{2}(M,N)
on the monad is given by
h\otimes_{B}(m\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}n)\mapsto(h_{(1)}\otimes_{B}m)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(h_{(2)}\otimes_{B}m)
which is well-defined because
f
is bialgebra map. Hence, we have a commuting diagram of strong monoidal functors:

\textstyle{{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{forg.}}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{forg.}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Vec}}

If
B
and
H
are both Hopf algebras then
f
automatically commutes with the antipodes since the antipodes, when they exist, are uniquely determined by the bialgebra structures. In this case, the forgetful functors in the above diagram both become left closed and
U
also becomes left closed since
f
respects the antipode and, thereby,
U
respects the actions on the left inner-homs.

When
B
and
H
are not Hopf, we can still consider the problem of when the bimonad
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
is left Hopf. This can be done in terms of the fusion operator but also in terms of the closed structure. While
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
is does not lift the closed structure of
\mathsf{Vec}
, it is still a closed monoidal category and
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
being Hopf would mean that
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
is lifting the inner-homs of
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
.

Now assume
f:B\rightarrow H
is a bialgebra injection and consider the subset
I=\{f(b)-\epsilon_{B}(b).1_{H}\mid b\in B\}
in
H
. It is straightforward to check that this subset is a coideal i.e. satisfies
\Delta_{H}(I)\subset I\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H+H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I
. Hence, we obtain a quotient coalgebra structure on
C:=H/I
and we denote the coalgebra projection
H\twoheadrightarrow C
by
\pi
. Note that
H
becomes a left
C
-comodule via
\delta:=(\pi\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{H})\Delta
. Moreover,
H
becomes a monoid in
{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}
(a
C
-
comodule algebra
) with this coaction. With this structure, we can identify
f(B)
with the space of
C
-coinvariants
{}^{C}H
i.e. elements
h
satisfying
\delta(h)=\pi(1_{H})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h
:

\displaystyle\delta(h)=\pi(1_{H})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h\Rightarrow\pi(h_{(1)})\epsilon(h_{(2)})=\pi(1_{H})\epsilon(h)\Rightarrow\pi(h-\epsilon(h).1_{H})=0\Rightarrow h\in f(B)

Note that for a general comodule algebra the space of coinvariants are formed by elements
h
satisfying
\delta(h^{\prime}h)=\pi(h^{\prime}_{(1)})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h^{\prime}_{(2)}h
for any
h^{\prime}\in H
but this is equivalent to
\delta(h)=\pi(1_{H})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h
in our case. In this setting, we call
H
a
C
-
Galois extension
of
B
if the map
\beta
is a bijection:

\textstyle{\beta:H\otimes_{B}H\longrightarrow C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H}
\textstyle{\hskip 14.22636pth\otimes_{B}h^{\prime}\longmapsto\pi(h_{(1)})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h_{(2)}h^{\prime}}

(50)

See Definition 1.5 of
[
59
]
for comparison. The notion of
C
-Galois extension also appeared earlier in
[
18
,
17
]
for entwined algebras and coalgebras under the name of
principal
C
-
bundles
. With this notation, we can prove the following result:

Theorem 4.10
.

If
f:B\rightarrow H
is a bialgebra injection as above, then the bimonad
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
on
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
is left pre-Hopf if and only if
H
is a
C
-Galois extension of
B
.

Proof.

First assume that
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
is left pre-Hopf. Now consider
\mathbb{K}
with its natural
B
-action induced by
\epsilon
and
B
with its trivial left
B
-action. Note that
T(\mathbb{K})=H\otimes_{B}\mathbb{K}\cong C
and
T(B)=H\otimes_{B}B\cong H
as left
B
-modules and
T(\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}T(B))\cong H\otimes_{B}H
. By these isomorphisms we recover
\beta
as
H^{l}_{\mathbb{K},B}
and if
H^{l}_{\mathbb{K},B}
is invertible then so is
\beta
.

In the converse direction, we assume
H
is a
C
-Galois extension of
B
and denote
\beta(c\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}1)=c_{(+)}\otimes_{B}c_{(-)}
. Then we can define the inverse of
H^{l}_{\mathbb{K},M}
for any
B
-module
M
by
c\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(h\otimes_{B}m)\mapsto c_{(+)}\otimes_{B}(c_{(-)}.h\otimes_{B}m)
. It is easy to check that this map is indeed well-defined and provides an inverse for
H^{l}_{\mathbb{K},M}
.
∎

For the induced bimonad of
f:B\rightarrow H
to be left Hopf we need a more generalised version of
\beta
:

Theorem 4.11
.

If
f:B\rightarrow H
is a bialgebra injection as above, then the bimonad
T={}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
on
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
is left Hopf if and only if the following map is a bijection:

\textstyle{\Gamma:H\otimes_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H)\longrightarrow H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H}
\textstyle{\hskip 14.22636pth\otimes_{B}(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h^{\prime})\longmapsto h_{(1)}f(b)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h_{(2)}h^{\prime}}

(51)

Here we consider
B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
as the tensor product of two left
B
-modules.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
4.10
, we observe that
T(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}T(B))\cong H\otimes_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H)
and recover
\Gamma
from
H^{l}_{B,B}
. Hence, if
T
is left Hopf then
\Gamma
becomes invertible. In the converse direction, assume
\Gamma
is invertible and denote
\Gamma(h\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}1)=h_{(+)}\otimes_{B}(h_{(+-)}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h_{(-)})
. Note that the component
h_{(+-)}
belongs to
B
. With this notation we can define the inverse of
H^{l}_{M,N}
for two arbitrary
B
-modules
M
and
N
by

\displaystyle(H\otimes_{B}M)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(H\otimes_{B}N)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H\otimes_{B}(M\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(H\otimes_{B}N))

\displaystyle(h\otimes_{B}m)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(h^{\prime}\otimes_{B}n)

\displaystyle\longmapsto h_{(+)}\otimes_{B}(h_{(+-)}.m\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(h_{(-)}h^{\prime}\otimes_{B}m))

It follows in a straightforward manner that this map is well-defined and provides an inverse for
H^{l}_{M,N}
.
∎

The invertibility of
\Gamma
is a difficult problem to check. Here we will provide an example of such an extension by directly checking that
H
lifts the left inner-homs of
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
.

For any bialgebra, the category
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
is left closed with
[M,N]^{l}=\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
, where
\mathrm{Hom}_{B}
denotes the space of left
B
-module morphisms and
\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
has a left
B
-action defined by
(b.f)(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=f(b^{\prime}.b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)
. The unit and counit of
-\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\dashv[M,-]^{l}
are defined by

\textstyle{\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}:N\rightarrow[M,N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M]^{l}}
\textstyle{\quad n\mapsto(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m\mapsto b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)}

\textstyle{\mathrm{ev}^{M}_{N}:[M,N]^{l}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\rightarrow N}
\textstyle{\quad f\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m\mapsto f(1_{B}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)}

(52)

This closed structure was observed in
[
56
]
at the level of bialgebroids. By Theorem
3.12
, the bimonad
T
being Hopf is equivalent to the functor
U
lifting the adjunction above to
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
. Hence, whenever
H
has a well-defined action on
[M,N]^{l}
so that
\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}
and
\mathrm{ev}^{M}_{N}
become
H
-module morphisms then
T
becomes a left Hopf monad. We will present one family of such examples here:

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
B
be a bialgebra and
\mathsf{d}:B\rightarrow B
a derivation on
B
satisfying
\Delta(\mathsf{d}(b))=\mathsf{d}(b_{(1)})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}+b_{(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathsf{d}(b_{(2)})
and
\epsilon(\mathsf{d})=0
. If
H=B[x;\mathsf{d}]
denotes the
Ore extension
of
B
, then
H
is a bialgebra and the natural algebra map
f:B\rightarrow H
is a bialgebra map and induces a left Hopf monad
{}_{B}H\otimes_{B}-
on
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

Recall from Chapter 2 of
[
29
]
that
H
is defined as the quotient of
B\langle x\rangle
by the ideal
\langle x.b-b.x-\mathsf{d}(b)\mid b\in B\rangle
. It was already shown in
[
52
]
that when
\mathsf{d}
satisfies the additional compatibility condition with
\Delta
and
\epsilon
, then
H
obtains a natural bialgebra structure with
\Delta(x)=x\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}1+1\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x
and
\epsilon(x)=0
extending the coalgebra structure defined on elements of
B
. Hence, the natural map
f:B\rightarrow H
defined by
b\mapsto b
becomes a bialgebra morphism.

Here we will define an action of
H
on
\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
such that the unit and counit in (
52
) lift to
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
. For a pair of
H
-modules
M
and
N
, we extend the
B
-action on
\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
to an
H
-action by defining
(x.f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=x.f(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(\mathsf{d}(b)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)
. First note that this action is well-defined i.e.
x.f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,N)
:

\displaystyle b.\big((x.f)(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)\big)=

\displaystyle b.x.f(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-b.f(\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-b.f(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b.x.f(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(b_{(1)}\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)-f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)-\mathsf{d}(b).f(b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(b_{(1)}\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)

\displaystyle-f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.b_{(2)}.m)+f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathsf{d}(b_{(2)}).m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)-f(\mathsf{d}(b_{(1)})b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)

\displaystyle-f(b_{(1)}\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)-f(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.b_{(2)}.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x.f)(b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b_{(2)}.m)

Secondly, note that this gives rise to a well-defined action of
H
since

\displaystyle(x.b^{\prime}.f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=

\displaystyle x.(b^{\prime}.f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-(b^{\prime}.f)(\mathsf{d}(b)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-(b^{\prime}.f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.f(bb^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(\mathsf{d}(b)b^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(bb^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle(b^{\prime}.x.f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=

\displaystyle(x.f)(bb^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=x.f(bb^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(\mathsf{d}(bb^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-f(bb^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime}).f)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=

\displaystyle f(b\mathsf{d}(b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)

Now we must check that
\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}
and
\mathrm{ev}^{M}_{N}
become
H
-module morphisms:

\displaystyle[x.\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}(n)](b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=

\displaystyle x.\big(\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}(n)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)\big)-\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}(n)(\mathsf{d}(b)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}(n)(b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.(b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-(\mathsf{d}(b).n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-(b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x.b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)+(b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)-(\mathsf{d}(b).n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)-(b.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x.m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b.x.n\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)=[\mathrm{coev}^{M}_{N}(x.n)](b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m)

For
\mathrm{ev}^{M}_{N}
the calculation follows in a trivial manner from
\mathsf{d}(1)=0
and its verification is left to the reader.
∎

Hence any suitable Ore extension
f:B\rightarrow B[x;\mathsf{d}]
gives rise to a left Hopf monad on
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}
. Additionally, note that this induced left Hopf monad admits an augmentation precisely when
f
admits a retraction, which cannot happen unless
\mathsf{d}
is an inner derivation.

4.5
Hopf Monads from Pivotal Pairs

In this section, we review our construction of Hopf monads from
pivotal pairs
in monoidal categories, which appeared in
[
27
]
. We will be assuming that the base category
\mathcal{C}
is closed and admits countable colimits. Thereby,
\otimes
commutes with colimits and the category of endofunctors
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
also has countable colimits. First we will recall the notion of pivotal pairs and their categories of intertwined objects.

Notation:
Throughout this section, we write
X
instead of the morphism
\mathrm{id}_{X}
for brevity.

Recall from
[
27
]
, that we call a pair of objects
(P,Q)
a
pivotal pair
in
\mathcal{C}
if there exist a quadruple of morphisms
\mathrm{coev}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow P\otimes Q
,
\mathrm{ev}:Q\otimes P\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
and
\underline{\mathrm{coev}}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow Q\otimes P
,
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}:P\otimes Q\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
, making
Q
a left and right dual of
P
, respectively. In fact, whenever we talk about a pivotal pair, we always make an implicit choice for such morphisms.

Given a pivotal pair
(P,Q)
, we defined the category of
P
and
Q

intertwined objects
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, as the category whose objects are pairs
(X,\sigma)
, where
X
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
\sigma:X\otimes P\rightarrow P\otimes X
an invertible morphism in
\mathcal{C}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\sigma\otimes Q)(Q\otimes X\otimes\mathrm{coev}):Q\otimes X\rightarrow X\otimes Q

(53)

\displaystyle(Q\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(Q\otimes\sigma^{-1}\otimes Q)(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q):X\otimes Q\rightarrow Q\otimes X

(54)

are inverses. Morphisms between objects
(X,\sigma)
,
(Y,\tau)
of
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
are morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, which satisfy
\tau(f\otimes P)=(P\otimes f)\sigma
. For an object
(X,\sigma)
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, we call
\sigma
a
P
-
intertwining
and denote the induced morphisms (
53
) and (
54
), by
\overline{\sigma}
and
\overline{\sigma}^{-1}
, respectively, and call them
induced

Q
-intertwinings
.

The category
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
obtains a natural monoidal structure by defining
(X,\sigma)\otimes(Y,\tau)
to be the pair
(X\otimes Y,(\sigma\otimes Y)(X\otimes\tau))
[Theorem 4.1
[
27
]
]. It was already noted in
[
27
]
that the category
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
can be viewed as the dual of a strong monoidal functor in the sense of Section
2.3
. The choice of a pivotal pair
(P,Q)
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to the choice of a strict monoidal functor from the monoidal category generated by a single pivotal object, which we denote by
\mathrm{Piv}(1)
. The category
\mathrm{Piv}(1)
is the monoidal category generated by two objects
+
and
-
and two pairs of duality morphisms making
-
both the left and right dual of
+
. It should be clear that given any pivotal pair in
\mathcal{C}
, we have a strict monoidal functor
\omega_{(P,Q)}:\mathrm{Piv}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
which sends
+
to
P
and
-
to
Q
and the relevant duality morphisms to the duality morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 4.13
.

If
(P,Q)
is a pivotal pair in
\mathcal{C}
and
\omega:\mathrm{Piv}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
its corresponding functor, then there is a monoidal isomorphism
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)\cong\left({}_{\mathrm{Piv}(1)}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
which commutes with the forgetful functors from each category to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

In one direction, any object
(A,\sigma)
of
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
has a natural braiding
\mathbf{\sigma}:A\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\omega}\otimes A
defined by
\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}=\sigma
and
\mathbf{\sigma}_{Q}=\overline{\sigma}^{-1}
. It follows by definition that the duality morphisms between
P
and
Q
commute with these braidings and that
(A,\mathbf{\sigma})
belongs to
\left({}_{\mathrm{Piv}(1)}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
. Conversely, for any object
(A,\mathbf{\sigma})
in
\left({}_{\mathrm{Piv}(1)}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
the pair
(A,\mathbf{\sigma}_{P})
is an object in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, since
\overline{\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}}^{-1}
and
\overline{\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}}
become inverses because of
\sigma
commuting with the duality morphisms e.g.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}}\ \overline{\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}}^{-1}=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}\otimes Q)(Q\otimes X\otimes\mathrm{coev})(Q\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}^{-1}\otimes Q)

\displaystyle(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}\otimes Q)(Q\otimes X\otimes\mathrm{coev})\sigma_{Q}\sigma_{Q}^{-1}(Q\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}^{-1}\otimes Q)

\displaystyle(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}\otimes Q)(\sigma_{Q}\otimes P\otimes Q)(X\otimes Q\otimes\mathrm{coev})(X\otimes Q\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})

\displaystyle(\sigma_{Q}^{-1}\otimes P\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\mathbf{\sigma}_{P}^{-1}\otimes Q)(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(X\otimes\mathrm{ev}\otimes Q)(X\otimes Q\otimes\mathrm{coev})(X\otimes Q\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes Q)=\mathrm{id}_{X\otimes Q}

It should be clear that the described correspondence is a monoidal isomorphism of categories preserving the underlying object of the pairs in each category.∎

Consequently, all the properties of the dual of a monoidal functor which we discussed in Section
2.3
hold for
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
. For example, by Theorem
2.3
we can conclude:

Corollary 4.14
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a left (right) closed monoidal category, then
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
has a unique left (right) closed monoidal structure lifting that of
\mathcal{C}
, such that the forgetful functor
U
becomes left (right) closed.

By Corollary
4.14
, we know that when
\mathcal{C}
is closed we obtain a
P
-intertwining on
[A,B]^{l(r)}
corresponding to every pair of objects
(A,\sigma_{A})
and
(B,\sigma_{B})
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
. The precise definition of the induced
P
-intertwinings on the inner-homs in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
can be found in Section 4 of
[
27
]
.

Next we recall how we can construct a Hopf monad whose Eilenberg-Moore category recovers
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
from Section 5 of
[
27
]
. We will call a morphism
F(X)\rightarrow X
, for any endofunctor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, an
action
of
F
on an object
X
. Although the functors in question will not carry any monad structures, we will build a monad on the colimit of a diagram of these functors, so that these
actions
induce a genuine module structure over the resulting monad, thereby justifying our terminology.

Observe that for a pair
(X,\sigma)
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, we can view
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
as certain actions of the functors
Q\otimes-\otimes P
and
P\otimes-\otimes Q
on
X
:

\textstyle{Q\otimes X\otimes P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X)(Q\otimes\sigma)}
\textstyle{X}

\textstyle{P\otimes X\otimes Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(\sigma^{-1}\otimes Q)}
\textstyle{X}

Moreover, for any pair
(X,\sigma)
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, we can translate the mentioned actions in terms of the induced
Q
-intertwinings, since
(X\otimes\mathrm{ev})(\overline{\sigma}\otimes P)=(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X)(Q\otimes\sigma)
and
(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}\otimes X)(P\otimes\overline{\sigma}^{-1})=(X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(\sigma^{-1}\otimes Q)
.

Conversely, when provided with two morphisms
\alpha:Q\otimes X\otimes P\rightarrow X
and
\beta:P\otimes X\otimes Q\rightarrow X
, we can recover right and left
P
-intertwinings as set out below:

\textstyle{X\otimes P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(P\otimes\alpha)(\mathrm{coev}\otimes X\otimes P)}
\textstyle{P\otimes X}

\textstyle{P\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\beta\otimes Q)(P\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}})}
\textstyle{X\otimes P}

If we want the induced
P
-intertwinings of
\alpha
and
\beta
to be inverses, we need the following equalities to hold:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}\otimes X=\alpha(Q\otimes\beta\otimes P)(Q\otimes P\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}):Q\otimes P\otimes X\rightarrow X

(55)

\displaystyle X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}}=\beta(P\otimes\alpha\otimes Q)(\mathrm{coev}\otimes X\otimes P\otimes Q):X\otimes P\otimes Q\rightarrow X

(56)

Similarly,
\alpha
and
\beta
induce
Q
-intertwinings, (
53
) and (
54
) which can be written as

\textstyle{X\otimes Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(P\otimes\beta)(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q)}
\textstyle{X\otimes Q}

\textstyle{Q\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha\otimes Q)(Q\otimes X\otimes\mathrm{coev})}
\textstyle{X\otimes Q}

In order for the induced
Q
-intertwinings to be inverses, we require the following equalities to hold:

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{ev}}\otimes X=\beta(P\otimes\alpha\otimes Q)(P\otimes Q\otimes X\otimes\mathrm{coev}):P\otimes Q\otimes X\rightarrow X

(57)

\displaystyle X\otimes\mathrm{ev}=\alpha(Q\otimes\beta\otimes P)(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes X\otimes Q\otimes P):X\otimes Q\otimes P\rightarrow X

(58)

With this view of
P
-intertwinings in mind, we construct the left adjoint functor to the forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{C}(P,Q)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

Define the endofunctors
F_{+},F_{-}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
by
F_{+}(X)=Q\otimes X\otimes P
and
F_{-}(X)=P\otimes X\otimes Q
. Let the endofunctor
F^{\star}
be defined as the coproduct

F^{\star}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in\{-,+\}^{n}}F_{i_{1}}F_{i_{2}}\cdots F_{i_{n}}

where the term
F_{i_{1}}F_{i_{2}}\cdots F_{i_{n}}
at
n=0
, is just the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. For arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in\{-,+\}^{n}
, we denote
F_{i_{1}}F_{i_{2}}\cdots F_{i_{n}}
by
F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
and the respective natural transformations
F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\Rightarrow F^{\star}
by
\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
. We denote the additional natural transformation
\mathrm{id}\Rightarrow F^{\star}
by
\iota_{0}
. Hence, for any
F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
we have four parallel pairs:

\textstyle{P\otimes Q\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{-,+,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(P\otimes Q\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes\mathrm{coev})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}})}
\textstyle{F^{\star}}

\textstyle{F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes Q\otimes P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{+,-,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes Q\otimes P)}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes\mathrm{ev})}
\textstyle{F^{\star}}

\textstyle{Q\otimes P\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{+,-,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(Q\otimes P\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(\mathrm{ev}\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}})}
\textstyle{F^{\star}}

\textstyle{F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes P\otimes Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{-,+,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(\mathrm{coev}\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes P\otimes Q)}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})}
\textstyle{F^{\star}}

Consider the diagram in
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
which the described parallel pairs create. We denote the colimit of this diagram by
T
, the unique natural transformation
F^{\star}\Rightarrow T
, by
\psi
, and the compositions
\psi\iota_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
and
\psi\iota_{0}
, by
\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
and
\psi_{0}
, respectively.

Since
\otimes
commutes with colimits, the family of morphisms
\psi_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}:Q\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes P\rightarrow T
induce a unique morphism
\alpha:Q\otimes T\otimes P\rightarrow T
such that
\alpha(Q\otimes\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes P)=\psi_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
. Similarly, the family of morphisms
\psi_{-,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}:P\otimes F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes Q\rightarrow T
induce a morphism
\beta:P\otimes T\otimes Q\rightarrow T
such that
\beta(P\otimes\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes Q)=\psi_{-,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
. As mentioned at the start of the section, such actions
\alpha
and
\beta
provide us with the necessary
P
-intertwinings.

Let us denote the natural transformation
(P\otimes\alpha)(\mathrm{coev}\otimes T\otimes P):T\otimes P\Rightarrow P\otimes T
by
\sigma^{T}
. In Lemma 5.1 of
[
27
]
, we demonstrated that indeed for any object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, the pair
F(X):=(T(X),\sigma^{T}_{X})
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
. Additionally, the assignment
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
is functorial by construction where with
F(f)=T(f)
for morphisms
f
of
\mathcal{C}
. Finally, in Theorem 5.2 of
[
27
]
, we showed that
F
is left adjoint to the relevant forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{C}(P,Q)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

The unit of the adjunction
F\dashv U
has already appeared as
\nu:=\psi_{0}:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow UF=T
. For the counit, consider a pair
(X,\sigma)
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
and denote its induced actions
(\mathrm{ev}\otimes X)(Q\otimes\sigma)
and
(X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(\sigma^{-1}\otimes Q)
by
\alpha_{\sigma}:F_{+}(X)\rightarrow X
and
\beta_{\sigma}:F_{-}(X)\rightarrow X
, respectively. One can then define
\theta_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}:F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\rightarrow X
, for arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in\{-,+\}^{n}
, by applying
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\beta_{\sigma}
iteratively so that
\theta_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}=\alpha_{\sigma}(Q\otimes\theta_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes P)
and
\theta_{-,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}=\beta_{\sigma}(P\otimes\theta_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes Q)
, where
\theta_{+}=\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\theta_{-}=\beta_{\sigma}
. Together with
\theta_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, we obtain a family of morphisms from
F_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}(X)
to
X
, which must factorise through
F^{\star}(X)
. In Theorem 5.2 of
[
27
]
, we denoted the unique morphism
F^{\star}(X)\rightarrow X
by
\theta^{\star}
and observed that the family of morphisms described commute with the parallel pairs (
4.5
), (
4.5
), (
4.5
) and (
4.5
), in a way that
\theta^{\star}
must factorise through
T(X)
. Hence, there exists a unique morphism
\theta_{(X,\sigma)}:T(X)\rightarrow X
such that
\theta_{(X,\sigma)}\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}=\theta_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
. It then follows that
\theta
is a morphism between
(T(X),\sigma^{T}_{X})
and
(X,\sigma)
in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
, and
(P\otimes\theta_{(X,\sigma)})\sigma^{T}_{X}=\sigma(\theta_{(X,\sigma)}\otimes P)
holds with
\theta
becoming a natural transformation
\theta:FU\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(P,Q)}
acting as the counit of
F\dashv U
.

Now let us reflect on the the bimonad structure on the functor
T:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. First, we observe the monad structure. By definition, for any pair
F(X)=(T(X),\sigma^{T}_{X})
, the multiplication of the monad
\theta_{F(X)}:TT(X)\rightarrow T(X)
will be the unique morphism such that

\theta_{F(X)}(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{T(X)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big((\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{X}\big)=(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{X}

for arbitrary non-negative integers
n,m
and
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n},j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m}\in\{+,-\}
. Meanwhile, the unit of the monad was provided in the definition of
T
as
\psi_{0}:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow T
. The comonoidal structure on
T
arises directly from the monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
. Observe that for pairs
(X,\sigma)
and
(Y,\tau)
the induced action
\alpha_{\sigma\otimes\tau}
on
A\otimes B
is the composition
(\alpha_{\sigma}\otimes\alpha_{\tau})(P\otimes X\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes Y\otimes Q)
. Consequently, we can obtain the comonoidal structure of
T
,
T_{2}:T(-\otimes-)\rightarrow T(-)\otimes T(-)
, by Theorem
3.2
as the unique morphism satisfying

T_{2}\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}=\big(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big)F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(-\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes-)

where
\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow F_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}}(\mathtt{1})
are iteratively defined by
\mathrm{coev}_{+}=\mathrm{coev}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{-}=\underline{\mathrm{coev}}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{\pm,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}=F_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}}(\mathrm{coev}_{\pm})\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
. Note that the
P
-intertwining making
\mathtt{1}
the unit of the monoidal structure in
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
is simply the identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{P}
and its induced actions are
\alpha_{\mathrm{id}_{P}}=\mathrm{ev}
and
\beta_{\mathrm{id}_{P}}=\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
. Hence, we obtain
T_{0}:T(\mathtt{1})\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
as the unique morphism satisfying
T_{0}\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}=\mathrm{ev}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}
, where
\mathrm{ev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}:F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(\mathtt{1})\rightarrow\mathtt{1}
is defined iteratively by
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm,i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}=\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}F_{\pm}(\mathrm{ev}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}})
with
\mathrm{ev}_{+}=\mathrm{ev}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{-}=\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
.

In Corollary 5.3 of
[
27
]
, we noted that since
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
lifts the closed monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
via
U
when
\mathcal{C}
is closed, then
T
will be a Hopf monad under our base assumptions. Equivalently, since
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
can be viewed as the dual of a strong monoidal functor, this is an application of Theorem
2.3
. However, this is the only point where we needed
\mathcal{C}
to be closed in
[
27
]
. The other application of this condition was the fact that
-\otimes-
would preserve colimits in both entries as a consequence. Here we will show that
T
will be a Hopf monad under more relaxed conditions:

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category (not necessarily closed) where
\otimes
preserves colimits in both entries and
(P,Q)
be a pivotal pair and assume suitable colimits exist so that the bimonad
T
can be constructed as before. In this case,
T
is a Hopf monad and the fusion operators of Definition
3.10
are invertible.

Proof.

We will discuss this for the left fusion operator and leave the computations for the right fusion operator to the reader. For a pair of arbitrary objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, we first note that because
\otimes
preserves colimits in each entry and, thereby, functors
F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
also preserve colimits, then
T(X\otimes T(Y))
and
T(X)\otimes T(Y)
both become colimits with respect to the following families of morphisms:

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}):

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))\rightarrow T(X\otimes T(Y))

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}:

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\rightarrow T(X)\otimes T(Y)

We define the inverse
Q_{X,Y}
of
H^{l}_{X,Y}
as the unique morphism satisfying

\displaystyle Q_{X,Y}\big((\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)=

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)

\displaystyle(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

Using this definition and the universal property of
T
with respect to iterations of (
4.5
) and (
4.5
), we now show that
Q_{X,Y}H^{l}_{X,Y}=\mathrm{id}_{T(X\otimes T(Y))}
:

\displaystyle Q

{}_{X,Y}H^{l}_{X,Y}(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{X,Y}(\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\theta_{F(Y)})T_{2}(X,T(Y))(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{X,Y}(\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\theta_{F(Y)})\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{T(Y)}\big)

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{X,Y}\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{n},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)

\displaystyle(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

\displaystyle\big(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)

By the universal property of
T(X\otimes T(Y))
we conclude that
Q_{X,Y}H^{l}_{X,Y}=\mathrm{id}_{T(X\otimes T(Y))}
. By a similar argument we see that
H^{l}_{X,Y}Q_{X,Y}=\mathrm{id}_{T(X)\otimes T(Y)}
:

\displaystyle H

{}^{l}_{X,Y}Q_{X,Y}\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)=(\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\theta_{F(Y)})T_{2}(X,T(Y))(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X\otimes T(Y)}

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{T(X)}\otimes\theta_{F(Y)})\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{T(Y)}\big)F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes T(Y))

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\big(X\otimes(\psi_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n},-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes F_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))

\displaystyle(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{-i_{n},\dots,-i_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big((\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)\big(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\big)

\displaystyle\big(F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y)\big)=(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}

Here we used the universal property of
T
with respect to iterations of (
4.5
) and (
4.5
). The inverse of the right fusion operator is the unique map satisfying:

\displaystyle(H^{r}_{X,Y})^{-1}\big((\psi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes(\psi_{j_{1},i_{2},\dots,j_{m}})_{Y}\big)=(\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{T(X)\otimes Y}

\displaystyle F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}((\psi_{-j_{m},\dots,-j_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{X}\otimes Y)(\mathrm{coev}_{-j_{m},\dots,-j_{1}}\otimes F_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(X)\otimes F_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(Y))

and the corresponding computation follows in a symmetric manner.
∎

Finally, we recall the criterion for when the constructed Hopf monad is augmented from
[
27
]
. We say a pivotal pair
(P,Q)
in
\mathcal{C}

lifts to

Z(C)
, if there exist braidings
\lambda:P\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes P
and
\chi:Q\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes Q
, such that
(P,\lambda)
and
(Q,\chi)
are objects in
Z(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathrm{coev},\mathrm{ev},\underline{\mathrm{coev}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
become morphisms in
Z(\mathcal{C})
, making
(P,\lambda)
and
(Q,\chi)
a pivotal pair in
Z(\mathcal{C})
.

Theorem 4.16

(Theorem 5.4
[
27
]
)
.

The Hopf monad
T
is augmented if and only if the pivotal pair
(P,Q)
lifts to
Z(\mathcal{C})
.

Sketch of proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assuming
T
is augmented with a Hopf monad morphism
\epsilon:T\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
, we obtain a braiding on
P
by
\lambda:=(\epsilon\psi_{-}\otimes P)(P\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}})
. Additionally,
\lambda
is invertible and
\lambda^{-1}:=(P\otimes\epsilon\psi_{+})(\mathrm{coev}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes P)
. In a symmetric manner, one can introduce
\chi:=(\epsilon\psi_{+}\otimes Q)(Q\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathrm{coev})
with
\chi^{-1}:=(Q\otimes\epsilon\psi_{-})(\underline{\mathrm{coev}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes Q)
as its inverse and show that
\mathrm{coev},\mathrm{ev},\underline{\mathrm{coev}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
become morphisms in
Z(\mathcal{C})
and commute with the braidings of
\mathtt{1}
,
P\otimes Q
and
Q\otimes P
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assuming there exist braidings
\lambda:P\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes P
and
\chi:Q\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes Q
making
(P,\lambda)
and
(Q,\chi)
objects in
Z(\mathcal{C})
, such that
\mathrm{coev},\mathrm{ev},\underline{\mathrm{coev}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
are morphisms in
Z(\mathcal{C})
, we can iteratively define the natural transformations
\epsilon_{i_{1},\dots i_{n}}:F_{i_{1},\dots i_{n}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
by
\epsilon_{+,i_{1},\dots i_{n}}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathrm{ev})(\chi\otimes P)F_{+}(\epsilon_{i_{1},\dots i_{n}})
and
\epsilon_{-,i_{1},\dots i_{n}}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}})(\lambda\otimes Q)F_{-}(\epsilon_{i_{1},\dots i_{n}})
where
\epsilon_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Since
\mathrm{ev}
and
\underline{\mathrm{ev}}
commute with the braidings, then
\epsilon_{+}=(\mathrm{ev}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})(Q\otimes\lambda^{-1})
and
\epsilon_{-}=(\underline{\mathrm{ev}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})(P\otimes\chi^{-1})
. It is straightforward to check that
\epsilon_{i_{1},\dots i_{n}}
commute with the parallel pairs (
4.5
), (
4.5
), (
4.5
) and (
4.5
), and therefore induce a unique morphism
\epsilon:T\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
and from the universal properties of
TT
and
T(-\otimes-)
, we can conclude that
\epsilon
is a bimonad morphism.

\diamond

In Examples 5.6 of
[
27
]
, we looked at the case when
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}
. In this case, pivotal pairs in
\mathsf{Vec}
correspond to invertible matrices and given any such matrix, we obtain an involutive Hopf algebra
H
such that
T\cong H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
. On the other hand, as we will see in Theorem
4.19
, additive Hopf monads on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
which admit a right adjoint correspond to Hopf algebroids over
A
, in the sense of Schauenburg
[
56
]
. In Theorem 4.3 of
[
26
]
, we constructed the relevant Hopf algebroids which arise in this way by considering a pivotal pair
(P,Q)
in
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.

4.6
Bialgebroids and Hopf Algebroids

In this section we will review the theory of bialgebroids and Schauenburg’s notion of Hopf algebroids as examples of bimonads and Hopf monads on the category of bimodules
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
. The notation used for describing Hopf algebroids varies quite a bit depending on the reference, but here we adapt the notation from Chapter 5 of
[
9
]
. Throughout this section
A
denotes a fixed
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
\otimes
denotes
\otimes_{A}
, unless it appears with a different subscript.

For an algebra
A
, the
opposite algebra

A^{\mathrm{op}}
is the algebra structure defined on
A
by
(\overline{a})(\overline{b})=\overline{ba}
, where we denote elements of the opposite algebra with a line above i.e
a,b\in A
and
\overline{a},\overline{b}\in A^{\mathrm{op}}
. It is a well-known fact that
A
-bimodules correspond to left
A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A^{\mathrm{op}}
-modules, where
A^{e}=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A^{\mathrm{op}}
is called the
enveloping algebra
of
A
. More concretely, there exists an isomorphism of categories, between the category of
A
-bimodules
\prescript{}{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and that of left
A^{e}
-modules
\prescript{}{A^{e}}{\mathcal{M}}
. Hence, we use
{}_{A^{e}}{\mathcal{M}}
and
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
interchangeably. We will denote elements of
A^{e}=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A^{\mathrm{op}}
by
a\overline{b}
where
a\in A
and
\overline{b}\in A^{\mathrm{op}}
. The reader should note, that other authors often prefer the notation
