a\overline{b}=\mathsf{s}(a)\mathsf{t}(b)
, where the algebra morphisms
A\hookrightarrow A^{e}\rightarrow H
and
A^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow A^{e}\rightarrow H
are denoted by
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{t}
and called
source
and
target
, respectively.

For an
A^{e}
-bimodule
B
we denote the functor
\prescript{}{A^{e}}{B}\otimes_{A^{e}}-
by
B\boxtimes-:{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
. This functor absorbs the bimodule structure of its input via its right
A^{e}
-action and produces a new bimodule actions via its left
A^{e}
-action. Explicitly, for an
A
-bimodule
M
we have

\displaystyle B\boxtimes M=B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M/\{(br\overline{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m

\displaystyle-b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(rms)\mid m\in M,\ r,s\in A,\ b\in B\}

\displaystyle r(b\boxtimes m)s=(r\overline{s}b)\boxtimes m

\displaystyle\quad\forall m\in M,\ \forall r,s\in A,\ \forall b\in B

Note that any
A^{e}
-bimodule
B
can be considered as an
A
-bimodule either by its right or left
A^{e}
-action, and we denote the latter
A
-bimodule by
|B
. We continue to adapt the notation of
[
9
]
and recall the following definitions from Chapter 5.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
A
be an algebra and
B
an
A^{e}
-bimodule.

(I)

An
A^{e}
-
ring
structure on
B
consists of a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra structure
(\mu,1_{B})
on
B
with an algebra homomorphism
\eta:A^{e}\rightarrow B
, such that the
A^{e}
-bimodule structure on
B
is induced by the algebra homomorphism i.e.
\mu(\eta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{B})
coincides with the left action of
A^{e}
and
\mu(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\eta)
with the right action of
A^{e}
.

(II)

An
A|A
-
coring
structure on
B
consists of bimodule maps
\Delta:|B\rightarrow|B\otimes|B
and
\epsilon:|B\rightarrow A
satisfying

\displaystyle b_{(1)}\otimes(b_{(2)})_{(1)}\otimes(b_{(2)})_{(2)}=

\displaystyle(b_{(1)})_{(1)}\otimes(b_{(1)})_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)}

(67)

\displaystyle\epsilon(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}=b

\displaystyle=\overline{\epsilon(b_{(2)})}b_{(1)}

(68)

\displaystyle\Delta(br\overline{s})=

\displaystyle b_{(1)}r\otimes b_{(2)}\overline{s}

(69)

\displaystyle\epsilon(br)=

\displaystyle\epsilon(b\overline{r})

(70)

for any
b\in B
and
r,s\in A
, where
\Delta(b)=b_{(1)}\otimes b_{(2)}
is denoted by Sweedler’s notation as in the case of Hopf algebras. Conditions (
67
) and (
68
) are equivalent to
(|B,\Delta,\epsilon)
being a comonoid in the category of
A
-bimodules.

(III)

A
left

A
-
bialgebroid
structure on
B
consists of an
A^{e}
-ring structure
(\mu,\eta)
and an
A|A
-coring structure
(\Delta,\epsilon)
on
B
satisfying

\displaystyle(bb^{\prime})_{(1)}\otimes(bb^{\prime})_{(2)}

\displaystyle=b_{(1)}b_{(1)}^{\prime}\otimes b_{(2)}b_{(2)}^{\prime}

(71)

\displaystyle\Delta(1_{B})

\displaystyle=1_{B}\otimes 1_{B}

(72)

\displaystyle\epsilon(1_{B})

\displaystyle=1_{A}

(73)

\displaystyle\epsilon(bb^{\prime})=\epsilon

\displaystyle(b\epsilon(b^{\prime}))=\epsilon\left(b\overline{\epsilon(b^{\prime})}\right)

(74)

for any
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, where
1_{B}=\eta(1_{A^{e}})
.

First note that an
A^{e}
-ring structure as defined above is equivalent picking
A^{e}
-bimodule maps
\mu_{A^{e}}:B\otimes_{A^{e}}B\rightarrow B
and
\eta_{A^{e}}:A^{e}\rightarrow B
, which provide
B
with the structure of a monoid in the monoidal category of
A^{e}
-bimodules. Secondly, we can deduce from the definition of an
A|A
-coring
B
that the image of
\Delta
lands in

B\times_{A}B:=\left\{\sum_{i}b_{i}\otimes b^{\prime}_{i}\in|B\otimes|B\middle|\sum_{i}b_{i}\overline{a}\otimes b^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{i}b_{i}\otimes b^{\prime}_{i}a,\ a\in A\right\}\subset|B\otimes|B

Bialgebroids are often defined with reference to
B\times_{A}B
, the
Takeuchi
\times
-product

[
63
]
, and therefore called
\times
-bialgebras. The equivalence of the above definition and the more popular variation is present in both
[
9
,
11
]
.

It is easy to check that
A^{e}
-ring structures on
B
correspond to monad structures on the endofunctor
B\boxtimes-
(Proposition 5.2
[
9
]
) since there is a monoidal embedding of
{}_{A^{e}}\mathcal{M}_{A^{e}}
into
\mathrm{End}({}_{A^{e}}{\mathcal{M}})
. In particular, by restriction of scalars along
\eta
, any
B
-module is equipped with an
A
-bimodule structure and there exists a forgetful functor
U:\prescript{}{B}{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
with its free left adjoint functor also denoted by
B\boxtimes-
. In this way we consider left actions
B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\rightarrow M
of
B
on
A
-bimodules
M
which factor through an
A
-bimodule map
B\boxtimes M\rightarrow M
.

Similarly,
A|A
-coring structures on
B
correspond to comonoidal structures on the functor
B\boxtimes-
(Proposition 5.5
[
9
]
). In our notation if
T=B\boxtimes-
, then
T_{2}(M,N)=\Delta_{M,N}
is defined as the map

\textstyle{\Delta_{M,N}:B\boxtimes(M\otimes N)\longrightarrow(B\boxtimes M)\otimes(B\boxtimes N)}
\textstyle{\hskip 52.63777ptb\boxtimes(m\otimes n)\longmapsto(b_{(1)}\boxtimes m)\otimes(b_{(2)}\boxtimes n)}

(75)

which is well-defined and a bimodule map for any pair of
A
-bimodules
M
and
N
by condition (
69
). Additionally, by (
70
) the map
\epsilon
factorizes through a map
T_{0}:B\boxtimes A\rightarrow A
. Furthermore, conditions (
67
) and (
68
) assure that (
1
) and (
2
) hold, respectively.

Given the above correspondences, the conditions in Definition
4.17
(III) become equivalent to the conditions in Definition
3.1
and bimonad structures on
B\boxtimes-
correspond precisely to left
A
-bialgebroid structures on
B
(Theorem 5.9
[
9
]
). From this point of view the category of
B
-modules lifts the monoidal structure of
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
: If
(M,\triangleright_{M})
and
(N,\triangleright_{N})
are
B
-modules, the
B
-action on
M\otimes N
given by (
22
) is defined by the composition
(\triangleright_{M}\otimes\triangleright_{N})\Delta_{M,N}
.

We must point out that the theory described above is not symmetric. A
right

A
-bialgebroid structure on
B
arises when we ask the category of right
B
-modules to be monoidal so that the forgetful functor
\mathcal{M}_{B}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
becomes strong monoidal. In other words, right
A
-bialgebroid structures are those which make the functor
-\boxtimes B=-\otimes_{A^{e}}B_{A^{e}}
a bimonad. From here onwards an
A
-bialgebroid structure always refers to a left
A
-bialgebroid structure.

There have been several variations of the Hopf condition for bialgebroids to mimic the Hopf condition for bialgebras. The choice which interests us is the condition which makes the corresponding bimonad of a bialgebroid
B
into a Hopf monad. This is the case for Schauenburg’s Hopf algebroids which were introduced in
[
56
]
.

Definition 4.18
.

A
Schauenburg Hopf algebroid
or
\times
-
Hopf algebra
structure on
B
consists of an
A
-bialgebroid structure as above, such that the induced maps

\textstyle{\beta:B\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}B\longrightarrow B\diamond B}
\textstyle{\vartheta:B\odot B\longrightarrow B\diamond B}
\textstyle{\hskip 34.14322ptb\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}b^{\prime}\mapsto b_{(1)}\diamond b_{(2)}b^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\hskip 34.14322ptb\odot b^{\prime}\mapsto b_{(1)}b^{\prime}\diamond b_{(2)}}

are invertible, where we define the tensor products
\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}
,
\odot
and
\diamond
as follows:

\displaystyle B\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}B

\displaystyle=B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}B/\{b\overline{s}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b^{\prime}-b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\overline{s}b^{\prime}\mid b,b^{\prime}\in B,\overline{s}\in A^{\mathrm{op}}\}

\displaystyle B\odot B

\displaystyle=B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}B/\{br\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b^{\prime}-b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}rb^{\prime}\mid b,b^{\prime}\in B,r\in A\}

\displaystyle B\diamond B

\displaystyle=B\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}B/\{\overline{s}b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}b^{\prime}-b\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}sb^{\prime}\mid b,b^{\prime}\in B,s\in A\}

Once one writes down the fusion operators for the bimonad
T=B\boxtimes-
, it is easy to see that the maps
\beta
and
\vartheta
being invertible are equivalent to the bimonad
T=B\boxtimes-
being left and right Hopf, respectively. Consequently, if
B
is a Schauenburg Hopf algebroid and
\beta,\vartheta
are invertible, we usually denote
\beta^{-1}(b\diamond 1)=b_{(+)}\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}b_{(-)}
and
\vartheta^{-1}(1\diamond b)=b_{[+]}\odot b_{[-]}
and thereby observe that the closed structure of
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is lifted to
\prescript{}{B}{\mathcal{M}}
via the following
B
-actions:

\textstyle{B\boxtimes\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,N)}
\textstyle{B\boxtimes\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,N)\rightarrow\prescript{}{A}{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,N)}
\textstyle{b\boxtimes f\mapsto(m\mapsto b_{(+)}f(b_{(-)}m))}
\textstyle{b\boxtimes g\mapsto(m\mapsto b_{[+]}g(b_{[-]}m))}

(80)

for any pair of
B
-bimodules
M
and
N
.

Finally, we refer the reader to Chapter 5 of
[
9
]
and
[
11
]
for further details on these facts. We conclude by presenting the following observation. The Eilenberg-Watts theorem
[
66
]
tells us that any additive left adjoint functor
F:{}_{A^{e}}{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow{}_{A^{e}}{\mathcal{M}}
is isomorphic to a functor
\prescript{}{A^{e}}{B}\otimes_{A^{e}}-
, where
B
is an
A^{e}
-bimodule. Using this result one can classify additive left adjoint bimonads and Hopf monads on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
:

Theorem 4.19

(
[
62
]
)
.

For an algebra
A
and an abelian monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, if
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is an additive functor with a left adjoint
F
, such that
GF:{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
has a right adjoint , then
G
is (closed) strong monoidal if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to
\prescript{}{B}{\mathcal{M}}
for a left (Hopf) bialgebroid
B
.

This result was translated into the language of Hopf monads in
[
12
]
and simply states that colimit-preserving (Hopf monad) bimonads on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
correspond to (Schauenburg Hopf algebroids) left bialgebroids over
A
.

Remark 4.20
.

We should warn the reader that the notion of a Hopf algebroid over an algebra
A
described here is not the same as the notion of a
weak Hopf algebra
. However, any weak Hopf algebra structure defined over a field
\mathbb{K}
is equivalent to a Hopf algebroid structure over a separable Frobenius
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
A
. The details of this correspondence are explained in Chapter 6 of
[
9
]
.

5
Combining Hopf monads and Bimonads

In this section, we review two ways in which we can combine Hopf monads to obtain new Hopf monads: Bosonisation or cross product, and distributive laws.

5.1
Bosonisation and Cross Products

In this section we review the theory of cross products or bosonisation for Hopf monads which describes how we can compose a Hopf monad with a secondary Hopf monad on its module category. This in turn generalises Radford’s biproduct construction and Majid’s bosonisation in the theory of ordinary Hopf algebras.

Assume
T
is a monad on
\mathcal{C}
and
P
is a monad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. Then we can consider the composition of the following adjunctions

\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{P}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{P}}
\scriptstyle{U_{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{T}}

In this situation, the endofunctor
U_{T}PF_{T}
is called the
cross product
of
T
by
P
and is denoted by
P\rtimes T
. If
\eta
and
\epsilon
denote the unit and counit of the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
and
\eta^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}
denote the unit and multiplication providing the monad structure on
P
, then the monad structure
(P\rtimes T,q,\nu)
is defined as follows

q=(U_{T}\mu^{\prime}_{F_{T}})(U_{T}P\epsilon_{PF_{T}}):U_{T}PF_{T}U_{T}PF_{T}\rightarrow U_{T}PF_{T},\quad\nu=(U_{T}\eta^{\prime}_{F_{T}})\eta

(81)

Observe that although
P\rtimes T
is the corresponding monad of the adjunction
F_{P}F_{T}\dashv U_{T}U_{P}
, which is the composition of two monadic adjunctions,
F_{P}F_{T}\dashv U_{T}U_{P}
itself is not necessarily monadic. In other words the comparison functor
K:\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{P\rtimes T}
might not be an equivalence.

Theorem 5.1

(Proposition 5.1
[
4
]
)
.

If
T
is a monad on
\mathcal{C}
and
P
a monad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
which preserves reflexive coequalizers then the adjunction
F_{P}F_{T}\dashv U_{T}U_{P}
is monadic. Moreover, the comparison functor
K:\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{P\rtimes T}
is an isomorphism of categories.

If
\mathcal{C}
is monoidal and
T
and
P
are both bimonads then
U_{T}
and
U_{P}
are both strong monoidal and thereby so is their composition
U_{T}U_{P}
. Therefore the cross product of two bimonads also becomes a bimonad, where
P\rtimes T
obtains a canonical comonoidal structure as the composition of comonoidal functors. In fact one can show that the composition of Hopf monads also satisfies the Hopf condition:

Theorem 5.2

(Proposition 4.4
[
12
]
)
.

The cross product of two (left or right) Hopf monads is also a (left or right) Hopf monad.

Sketch of proof.

Let us assume that
T
and
P
are both left Hopf monads and denote the units and counits of adjunctions
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
and
F_{P}\dashv U_{P}
by
\eta
,
\epsilon
and
\eta^{\prime}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}
, respectively. We observe that the left fusion operator of the adjunction
F_{P}F_{T}\dashv U_{T}U_{P}
appears in the left edge of the commuting rectangle below:

\textstyle{F_{P}F_{T}(X\otimes U_{T}U_{P}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F_{P})_{2}(F_{T}(X),F_{T}U_{T}U_{P}(Y))(F_{T})_{2}(X,U_{T}U_{P}(Y))}
\scriptstyle{F_{P}(F_{T})_{2}(X,U_{T}U_{P}(Y))}
\textstyle{F_{P}(F_{T}(X)\otimes F_{T}U_{T}U_{P}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{P}\left(\mathrm{id}_{F_{T}(X)}\otimes\epsilon_{U_{P}(Y)}\right)}
\textstyle{F_{P}F_{T}(X)\otimes F_{P}F_{T}U_{T}U_{P}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}_{F_{P}F_{T}(X)}\otimes\epsilon^{\prime}_{Y}F_{P}\epsilon_{U_{P}(Y)})}
\textstyle{F_{P}(F_{T}(X)\otimes U_{P}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F_{P})_{2}(F_{T}(X),U_{P}(Y))}
\textstyle{F_{P}F_{T}(X)\otimes Y}
\textstyle{F_{P}F_{T}(X)\otimes F_{P}U_{P}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{F_{P}F_{T}(X)}\otimes\epsilon^{\prime}_{Y}}

(82)

Since the above diagram commutes, the left fusion operator of the composed adjunction can be written as the composition of the two fusion operators of
T
and
P
. Hence, if
T
and
P
both have invertible left fusion operators, then so does
P\rtimes T
. A symmetric argument can be applied to the right fusion operator.
\diamond

This notion of cross product for Hopf monads generalises Radford’s biproduct and Majid’s bosonisation for ordinary Hopf algebras. Given a bialgebra
(H,m_{H},1_{H},\Delta_{H},\epsilon_{H})
and an
H
-module algebra
A
i.e. an algebra
((A,\triangleright),m_{A},1_{A})
in the category of left
H
-modules
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
, we can form a new algebra on the vector space
A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
called the
cross product algebra

A\rtimes H
. This structure is exactly what we obtain by composing monads
T=H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
on
\mathsf{Vec}
and
P=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
on
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
, where we obtain a monad structure on
P\rtimes T=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
and an algebra structure on
A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
with
1_{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}1_{H}
as its unit and its multiplication defined by

\displaystyle(m_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H})(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\triangleright\otimes m_{H})(\mathrm{id}_{A\otimes H}\otimes\Psi_{H,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H})(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A\otimes H})

where
\triangleright:H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A\rightarrow A
denotes the
H
-module action on
A
and
\Psi
the symmetry on
\mathsf{Vec}
.

Now let us assume
(H,m_{H},1_{H},\Delta_{H},\epsilon_{H},S_{H})
is a Hopf algebra and that the category of
H
-modules is braided (in this case
H
has a quasitriangular structure, see Section
7.3
). In this case, one can pick an
H
-module Hopf algebra
((A,\triangleright),m_{A},1_{A},\Delta_{A},\epsilon_{A},S_{A})
i.e. a Hopf algebra in the braided category of
H
-modules, and as previously discussed
P=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
will have a natural left Hopf monad structure. By Theorem
5.2
the bimonad
P\rtimes T=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
will also be left Hopf. Hence, we obtain a new Hopf algebra
A\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H
where the coalgebra and antipode, now use the braiding
\Psi^{\prime}
of the category
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{A\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H}:=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Psi^{\prime}_{A,H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{H})(\Delta_{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Delta_{H})

\displaystyle S_{A\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H}:=

\displaystyle(\triangleright\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{H})(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Psi_{H,A})(\Delta_{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{A})\Psi^{\prime}_{A,H}(S_{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}S_{H})

This procedure is called
bosonisation
and was introduced by Majid in
[
45
]
.

Even if the category of
H
-modules is not necessarily braided, but
(A,\tau)
has a central Hopf algebra structure in
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
or equivalently
A
is a braided Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
, then we obtain a natural left Hopf monad
P=A\otimes_{\tau}-
on
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
. By Theorem
5.2
we again obtain a Hopf algebra structure
A\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H
on
A\otimes H
, where the coalgebra and antipode are defined in the same way but the braiding
\Psi^{\prime}_{A,H}
is replaced with the braiding
\tau_{H}
. This construction is referred to as
Radford’s biproduct
and first appeared in
[
53
]
without any reference to braided Hopf algebras. Its interpretation in terms of braided Hopf algebras appeared in the Appendix of
[
42
]
. While from the monadic point of view, the constructions look exactly the same, in bosonisation the braidings arise from the braiding on
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
via a quasitriangular structure on
H
, while in Radford’s biproduct the braiding
\tau
can be written in terms of the
H
-comodule structure on
A
making
A
a Yetter-Drinfeld module and the formulas take a very different look in this way. We refer the reader to Section 9.4 of
[
46
]
for further details on these constructions.

Example 5.3

(Cross product for Hopf algebroids)
.

Consider a Hopf algebroid
H
over a base algebra
A
and its corresponding Hopf monad
T=H\boxtimes-
on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
. By the described theory of cross products, we can define an analogous procedure for Hopf algebroids if we are given a braided Hopf algebra
B
in the center of
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
. This provides us with a Hopf monad
B\otimes_{A}-
on
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
and, by Theorem
5.2
, we can compose these monads to obtain a new Hopf monad on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
, which itself will correspond to a new Hopf algebroid over
A
, by Theorem
4.19
. Let us briefly describe the new Hopf algebroid structure on
B\otimes_{A}H
.

First, we recall from Proposition 4.4
[
56
]
that the lax left dual
\mathcal{Z}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{H}{\mathcal{M}})
can be identified with the category of left
Yetter-Drinfeld
modules over
H
, which are defined in a completely analogous way to classical Hopf algebras. First recall that over a bialgebroid
H
, a left
H
-
comodule structure
means a left
A
-module
B
morphism
\delta:{}_{A}B\rightarrow|H\otimes_{A}B
which atisfies
\Delta(b_{(-1)})\otimes_{A}b_{(0)}=b_{(-1)}\otimes_{A}\delta(b_{(0)})
and
\epsilon_{H}(b_{(-1)}).b_{(0)}=b
, where we denote
\delta(b)=b_{(-1)}\otimes_{A}b_{(0)}
. The left
A
-module
B
obtains a natural
A
-bimodule structure with its right
A
-action defined by
ma=\epsilon_{H}(h_{(-1)}a).h_{(0)}
so that
\delta
factors through

H\times_{A}B=\{\sum_{i}h_{i}\otimes_{A}b_{i}\in|H\otimes_{A}B\mid\sum_{i}h_{i}\overline{a}\otimes_{A}b_{i}=\sum_{i}h_{i}\otimes_{A}b_{i}a\ \text{for}\ \forall a\in A\}

A Yetter-Drinfeld module over
H
is an
A
-bimodule
B
with a left
H
-action
\triangleright
and a compatible left
H
-coaction
\delta
satisfying
h_{(1)}.b_{(-1)}\otimes_{A}h_{(2)}\triangleright b_{(0)}=(h_{(1)}\triangleright b)_{(-1)}.h_{(2)}\otimes_{A}(h_{(1)}\triangleright b)_{(0)}
. The natural braiding obtained for such a Yetter-Drinfeld module is defined by
b\otimes_{A}m\mapsto b_{(-1)}\triangleright^{\prime}m\otimes_{A}b_{(0)}
where
m\in M
, for arbitrary
(M,\triangleright^{\prime})
in
{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}
. We refer the reader to Section 4 of
[
56
]
for more details.

Assume
(B,\triangleright,\delta)
is equipped with a braided Hopf algebra structure in
\mathcal{Z}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{H}{\mathcal{M}})
via
(\Delta_{B},\epsilon_{B},S_{B})
. Note that these maps are all
A
-bimodule maps and respect the relevant
H
-actions and
H
-coactions. Additionally, the codomain of
\epsilon_{B}
is the trivial bimodule
A
which acts as the unit of
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{l,lax}({}_{H}{\mathcal{M}})
. By Theorem
5.2
, we obtain a Hopf algebroid structure on
B\otimes_{A}{}_{\mathsf{s}}H
where
{}_{\mathsf{s}}H
denotes the left
A
-module structure of
|H
and the
A^{e}
-bimodule structure on
B\otimes_{A}{}_{\mathsf{s}}H
is defined by
a_{1}\overline{a_{2}}(b\otimes_{A}h)a_{3}\overline{a_{4}}=a_{1}b\otimes_{A}\overline{a_{2}}ha_{3}\overline{a_{4}}
. Furthermore, the element
1_{B}\otimes_{A}1_{H}
acts as the unit and the other structural morphisms can be obtained as follows:

\displaystyle(b\otimes_{A}h).(b^{\prime}\otimes_{A}h^{\prime})=

\displaystyle b.(h_{(1)}\triangleright b^{\prime})\otimes_{A}h_{(2)}.h^{\prime}

\displaystyle(b\otimes_{A}h)_{(1)}\otimes_{A}(b\otimes_{A}h)_{(2)}=

\displaystyle(b_{(1)}\otimes_{A}b_{(2)(-1)}.h_{(1)})\otimes_{A}(b_{(2)(0)}\otimes_{A}h_{(2)})

\displaystyle\epsilon(b\otimes_{A}h)=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{B}(b).\epsilon_{H}(h)

Similarly, the inverse of the left canonical map is obtained by observing (
82
):

\displaystyle(b\otimes_{A}h)_{(+)}\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}(b\otimes_{A}h)_{(-)}=(b_{(1)}\otimes_{A}b_{(2)(-1)}.h_{(+)})\otimes_{A^{\mathrm{op}}}(S_{B}(b_{(2)(0)})\otimes_{A}h_{(-)})

With any result regarding Hopf algebroids, one needs to check that all the maps and compositions defined behave well with regard to the various
A
and
A^{\mathrm{op}}
actions. But in our case, since we are simply writing out the relevant morphisms after regarding the structures as Hopf monads, Theorem
5.2
guarantees that the morphisms will be well-defined with respect to the relevant actions. We will denote the obtained Hopf algebroid by
B\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H
.

5.2
Distributive Laws

In this section we review the notion of distributive laws and how one can compose two (Hopf) bimonads with a distributive law between them. This construction can be viewed as a generalisation of the tensor product of two braided Hopf algebras.

Distributive laws were introduced by Beck in
[
5
]
and determine when a monad (or an adjunction, as seen in Section
3.4
) can lift to the Eilenberg-Moore category of another monad. We recall the basic theory of distributive laws without providing proofs and refer the reader to
[
67
]
for detailed references and historical notes on the topic. Throughout this section
(T,\mu,\eta)
and
(S,\nu,\iota)
will denote a pair of monads on
\mathcal{C}
.

A
distributive law
or
entwining
from a monad
T
to a monad
S
is a natural transformation
\lambda:TS\rightarrow ST
such that the following diagrams commute

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\scriptstyle{T\iota}
\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{ST}
\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{ST}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{S}}
\scriptstyle{S\eta}

\textstyle{TTS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{S}}
\textstyle{TST\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{T}}
\textstyle{STT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\mu}
\textstyle{TSS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{S}}
\scriptstyle{T\nu}
\textstyle{STS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\lambda}
\textstyle{SST\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{T}}
\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{ST}
\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{ST}

Such a distributive law
\lambda:TS\rightarrow ST
from a monad
T
to a monad
S
, allows
S
to lift to
\mathcal{C}^{T}
as
(\hat{S},\hat{\nu},\hat{\iota})
, where
\hat{S}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
,
\hat{\nu}
and
\hat{\iota}
are defined as

\hat{S}(M,r)=(S(M),(Sr)\lambda_{M}:TS(M)\rightarrow ST(M)\rightarrow S(M)),\quad\hat{\nu}_{(M,r)}=\nu_{M},\quad\hat{\iota}_{(M,r)}=\iota_{M}

for any
T
-module
(M,r)
, such that
U_{T}\hat{S}=SU_{T}
. The objects of
\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{\hat{S}}
have an equivalent description as triples
(M,r,\rho)
where
M
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
,
r:T(M)\rightarrow M
is a
T
-action and
\rho:S(M)\rightarrow M
is a
S
-action satisfying

\textstyle{TS(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{M}}
\textstyle{ST(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Sr}
\textstyle{T(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{S(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}

Note that the theory is not symmetric and that we cannot necessarily lift
T
to
\mathcal{C}^{S}
via
\lambda
. For this we require another distributive law
\xi:ST\rightarrow TS
. In particular, if
\lambda
is invertible then we can lift
T
via
\xi=\lambda^{-1}
and in this case
\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{\hat{S}}\cong\left(\mathcal{C}^{S}\right)^{\hat{T}}
since for any triple
(M,r,\rho)
the conditions
\rho(Sr)\lambda_{M}=r(T\rho)
and
\rho(Sr)=r(T\rho)\xi_{M}
become equivalent by
\xi=\lambda^{-1}
.

Given a distributive law
\lambda:TS\rightarrow ST
, the endofunctor
ST
obtains a natural monad structure via multiplication
\nu(SS\mu)(S\lambda_{T})
and unit
\iota_{T}\eta
. We will follow the notation of
[
12
]
and denote this monad by
S\circ_{\lambda}T
. One can directly show that the monads
\hat{S}\rtimes T
and
S\circ_{\lambda}T
are isomorphic and that
\mathcal{C}^{\hat{S}\rtimes T}\cong\mathcal{C}^{S\circ_{\lambda}T}
. Furthermore, when
T
and
S
are bimonads the following observation can be made:

Lemma 5.4
.

If
T,S
and
\lambda
are as above and
T
and
S
carry bimonad structures such that
\lambda:TS\rightarrow ST
is a comonoidal natural transformation, then
S\circ_{\lambda}T
also becomes a bimonad.

Sketch of proof.

The endofunctor
ST
has a natural comonoidal structure with
(S_{2})_{T\otimes T}S(T_{2})
and
S_{0}S(T_{0})
. Now observe that for
S\circ_{\lambda}T
, conditions (
19
) and (
21
) automatically follow from
S
and
T
being bimonads. Conditions (
18
) and (
20
) then follow from
\lambda
being a comonoidal natural transformation or equivalently satisfying
(\lambda\otimes\lambda)(T_{2})_{S\otimes S}T(S_{2})=(S_{2})_{T\otimes T}ST_{2}\lambda_{-\otimes-}
and
T_{0}T(S_{0})=S_{0}S(T_{0})\lambda_{\mathtt{1}}
.

\diamond

Instead of verifying the bimonad conditions on
S\circ_{\lambda}T
, one can simply observe that
\lambda
being a comonoidal natural transformation implies that
(S_{2},S_{0})
lift to a well-defined comonoidal structure on
\hat{S}
. Consequently,
\hat{S}
becomes a bimonad and by the results of Section
5.1
, so does
\hat{S}\rtimes T=S\circ_{\lambda}T
.

Theorem 5.5

(Corollary 4.7
[
12
]
)
.

If
T
and
S
are Hopf monads on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
and
\lambda:TS\rightarrow ST
a comonoidal distributive law from
T
to
S
, then the lifted monad
\hat{S}
, on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
, has a Hopf monad structure and the composition
S\circ_{\lambda}T
defines a Hopf monad on
\mathcal{C}
.

Sketch of proof.

By the observations made above its easy to see that the image of the left (right) fusion operator of
\hat{S}
under
U_{T}
becomes the left (right) fusion operator of
S
. Since
U_{T}
is conservative, then
\hat{S}
is left (right) Hopf if and only if
S
is left (right) Hopf. Consequently, if
S
is left (right) Hopf then by Theorem
5.2
,
\hat{S}\rtimes T=S\circ_{\lambda}T
becomes left (right) Hopf.

\diamond

Composing Hopf monads via distributive laws can be viewed as a generalisation of tensoring Hopf algebras: If
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
is a braided category and
B
and
C
are two braided Hopf algebras in
\mathcal{C}
, then it is well-known that
C\otimes B
obtains a natural braided Hopf algebra structure by:

\displaystyle m_{C\otimes B}:=(m_{C}\otimes m_{B}

\displaystyle)(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\Psi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B}),\quad\Delta_{C\otimes B}:=(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\Psi_{C,B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\Delta_{C}\otimes\Delta_{B})

\displaystyle\eta_{C\otimes B}

\displaystyle:=\eta_{C}\otimes\eta_{B},\quad\epsilon_{C\otimes B}:=\epsilon_{C}\otimes\epsilon_{B},\quad S_{C\otimes B}:=S_{C}\otimes S_{B}

Viewing
B
and
C
as left Hopf monads via
T=B\otimes-
and
S=C\otimes-
, we see that
\lambda=\Psi_{B,C}\otimes-:TS\rightarrow ST
defines a distributive law between the two monads. Moreover,
\lambda
is comonoidal. All the necessary conditions simply follow because the structural morphisms of
B
and
C
respect the braiding of the category. It is then easy to see that the Hopf monad structure on
S\circ_{\lambda}T=C\otimes B\otimes-
agrees with the described Hopf algebra structure on
C\otimes B
.

6
Central Coalgebras and Hopf Monads

In this section we review the correspondence between Hopf monads and central cocommutative coalgebras, as presented in Section 6 of
[
12
]
. In summary, every (pre-)Hopf monad
T
on a category
\mathcal{C}
induces a central cocommutative coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. In the converse direction, for any central cocommutative coalgebra
(C,\tau)
in a category
\mathcal{D}
, under suitable exactness conditions,
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
becomes monoidal and the adjunction
U_{C\otimes-}\dashv F_{C\otimes-}
a comonoidal adjunction, inducing a Hopf monad on
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
. Moreover, under additional assumptions discussed in Theorem
6.6
these procedures are inverses to each other upto isomorphism.

6.1
From Central Coalgebras to Hopf Adjunctions

In this section we will recall how suitable central coalgebras
C
in a category
\mathcal{D}
induce a Hopf monads on their category of comodules
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
.

Assume
(C,\Delta,\epsilon)
is a coalgebra in a monoidal category
\mathcal{D}
. The corresponding free/forgetful functors for the comonad
C\otimes-
, provide an adjunction
V\dashv R:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\prescript{C}{}{\mathcal{D}}
, where the forgetful functor
V
is left adjoint to the free functor
R
defined by
R(M)=(C\otimes M,\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})
. Hence, we obtain a monad
T=RV
on
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
.

We say
(C,\tau)
is a
(lax) central coalgebra
if
\tau:C\otimes-\rightarrow-\otimes C
is a (lax) braiding such that
(C,\tau)
is a coalgebra in the (lax) center of
\mathcal{D}
and thereby satisfies
(\tau\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\tau)(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}})=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\Delta)\tau
and
(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\epsilon)\tau=\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
. Furthermore, we say
C
is a
cocommutative (lax) central coalgebra
or (lax)
CCC
if
\tau_{C}\Delta=\Delta
.

In
[
12
]
, the authors discuss the conditions under which a CCC structure on
C
provides a monoidal structure on the category of
C
-comodules. A lax CCC is called
cotensorable
if for each pair of comodules
(M,\delta)
and
(N,\delta^{\prime})
, the pair

\textstyle{M\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{M}\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}
\textstyle{M\otimes C\otimes N}

(83)

admits an equalizer and
C\otimes-
preserves them. We denote the equalizer of this pair by
M\otimes_{C}N
. Note that such pairs are coreflexive via
\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}
and thereby if
\mathcal{D}
admits CEs and
\otimes
preserves them, then any lax CCC is cotensorable. In particular, this theory generalises the theory of
cotensors
for ordinary coalgebras in
\mathsf{Vec}
where
\tau
is given by the symmetry of the category, see Section 10 of
[
19
]
.

Theorem 6.1

(Theorem 6.4
[
12
]
)
.

If
C
denotes a lax cotensorable CCC as above:

(I)

The equalizers
M\otimes_{C}N
have a natural
C
-coaction and the bifunctor
\otimes_{C}
defines a monoidal structure on
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
.

(II)

The free functor
R:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
is strong monoidal and
V\dashv R
is a comonoidal adjunction.

(III)

The adjunction
V\dashv R
is in fact left Hopf. If
\tau
is invertible, then the adjunction is also right Hopf.

Sketch of proof.

For part (I), we observe that for a pair
(M,\delta)
and
(N,\delta^{\prime})
, the object
M\otimes_{C}N
carries a natural
C
-comodule structure
t
, where
t
is defined as the unique map satisfying

\textstyle{M\otimes_{C}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{M\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes N}}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes N}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes_{C}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\pi}

where
\pi:M\otimes_{C}N\rightarrow M\otimes N
is the equalizer of the pair (
83
). Note that
t
is determined uniquely because
\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\pi
is an equalizer for the coreflexive pair
\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\tau_{M}\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}
,
\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes M}\otimes\delta^{\prime}
. It is then straightforward to show that
t
defines a coaction.

For (II), first recall that the free functor
R
takes an object
M
in
\mathcal{D}
to the free comodule
(C\otimes M,\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})
. Additionally, observe that the coproduct
\Delta
forms a split equalizer for the pair
\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C}
and
\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\Delta
, with sections
\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\epsilon
and
\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes C}\otimes\epsilon
. Hence, if we denote the equalizer of (
83
) for comodules
R(M)
and
R(N)
by
\pi^{\prime}
, we obtain a natural isomorphism between two equalizers and a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{(C\otimes M)\otimes_{C}(C\otimes N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes C\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes M}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{C\otimes M}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes N}}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes C\otimes C\otimes N}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 28.45274pt(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\tau_{M})(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}

Notice that
(C\otimes M)\otimes_{C}(C\otimes N)=R(M)\otimes_{C}R(N)
and
C\otimes M\otimes N=R(M\otimes N)
and thereby we obtain a natural strong monoidal structure on
R
. Of course one must also check whether the isomorphism respects the coactions, but this also follows easily.

Now we demonstrate that
V\dashv R
is left Hopf. Recall from Lemma
3.4
that a strong monoidal structure on
R
provides a comonoidal structure on its left adjoint
V
. In the diagram below, the upper edges form the comonoidal structure of
V
as described by (
25
):

\textstyle{M\otimes_{C}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta\otimes_{C}\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{(C\otimes M)\otimes_{C}(C\otimes N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes N}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{C\otimes M}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}
\textstyle{M\otimes N}
\textstyle{M\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta\otimes\delta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\otimes M\otimes C\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}

Since the diagram commutes, we can alternatively identify the comonoidal structure with the composition of the lower edges of the diagram. In particular,
V_{2}((M,\delta),(N,\delta^{\prime}))=\pi
where
V(M\otimes_{C}N,t)=M\otimes_{C}N
and
V(M,\delta)\otimes V(N,\delta^{\prime})=M\otimes N
. Therefore, the left fusion operator of the adjunction
V\dashv R
decomposes as

\overline{H}^{l}_{(M,\delta),d}=(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d})\pi:V((M,\delta)\otimes_{C}R(d))\rightarrow V((M,\delta))\otimes d

for an object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
and a
C
-comodule
(M,\delta)
. By a similar argument to the one above we can show that
\tau_{M}\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d}:V((M,\delta))\otimes d\rightarrow M\otimes C\otimes d
becomes a split equalizer for the same parallel pair which
\pi:M\otimes_{C}R(d)\rightarrow M\otimes C\otimes d
is an equalizer of. Thereby there exists a natural isomorphism such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{M\otimes_{C}R(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{M\otimes C\otimes d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d})}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{M}\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes d}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d}}
\textstyle{M\otimes C\otimes C\otimes d}
\textstyle{M\otimes d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{M}\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d}}

Hence, the left fusion operator is invertible. When
\tau
is invertible a symmetric argument can be applied to show that the right fusion operator is invertible.
\diamond

The above result can provide us with many additional examples of Hopf monads. However, unlike Section
4
where we were looking at describing Hopf monads over a fixed base category
\mathcal{C}
, a suitable CCC in a category
\mathcal{D}
provides us with a Hopf monad on
{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
rather than the category
\mathcal{D}
which we started with.

Example 6.2
.

For any cocommutative coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\epsilon)
in
\mathsf{Vec}
, the category of left
C
-comodules
{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}
is monoidal via the cotensor product of comodules (Section 10 of
[
19
]
) and by Theorem
6.1
the free/forgetful adjunction
\mathrm{forg.}\dashv(C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-,\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-):\mathsf{Vec}\leftrightarrows{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}
is Hopf. In particular the monad
T
on
{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}
which sends every
C
-comodule
(M,\delta)
to the free comodule
(C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{M})
is Hopf. The multiplication and unit of the monad
T
are given by
(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\epsilon\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{{}^{C}{\mathcal{M}}})
and
\delta:(M,\delta)\rightarrow(C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M,\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{M})
, respectively. The comonoidal structure on
T
is defined using
T((M,\delta))\otimes_{C}T((N,\delta^{\prime}))\cong(C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N,\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N})
:

\textstyle{T_{2}((M,\delta),(N,\delta^{\prime})):C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(M\otimes_{C}N)\rightarrow C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N}
\textstyle{\hskip 99.58464ptc\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m\otimes_{C}n\longmapsto c\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}m\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}n}

and
T_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\epsilon:C\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}C\rightarrow C
.

6.2
Induced Central Coalgebra of a Hopf Adjunction

In this section, we review the construction of central coalgebras from Hopf adjunctions based on Section 6.2 of
[
12
]
. It is well-known that any braided Hopf algebra
B
in
\mathcal{C}
becomes an object of
{}^{B}_{B}\mathcal{YD}
via its
left adjoint coaction

(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Psi_{B,B})(\Delta\otimes S)\Delta
. In particular, this coaction respects the original coalgebra structure on
B
and thereby
(B,\Delta,\epsilon)
becomes a natural coalgebra object in
Z({}_{B}\mathcal{C})
. Below we will describe how any Hopf monad
T
induces a coalgebra object in
Z(\mathcal{C}^{T})
.

Given a comonoidal adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
, the object
\hat{C}:=F(\mathtt{1})
obtains a coalgebra structure via
F_{2}(\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1})
and
F_{0}
, and is referred to as the
induced coalgebra
of the adjunction. Hence, we obtain three distinct comonads
\hat{T}=FU
,
\hat{C}\otimes-
, and
-\otimes\hat{C}
on
\mathcal{D}
. Observe that the fusion operators of the adjunctions provide natural transformations between two of the three comonads as presented:

\textstyle{\overline{H}^{l}_{\mathtt{1},X}:}
\textstyle{\hat{T}(X)=F(\mathtt{1}\otimes U(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}_{F(\mathtt{1})}\otimes\epsilon_{X})F_{2}(\mathtt{1},U(X))}
\textstyle{\hat{C}\otimes X=F(\mathtt{1})\otimes X}

\textstyle{\overline{H}^{r}_{X,\mathtt{1}}:}
\textstyle{\hat{T}(X)=F(U(X)\otimes\mathtt{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{F(\mathtt{1})})F_{2}(U(X),\mathtt{1})}
\textstyle{X\otimes\hat{C}=X\otimes F(\mathtt{1})}

In Lemma 6.5 of
[
12
]
it is demonstrated that
\overline{H}^{l}_{\mathtt{1},-}
and
\overline{H}^{r}_{-,\mathtt{1}}
are comonad morphisms.

Theorem 6.3

(Corollary 6.7
[
12
]
)
.

With notation as above, if
F\dashv U
is a left pre-Hopf adjunction, then

\tau_{X}:=\overline{H}^{r}_{X,\mathtt{1}}\left(\overline{H}^{l}_{\mathtt{1},X}\right)^{-1}:\hat{C}\otimes X\rightarrow X\otimes\hat{C}

(84)

defines a lax braiding in
\mathcal{D}
satisfying
F_{2}(X,\mathtt{1})=\tau_{F(X)}F_{2}(\mathtt{1},X)
. In particular,
(C,\tau)
becomes a lax CCC in
\mathcal{D}
. Additionally, If the adjunction is pre-Hopf, then
\tau
is invertible.

The proof of the above result is simply checking that the necessary conditions hold and we refer the reader to Section 6.2 of
[
12
]
. Note that
\overline{H}^{l}_{\mathtt{1},X}
being invertible tells us that
\hat{T}=FU\cong\hat{C}\otimes-
are isomorphic comonads. The above result also shows that given a pre-Hopf adjunction
\hat{C}\otimes-\cong-\otimes\hat{C}
as comonads. Additionally, note that for the free/forgetful adjunction induced by a braided Hopf algebra
B
it follows from the description of the fusion operators and their inverses in Section
3.3
that we can recover the natural braiding on
B
, induced by its adjoint coaction, as
\tau
in Theorem
6.3
.

In
[
12
]
, the lax CCC
\hat{C}
of a pre-Hopf adjunction
F\dashv U
is called its
induced
CCC. As seen in the last section, CCCs can induce comonoidal adjunctions and the next step is to understand the connection between this adjunction induced by
\hat{C}
and the original adjunction
F\dashv U
. We will first recall the conditions which make the induced CCC cotensorable and the comparison functor between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\hat{T}}\cong\mathcal{D}^{\hat{C}}
strong monoidal.

Theorem 6.4

(Proposition 6.9
[
12
]
)
.

Let
F\dashv U
be a left pre-Hopf adjunction as above. If the adjunction is comonadic and satisfies

(I)

For any pair of objects
X,Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, the fork below forms an equalizer:

\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{F(X)\otimes F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}\otimes F_{2}(\mathtt{1},Y)}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}(X,\mathtt{1})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{F(Y)}}
\textstyle{F(X)\otimes\hat{C}\otimes F(Y)}

(85)

(II)

The above equalizers are preserved by
F(\mathtt{1})\otimes-

Then the induced coalgebra
(\hat{C},\tau)
is cotensorable and the comparison functor between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\hat{C}}
is strong monoidal. In particular, the adjunction is left Hopf.

Sketch of proof.

Note that the comparison functor
K:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{}^{C}\mathcal{D}
is defined by sending any object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
to
(F(X),F_{2}(\mathtt{1},X))
. Since
K
is an equivalence and
F_{2}(X,\mathtt{1})=\tau_{F(X)}F_{2}(\mathtt{1},X)
, then the conditions for
\hat{C}
being cotensorable become equivalent to the conditions above. Additionally,
K
becomes a strong monoidal equivalence by (
85
). Finally, we observe that by Theorem
6.1
the free/forgetful adjunction for the comonad
\hat{C}\otimes-
is left Hopf, and since
U\dashv F
is comonadic then it is also left Hopf.
\diamond

While the induced coalgebra in the theory of Hopf algebras is quite well-known, we will now look at the induced coalgebra for another examples of Hopf monads:

Example 6.5
.

Let
H
be a bialgebroid over a base algebra
A
as in Section
4.6
. Recall that the free/forgetful adjunction induced on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is a comonoidal adjunction with a bimonad structure on the functor
H\boxtimes-
. The induced CCC for this adjunction will be a natural coalgebra structure on
H\boxtimes A
which is the quotient of
H
by the left ideal generated by
\{a-\overline{a}\mid a\in A\}
. The vector space
H\boxtimes A
has an
A
-bimodule structure by the left
A^{e}
-action on
H
. It follows by the above theory that
(\Delta,\epsilon)
descend to a well-defined coalgebra structure on
H\boxtimes A
. This can also be seen directly from (
69
) and (
70
) and the fact that the image of
\Delta
falls in the Takeuchi product. If
H
is left Hopf, then by Theorem
6.3
the coalgebra
H\boxtimes A
obtains a lax braiding defined by

\textstyle{(H\boxtimes A)\otimes_{A}M\rightarrow M\otimes_{A}(H\boxtimes A)}
\textstyle{(h\boxtimes a)\otimes_{A}M\longmapsto h_{(+)(1)}ah_{(-)}\triangleright m\otimes_{A}(h_{(+)}\boxtimes 1)}

(86)

where
(M,\triangleright)
is an arbitrary
H
-module. It is a difficult task to check that this map is indeed well-defined and a bimodule morphism, however we get this result for free from Theorem
6.4
.

6.3
Equivalence of Hopf Monads and Central Coalgebras

In this section, we review the equivalence between Hopf monads on a category
\mathcal{C}
, Hopf adjunctions
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
and lax cotensorable CCCs in
\mathcal{D}
from Section 6.6 of
[
12
]
. We then provide a simplification of this result for tensor categories.

Theorem 6.6

(Theorem 6.14
[
12
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are monoidal categories, then TFAE:

(I)

A conservative Hopf monad
T
on
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}
admits RCs and CEs, the monoidal product
\otimes
preserves CEs and
T
preserves RCs and CEs.

(II)

A Hopf adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}
, where both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
admit RCs and CEs, both
F
and
U
are conservative,
U
preserves RCs and
F
preserves CEs.

(III)

A CCC
(C,\tau)
in
\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{D}
admits RCs and CEs, the monoidal product
\otimes
preserves CEs, the functor
C\otimes-
is conservative and preserves RCs.

Note that when saying these structures are equivalent, we mean upto isomorphism and the processes of sending an adjunction to a CCC and a CCC to its corresponding adjunction are not strict inverses. Additionally, the emphasis on the existence and preservation of RCs has to do with the comonadicity of the adjunction corresponding to a CCC, see Remark 6.1 in
[
12
]
. For more details and the proof of this equivalence we refer the reader to to Section 6.6 of
[
12
]
.

Recall that
tensor categories
, see
[
24
]
, are rigid and abelian and thereby admit equalizers and coequalizers. Moreover, in rigid monoidal categories the tensor product preserves finite limits and colimits in both entries [Proposition 4.2.1
[
24
]
]. Additionally,
tensor functors
, as considered in
[
24
]
, are defined as being faithful exact strong monoidal functors between two tensor categories. Such functors are automatically conservative since abelian categories are balanced and any functor with a balanced domain which is faithful automatically becomes conservative. Finally, recall by Corollary
3.9
, that any Hopf monad on a rigid monoidal category is colimit-preserving. Hence, we can re-write Theorem
6.6
for the setting of tensor categories:

Theorem 6.7
.

If
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
are two tensor categories over a field
\mathbb{K}
, then TFAE:

(I)

A conservative Hopf monad
T
on
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}
and
T
preserves CEs.

(II)

A tensor functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
with a conservative left adjoint
F
which preserves CEs.

(III)

A CCC
(C,\tau)
in
\mathcal{D}
.

A dual statement to the above theorem can be made with regards to Hopf comonads and central cocommutative algebras. This dual statement is in part presented Section 6.1 of
[
13
]
.

7
Classical Hopf-algebraic results for Hopf monads

In this section we review some classical results in the theory of Hopf algebras which have been generalised to the monadic setting.

7.1
Radford’s Biproduct Theorem

Radford’s biproduct Theorem which we alluded to in Section
5.1
states the following stronger result: Given a pair of Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
and a Hopf algebra map
\pi:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
which splits by
\iota:H_{1}\hookrightarrow H_{2}
, we can find a braided Hopf algebra
B
in
{}^{H_{1}}_{H_{1}}\mathcal{YD}
, such that
H_{2}\cong B\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H_{1}
. In Section 4.4 of
[
12
]
, the authors discuss a more general version of this result which we will review here under the name of
cross quotients
. The more general question is understanding when a Hopf monad can be obtained as a cross product from another Hopf monad.

First observe that for a pair of monads
(T,\mu,\eta)
and
(Q,\mu^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a natural correspondence between monad morphisms and functors between
\mathcal{C}^{T}
and
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
which commute with the forgetful functors: Any monad morphism
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
defines a functor
\phi^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
by
\phi^{*}(M,r)=(M,r\phi)
which satisfies
U_{T}\phi^{*}=U_{Q}
. Conversely, from any functor
F:\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
satisfying
U_{T}F=U_{Q}
, we can recover a monad morphism
F^{*}:T\rightarrow Q
defined by
F^{*}_{X}=\rho_{(Q(X),\mu^{\prime}_{X})}T\eta^{\prime}_{X}
where
\rho
is the natural transformation given by
F((M,r))=(M,\rho_{(M,r)})
. For further details on this correspondence we refer the reader to Lemma 1.7 of
[
14
]
.

In
[
12
]
a monad morphism
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
is called
cross quotientable
if the functor
\phi^{*}
is monadic. Such a functor, in turn, defines a monad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
which is called the cross quotient of
\phi
and denoted by
Q\div T
. In this setting, it is easy to check that
\phi^{*}
is monadic if and only if it has a left adjoint and if so
(\mathcal{C}^{T})^{Q\div T}
becomes isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
, Lemma 4.9 of
[
12
]
. Moreover, it is shown that the left adjoint
G
of
\phi^{*}
exists precisely if for any
T
-module
(M,r)
, the pair
\mu^{\prime}_{M}Q(\phi_{M}),Q(r):F_{Q}T(M)\rightrightarrows F_{Q}(M)
admits a coequalizer
G(M,r)
in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
.

Lemma 7.1

(Proposition 4.11 (a)
[
12
]
)
.

If
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
is a cross quotientable bimonad morphism, then
Q\div T
is a bimonad.

Proof.

This statement follows from the fact that
\phi^{*}
becomes strong monoidal when
\phi
is a bimonad morphism. In this case the trivial comonoidal structure
\phi^{*}_{2}\big((M,r),(N,t)\big)=\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes N}
is well-defined since
(r\otimes t)Q_{2}(M,N)\phi_{M\otimes N}=(r\otimes t)(\phi_{M}\otimes\phi_{N})T_{2}(M,N)
.
∎

At this point we should note that the operations ‘cross quotient’ and ‘cross product’ are inverses, Proposition 4.11
[
12
]
. Fixing a (bi)monad
T
on
\mathcal{C}
, we see that for every (bi)monad
Q
equipped with a cross quotientable (bi)monad morphism
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
, we have an isomorphism
Q\cong(Q\div T)\rtimes T
. In the converse direction, for any (bi)monad
P
on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
, the unit
\eta^{\prime\prime}
of
P
provides a cross quotientable (bi)monad morphism
U_{T}\eta^{\prime\prime}_{F_{T}}
from
T
to
Q=P\rtimes T
.

Theorem 7.2

(Proposition 4.13
[
12
]
)
.

Let
T
and
Q
be left (right) Hopf monads on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
. Assume
\mathcal{C}
has RCs,
T
and
Q
both preserve RCs and
\otimes
preserves RCs in the left (right) entry. In this case, any bimonad morphism
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
is cross quotientable and
Q\div T
is left (right) Hopf.

Sketch of proof.

We previously mentioned that
\phi
is cross quotientable if and only if reflexive pairs
\mu^{\prime}_{M}Q(\phi_{M}),Q(r):F_{Q}T(M)\rightrightarrows F_{Q}(M)
corresponding to
T
-modules
(M,r)
admit coequalizers in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
[Lemma 4.9 of
[
12
]
]. Hence, under the assumptions made above
Q\div T
exists and is a bimonad by Lemma
7.1
. Let
\overline{H}^{l}
denote the left fusion operator of the adjunction corresponding to
Q\div T
. Next we observe that Diagram (
82
) commuting also shows that if the left fusion operators of
T
and
(Q\div T)\rtimes T\cong Q
are invertible, then
\overline{H}^{l}_{F_{T},-}
is invertible (
P=Q\div T
).

The final step is to view any
T
-module
(M,r)
as the coequalizer of its corresponding reflexive pair
F_{T}(r),\mu_{M}:F_{T}T(M)\rightrightarrows T(M)
in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
with
F_{T}(\eta_{M})
and
r:T(M)\rightarrow M
. By Lemma 4.2 of
[
12
]
,
\otimes
also preserves RCs in the left component in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
and
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
. Since
F_{P}
is left adjoint and preserves coequalizers, then the functors
F_{P}(-\otimes U_{P}(X))
and
F_{P}(-)\otimes X
both preserve RCs for any
X\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
. Therefore if
\overline{H}^{l}_{F_{T}(M),X}
is invertible, then so is
\overline{H}^{l}_{(M,r),X}
. We refer the reader to Lemma 4.8 of
[
12
]
for additional details on this argument.

\diamond

Given a pair of ordinary Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, their corresponding left Hopf monads
Q=H_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
and
T=H_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
on
\mathsf{Vec}
satisfy the conditions in Theorem
7.2
. Hence we can consider the cross quotient of
\phi=\iota\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
for any Hopf algebra map
\iota:H_{1}\rightarrow H_{1}
. In particular, the resulting adjunction given by restriction and extension of scalars between
{}_{H_{2}}{\mathcal{M}}
and
{}_{H_{1}}{\mathcal{M}}
will be left Hopf. We already mentioned this fact in Section
4.4
. The corresponding Hopf monad
Q\div T
is given by the functor
{}_{H_{1}}{H_{2}}\otimes_{H_{1}}-
on
{}_{H_{1}}{\mathcal{M}}
, where we consider the natural
H_{1}
-bimodule structure which
\iota
induces on
H_{2}
.

Theorem 7.3

(Corollary 5.12
[
12
]
)
.

If
T
,
Q
and
\mathcal{C}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
7.2
and the bimonad morphism
\phi:T\rightarrow Q
admits a retraction
\iota:Q\rightarrow T
then there exists a central Hopf algebra
(B,\tau)
in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
such that
Q\div T\cong B\otimes_{\tau}-
.

Sketch of proof.

The correspondence between bimonad morphisms
T\rightarrow Q
and bimonads
Q\div T
is functorial (Remark 4.12 of
[
12
]
). Therefore, if view the retraction of
\phi
as a morphism between bimonad maps
T\rightarrow Q
and
T\rightarrow T
, it will be sent to an augmentation map
Q\div T\rightarrow T\div T=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}
under this correspondence. Hence,
Q\div T
is augmented and by Theorem
4.1
it corresponds to a central Hopf algebra in
\mathcal{C}^{T}
.

\diamond

The setting of Radford’s biproduct Theorem for a pair of Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
is precisely the reduction of the above result for monads
Q=H_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
and
T=H_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
on
\mathsf{Vec}
, where the Hopf algebra map
\iota:H_{1}\hookrightarrow H_{2}
admits a retraction
\pi:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
.

Example 7.4

(Radford’s biproduct Theorem for Hopf Algebroids)
.

Note that for an arbitrary
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
A
, the tensor product of
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
preserves coequalizers in both entries. Additionally, the corresponding monads for bialgebroids were those which preserved colimits (Theorem
4.19
). Hence, we can apply Theorem
7.3
to the setting of Hopf algebroids: If
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are a pair of Hopf algebroids over a base algebra
A
and there exist a pair of bialgebroid morphisms
\pi:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
and
\iota:H_{1}\hookrightarrow H_{2}
such that
\pi\iota=\mathrm{id}_{H_{1}}
, then there exists a Hopf algebra
(B,\triangleright,\delta)
in
\mathcal{Z}({}_{H_{1}}{\mathcal{M}})
such that
H_{2}\cong B\mathrel{\rtimes\hskip-4.26773pt\cdot}H_{1}
. As in Radford’s result,
B
can be identified as the subspace of elements of the form
h-\iota\pi(h)
, for
h\in H
.

7.2
Hopf Modules

An important result in the theory of ordinary Hopf algebras concerns Hopf modules and is often referred to as the Fundamental Theorem of Hopf Algebras. This result describes an equivalence between the category of vector spaces and the category of Hopf modules and has been generalised to the case of braided Hopf algebras and braided categories in
[
6
]
under the additional requirement that idempotents split in the base category. In this section we review the analogous statement for Hopf monads.

A Hopf module for a Hopf algebra
H
consists of an
H
-module with a compatible
H
-comodule structure. Therefore, the first important step for defining Hopf modules over Hopf monads is picking a notion of comodules over the monad. Hopf modules over Hopf monads were first defined in
[
14
]
, where ‘
T
-comodules’ were defined as comodules with respect to the induced coalgebra of the monad
(T(\mathtt{1}),T_{2}(\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}),T_{0})
. Using this definition, a version of the Hopf module theorem was proved in
[
14
]
for right Hopf monads on right rigid categories. This result was then extended to Hopf monads on monoidal categories in
[
12
]
. More recently, the result of
[
12
]
was interpreted as an example of Galois entwining structures in
[
50
]
. We will discuss this viewpoint at the end of this section.

Note that for any bimonad
T
on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, the image of the induced coalgebra
\hat{C}
of Section
6.2
under
U_{T}
becomes a coalgebra
(T(\mathtt{1}),T_{2}(\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}),T_{0})
in
\mathcal{C}
. With this notation, a triple
(M,r,\delta)
is called a
(left) Hopf module
, if
r:T(M)\rightarrow M
is a
T
-action and
\delta:M\rightarrow T(\mathtt{1})\otimes M
is a
T(\mathtt{1})
-coaction satisfying

\textstyle{T(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\delta}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{T(\mathtt{1})\otimes M}
\textstyle{T(T(\mathtt{1})\otimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},X}}
\textstyle{T(\mathtt{1})\otimes T(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{T(\mathtt{1})}\otimes r}

Morphisms of Hopf modules are defined accordingly as morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
which commute with the relevant actions and coactions. We will denote the category of (left) Hopf modules by
\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)
. Note that for the corresponding Hopf monad of a braided Hopf algebra
B
, we recover the usual notion of Hopf modules which are triples
(M,\triangleright,\delta)
where
\triangleright
is a
B
-action and
\delta
a
B
-coaction satisfying
\delta\triangleright=(m\otimes\triangleright)(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\Psi_{B,B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})(\Delta\otimes\delta)
.

Let us briefly recall the notion of a
mixed distributive law
or
entwining
from monad
(T,\mu,\eta)
to a comonad
(G,\Delta,\epsilon)
from
[
67
]
(the axioms first appeared for entwinings between ordinary algebras and coalgebras in
[
18
]
). Such an entwining is a natural transformation
\lambda:TG\rightarrow GT
satisfying analogous conditions to the distributive laws in Section
5.2
. Given such a
\lambda
, we can define a comonad
\hat{G}
on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
such that
U_{T}\hat{G}=GU_{T}
. Explicitly, the functor
\hat{G}
is defined by
\hat{G}(M,r)=(G(M),(Gr)\lambda_{M}:TG(M)\rightarrow GT(M)\rightarrow G(M))
for any
T
-module
(M,r)
. The compatibility conditions on
\lambda
then ensure that
(\hat{G},\Delta,\epsilon)
defines a comonad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. In a symmetric fashion,
\lambda
lifts
T
to
\mathcal{C}_{G}
, defining a monad
\hat{T}(M,\delta)=(T(M),\lambda_{M}T\delta:T(M)\rightarrow TG(M)\rightarrow GT(M))
, where
(M,\delta)
is a
G
-comodule in
\mathcal{C}_{G}
. Finally, we obtain a natural isomorphism
\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}\cong\left(\mathcal{C}_{G}\right)^{\hat{T}}
.

In the definition of left Hopf modules, the right fusion operator
H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},X}
is acting as a distributive law. Hence,
\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)=\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}
where
\hat{G}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T}
is the comonad obtained by lifting
T(\mathtt{1})\otimes-
via
H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},X}
. We have already encountered
\hat{G}
in Section
6.2
with the notation
\hat{C}\otimes-
and know it to be isomorphic to the comonad
\hat{T}=FU
when
H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},X}
is invertible.

The Fundamental Theorem of braided Hopf algebras describes an equivalence between the base category and the category of Hopf modules. This equivalence is given by the free Hopf module functor and the functor sending Hopf modules to their coinvariant parts. For ordinary Hopf algebras the coinvariant part of an
H
-comodule is the subspace of elements which satisfy
\delta(h)=1\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h
. Now we recall the analogous notions for Hopf monads from
[
12
]
.

For any left Hopf module
(M,r,\delta)
over a bimonad
T
the equalizer, if it exists, of the coreflexive pair
\delta,\eta_{\mathtt{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}:M\rightarrow T(\mathtt{1})\otimes M
is called its
coinvariant part
and we denote it by
M^{T(\mathtt{1})}
. Furthermore,
T
is said to preserve coinvariant parts of (left) Hopf modules if
T
preserves this class of equalizers. There also exists a natural functor
H_{T}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)
defined by
H_{T}(X)=(T(X),\mu_{X},T_{2}(\mathtt{1},X))
which sends an object to the
free Hopf module
generated by it. As mentioned
\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)=\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}
and the free functor
H_{T}
satisfies
U^{\hat{G}}H_{T}=F_{T}
, where
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}:\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}^{T}
and
U^{\hat{G}}\dashv F^{\hat{G}}:\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}^{T}
denote the free/forgetful adjunctions for the monad
T
and the comonad
\hat{G}
, respectively.

For a general monad
T
, the question of when
F^{\hat{T}}F_{T}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{T}}
induces an equivalence of categories is answered in
[
25
]
. Theorem 6.11 of
[
12
]
combines this with the observation that
\hat{G}\cong\hat{T}
for a left pre-Hopf monad to obtain an analogous statement to the Fundamental Theorem:

Theorem 7.5

(Theorem 6.11
[
12
]
)
.

Assuming
T
is a left pre-Hopf monad, TFAE:

(I)

The functor
H_{T}
defines an equivalence of categories.

(II)

The functor
T
is conservative, left Hopf modules admit coinvariant parts and
T
preserves them.

Sketch of proof.

As in the classical setting, if Hopf modules admit coinvariants, the functor
E:\mathcal{H}^{l}(T)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
which is defined on objects by
E(M,r,\delta)=M^{T(\mathtt{1})}
and extended naturally to morphisms, becomes right adjoint to
H_{T}
. Furthermore, as demonstrated in
[
25
]
, the counit of the adjunction is an isomorphism if and only if
T
preserves coinvariants. When the counit of the adjunction is an isomorphism then the right adjoint
E
is full and faithful and it is part of an equivalence if and only if it is essentially surjective. It is an easy exercise to check that the latter condition is true if and only if the left adjoint
H_{T}
is conservative. Since
T=U_{T}F_{T}=U_{T}U^{\hat{G}}H_{T}
, where
U_{T}
,
U^{\hat{G}}
are both conservative functors, then
H_{T}
is conservative if and only if
T
is.
\diamond

First note that the corresponding Hopf monad of an ordinary Hopf algebra
H
on
\mathsf{Vec}
naturally satisfies the conditions of Theorem
7.5
and we recover the classical Fundamental Theorem of Hopf algebras in this case. Secondly, note that a symmetric theory of right Hopf modules can also be defined over a Hopf monad, with
H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},-}
acting as a distributive law between
T
and
-\otimes T(\mathtt{1})
. In the theory of ordinary Hopf algebras and braided Hopf algebras, the notions of left and right Hopf modules become equivalent via the braiding. However, this is not the case for Hopf monads. In Example 5.6 of
[
50
]
, a right pre-Hopf monad is provided which does not satisfy the left Hopf module Theorem.

Example 7.6

(Fundamental Theorem of Hopf algebras for Hopf algebroids)
.

Let
H
be a bialgebroid over an algebra
A
and let
(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\epsilon})
denote the coalgebra structure on
\underline{H}:=H\boxtimes A
, which we described in Example
6.5
. A left Hopf module for the bimonad
H\boxtimes-
on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
will consists of an
H
-module
(M,\triangleright)
with an
\underline{H}
-coaction
\delta:M\rightarrow\underline{H}\otimes_{A}M
satisfying

(h\triangleright m)_{(-1)}\otimes_{A}(h\triangleright m)_{(0)}=\pi({h_{(1)}.m_{(-1)}})\otimes_{A}(h_{(2)}\triangleright m_{(0)})\in\underline{H}\otimes_{A}M

where we denote the natural projection
H\rightarrow H\boxtimes A
by
\pi
and
\delta(m)=m_{(-1)}\otimes_{A}m_{(0)}
for
m\in M
. If
H
is a Hopf algebroid and
H
is faithfully flat as a right
A^{e}
-module then
H\boxtimes-
becomes conservative and preserves coinvariant parts and by Theorem
7.5
we obtain an equivalence between
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and the category of Hopf modules defined above. In particular, the equivalence is given by the functor which sends any
A
-bimodule
M
to the corresponding Hopf module
H\boxtimes M
with the relevant free
H
-action and
\underline{H}
-coaction
h\boxtimes m\rightarrow\pi(h_{(1)})\otimes_{A}(h_{(2)}\boxtimes m)
. Note that this result differs from the other version of the Fundamental Theorem for Hopf algebroids which appears in Section 4.3.4 of
[
7
]
. The latter result describes an equivalence of categories between another notion of Hopf modules and
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}
.

The above description and proof of the Hopf module Theorem can be generalised to the category mixed modules
\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}
obtained from any distributive law
\lambda:TG\rightarrow GT
between an arbitrary monad
T
and comonad
G
on a category
\mathcal{C}
. This theory is fully described in
[
48
]
and elaborated on in
[
49
,
50
]
. In this framework, a natural transformation
g:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow G
is called a
group-like morphism
if
g
is a comonad morphism i.e.
\Delta g=g_{G}g
and
\epsilon g=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
. If such a morphism exists, one can construct a functor
H^{g}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}
defined by
H^{g}(M)=(T(M),\mu_{M},\lambda Tg_{M})
satisfying
U^{\hat{G}}K=F_{T}
. Moreover, the functor
T
obtains two left
G
-comodule structure
\lambda(Tg)
and
g_{T}
, where we view
G
as a comonoid in the monoidal category
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
. In the case of bimonads discussed above,
\lambda=H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},-}
,
G=T(\mathtt{1})\otimes-
and
g=\eta_{\mathtt{1}}\otimes-
.

If the pairs
\lambda_{M}(Tg_{M})
and
g_{T(M)}
admit equalizers denoted by
i^{g}:T^{g}(M)\rightarrow T(M)
, then
T^{g}
becomes functorial and
i^{g}
a monad morphism. Moreover,
H^{g}
becomes monadic and
T^{g}
the monad generated by
H^{g}
. In the bimonad case,
T^{g}
would exist if all free
T
-modules admit coinvariants. The monad morphism
i^{g}
naturally induces a functor between
(i^{g})^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{T^{g}}
, which under suitable conditions [Section 3.7
[
48
]
] admits a left adjoint and induces a comonad on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. Whenever this comonad is isomorphic to
\hat{G}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{T^{g}}
is equivalent to
\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)_{\hat{G}}
, then the quadruple
(T,G,\lambda,g)
are called a
Galois entwining
. In Proposition 4.6 of
[
50
]
, it is shown for a bimonad
T
the quadruple
(T,T(\mathtt{1})\otimes-,H^{r}_{\mathtt{1},-},\eta_{\mathtt{1}}\otimes-)
becomes a Galois entwining if and only if
H^{l}_{\mathtt{1},-}
is invertible. For further details on Galois entwining and their connection to bimonads we refer the reader to
[
48
]
and Section 4 of
[
50
]
.

7.3
Quasitriangular Structures

Quasitriangular structures on ordinary Hopf algebras were originally introduced by Drinfeld in
[
22
,
23
]
and correspond to braidings on the category of modules of the Hopf algebra. For braided Hopf algebras, a diagrammatic generalisation was presented in
[
46
]
. In this section, we review the corresponding notion for bimonads, which was introduced in
[
14
]
.

An R-matrix or quasitriangular structure on an ordinary bialgebra
H
is an invertible element of
R\in H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
(where we
H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
has an algebra structure
\bullet
using the tensor product of algebras) satisfying

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathsf{Vec}}_{H,H}\Delta(h)\bullet R=R\bullet\Delta(h)

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathrm{id}_{H})R=R_{13}\bullet R_{23}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Delta)R=R_{13}\bullet R_{12}

where
R_{ij}:H^{\otimes 3}\rightarrow H^{\otimes 3}
are the appearances of
R
to the
i
and
j
-th components of
H^{\otimes 3}
i.e.
R_{23}=1_{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}R
. To generalise this theory to braided bialgebras,
B
, Majid
[
43
]
interpreted the R-matrix as a morphism
R:\mathbb{K}\rightarrow H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
satisfying analogous conditions (Fig. 9.13 of
[
46
]
).

A
quasitringular
structure or
R-matrix
on a bimonad
T
is a natural transformation
R_{X\otimes Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow T(Y)\otimes T(X)
satisfying

\displaystyle(\mu_{Y}\otimes\mu_{X})R_{T(X),T(Y)}T_{2}(X,Y)=(\mu_{Y}\otimes\mu_{X})T_{2}(T(Y),T(X))T(R_{X,Y})

(87)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{T(Z)}\otimes

\displaystyle T_{2}(X,Y))R_{X\otimes Y,Z}=(\mu_{Z}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(X)\otimes T(Y)})(R_{X,T(Z)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(Y)})(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes R_{Y,Z})

(88)

\displaystyle(T_{2}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle\otimes\mathrm{id}_{T(X)})R_{X,Y\otimes Z}=(\mathrm{id}_{T(Y)\otimes T(Z)}\otimes\mu_{X})(\mathrm{id}_{T(Y)}\otimes R_{T(X),Z})(R_{X,Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Z})

(89)

for any triple of objects
X,Y,Z
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 7.7

(Theorem 8.5
[
14
]
)
.

If
T
is a bimonad on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a bijection between R-matrices on
T
and lax braidings on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
.

Sketch of proof.

This bijection is constructed as follows: If
R
is an R-matrix on
T
then
\tau_{(M,r),(N,s)}=(s\otimes r)R_{M,N}
, where
(M,r)
and
(N,s)
are
T
-modules, defines a braiding on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
. The first condition (
87
) ensures that
(s\otimes r)R_{M,N}
is a well-defined
T
-module map, while the other two conditions (
88
) and (
89
) dictate the braiding axioms. In the other direction, if
\tau
is a braiding on
\mathcal{C}^{T}
then
R_{M,N}=\tau_{(TX,\mu_{X}),(TY,\mu_{Y})}(\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y})
defines an R-matrix on
T
.

\diamond

Note that the R-matrix in the theory of ordinary bialgebras is expected to be an invertible element of
H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
. For bimonads, a similar notion of invertiblilty is discussed in
[
14
]
, where a natural transformation
R_{X\otimes Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow T(Y)\otimes T(X)
is called
convolution invertible
if there exists another natural transformation
R^{-1}_{X,Y}:Y\otimes X\rightarrow T(X)\otimes T(Y)
satisfying

\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}=(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y})R^{-1}_{T(X),T(Y)}R_{X,Y},\quad\eta_{Y}\otimes\eta_{X}=(\mu_{Y}\otimes\mu_{X})R_{T(X),T(Y)}R^{-1}_{X,Y}

(90)

Whenever
R^{-1}
in the above sense exists, then the lax braiding
\tau
defined in Theorem
7.7
obtains an inverse defined by by
\tau^{-1}_{(M,r),(N,s)}=(r\otimes s)R^{-1}_{N,M}:M\otimes N\rightarrow N\otimes M
.

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}
and
T=H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
is given by a bialgebra
H
, then the existence of any R-matrix
R_{V,W}:V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W\rightarrow H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}V
for the bimonad
T
induces a classical R-matrix on
H
defined by
R_{\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}}(1)\in H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H
. It is then easy to check that the natural family of morphisms
R_{V,W}
are solely determined by the choice of
R_{\mathtt{1},\mathtt{1}}(1)
. In the opposite direction, we can encode any classical R-matrix
R=\sum_{i}h_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h^{\prime}_{i}
as an R-matrix on the bimonad
H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
as follows
R_{X\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}Y}(x\otimes y)=\sum_{i}h^{\prime}_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}y\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}h_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}x
.

In the more general setting where
\mathcal{C}
is an arbitrary braided category and
T=\mathbb{B}
corresponds to a braided bialgebra
B
in
\mathcal{C}
, R-matrices on
T
do not necessarily correspond to a morphism
\overline{R}:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow B\otimes B
satisfying the conditions in Fig. 9.13 of
[
46
]
. In particular, the latter R-matrices only define a braiding on a particular subcategory of
B
-modules (called
commutative modules
). In Section 8.6 of
[
15
]
it is shown that when
\mathcal{C}
is rigid and admits a coend
C=\int^{c\in\mathcal{C}}c\otimes c^{\vee}
, then R-matrices on
\mathbb{B}
are in bijection with morphisms
C\otimes C\rightarrow B\otimes B
satisfying analogous conditions to Fig. 9.13 of
[
46
]
. The case where
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathrm{fd}}
is particular since
C=\mathbb{K}
and, therefore, the classical notion of R-matrices are recovered.

Another key aspect of the theory of R-matrices and Hopf algebras is their relation with the Yang-Baxter equation. An analogous version of the Yang-Baxter equation can be shown to hold for Hopf monads as well, Corollary 8.7 in
[
14
]
. Other concepts relating to braided categories and Hopf algebras such as
Drinfeld elements
for a Hopf monad and analogous notions of
ribbon
and
sovereign
structures on Hopf monads have all been defined in
[
14
]
. We should also note that the notion of quasitriangular structures on bialgebroids first appeared in
[
21
]
.

7.4
Tannaka Reconstruction for Hopf monads

In this section we review the construction of
[
60
]
which produces Hopf monads from suitable strong monoidal functors. This theory generalises our construction of Hopf monads from pivotal pairs in Section
4.5
and recovers the usual Tannaka-Krein reconstruction for braided Hopf algebras
[
41
]
when the base category is braided. Let us first recall what we mean by Tannaka-Krein theory for Hopf algebras.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small monoidal category and
\omega:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a strict monoidal functor such that
\omega
factors through the inclusion functor
\mathrm{inc.}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}
denotes the subcategory of rigid objects in
\mathcal{C}
. We consider the functor
\omega\otimes\omega^{\vee}:\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and denote its coend (see Section
2.6
), if it exists, by

H_{\omega}:=\int^{a\in\mathcal{D}}\omega(a)\otimes\omega(a)^{\vee}

(91)

Recall that the coend is the colimit of the diagram consisting of objects
\omega(a)\otimes\omega(b)^{\vee}
and morphisms
\omega(f)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(b)^{\vee}}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\omega(a)}\otimes\omega(f)^{\vee}
corresponding to objects
a,b\in\mathcal{D}
and morphisms
f:a\rightarrow b
in
\mathcal{D}
, respectively.

Theorem 7.8
.

If
(\mathcal{C},\Psi)
is a braided monoidal category, and the mentioned coend exists, it comes equipped with the structure of a braided bialgebra, such that
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{r,\mathrm{lax}}
is monoidal equivalent to the the category of left
H_{\omega}
-modules,
{}_{H_{\omega}}{\mathcal{C}}
. Additionally, if
\mathcal{D}
is rigid then
H_{\omega}
admits a bijective antipode making it a braided Hopf algebra object in
\mathcal{C}
.

Tannaka-Krein reconstruction over
\mathsf{Vec}
and symmetric monoidal categories was first formally treated in
[
55
]
and later for algebraic groups in
[
20
]
. We refer the reader to
[
34
]
for a detailed comparison with Tannaka-Krein duality for compact topological groups. The formulation of braided Hopf algebras and braided Tannaka-Krein reconstruction as we have presented here appears in
[
41
]
. The detailed proof of this result can be found in Chapter 9 of
[
46
]
, where the coend is is described in terms of natural transformations between certain functors.

We will not go into the details of the proof of Theorem
7.8
, but only present the structural morphisms of the resulting Hopf algebra in the case where the functor
\omega
is strict monoidal and additionally
\omega(x)^{\vee}=\omega(x^{\vee})
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
. If
\mu_{x}:\omega(x)\otimes\omega(x)^{\vee}\rightarrow H_{\omega}
denote the unique natural morphisms, making
H_{\omega}
the colimit of the diagram, then the Hopf algebra structure
(m,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon,S)
on
H_{\omega}
is defined via the unique morphisms satisfying:

\displaystyle m:H_{\omega}\otimes H_{\omega}\rightarrow H_{\omega}\ ;

\displaystyle\quad m(\mu_{x}\otimes\mu_{y})=\mu_{x\otimes y}\big(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(x)}\otimes\Psi_{\omega(x)^{\vee},\omega(y)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(y)^{\vee}}\big)

\displaystyle\eta:\mathtt{1}\rightarrow H_{\omega}\ ;

\displaystyle\quad\eta=\mu_{\mathtt{1}}

\displaystyle\Delta:H_{\omega}\rightarrow H_{\omega}\otimes H_{\omega}\ ;

\displaystyle\quad\Delta\mu_{x}=(\mu_{x}\otimes\mu_{x})\big(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(x)}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{\omega(x)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(x)^{\vee}}\big)

\displaystyle\epsilon:H_{\omega}\rightarrow\mathtt{1}\ ;

\displaystyle\quad\epsilon\mu_{x}=\mathrm{ev}_{\omega(x)}

\displaystyle S:H_{\omega}\rightarrow H_{\omega}\ ;
