\displaystyle\quad S\mu_{x}=\mu_{x^{\vee}}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega(x)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(x^{\vee})\otimes\omega(x^{\vee\vee})})(\Psi_{\omega(x^{\vee})\otimes\omega(x^{\vee\vee}),\omega(x^{\vee})})

\displaystyle\quad(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(x)\otimes\omega(x)^{\vee}}\otimes\omega(\mathrm{ev}_{x})^{\vee})

where
x,y\in\mathcal{D}
. In
[
60
]
, a generalisation of Theorem
7.8
is described where
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category, not necessarily braided, with suitable colimits. The output in this setting is then a Hopf monad rather than a braided Hopf algebra. We will briefly review this theory with reference to our construction in Section
4.5
.

Assume
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category where
\otimes
preserves colimits in both entries. A
construction data
over
\mathcal{C}
consists of a strong monoidal functor
\omega:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{D}
is a small monoidal category and
\omega
factors through the inclusion functor
\mathrm{inc.}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Additionally, we assume that the following coends exist for every object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
:

T_{\omega}(X):=\int^{d\in\mathcal{D}}\omega(d)\otimes X\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}

(92)

Now we review the main result of
[
60
]
:

Theorem 7.9

(Theorem 3.3
[
60
]
)
.

Let
\omega:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a functor factorising through
\mathrm{inc.}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that the coends
T_{\omega}(X)
in (
92
) exist. In this case:

(I)

T_{\omega}
is functorial and comonoidal.

(II)

If
\mathcal{D}
is monoidal and
\omega
is strong monoidal then
T_{\omega}
also has a monad structure. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}^{T_{\omega}}
becomes isomorphic to the lax right dual of
\omega
.

(III)

If
\mathcal{D}
is left (right) rigid then
T_{\omega}
is right (left) Hopf.

The proofs of these results are rather lengthy and elaborate and we refer the reader to Section 3 of
[
60
]
for the full proofs. However, we will briefly review how the Hopf monad structure on
T_{\omega}
is defined with reference to our construction from Section
4.5
.

Let us denote the universal morphisms
\omega(d)\otimes X\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}\rightarrow T_{\omega}(X)
by
\psi_{d,X}
. Recall from Section
4.5
that any pivotal pair
(P,Q)
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to a strict monoidal functor
\omega:\mathrm{Piv}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. By definition the image of
\omega
falls in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}
and the coends
T_{\omega}(X)
become precisely the colimits
T(X)
described in Section
4.5
in this case: Objects of
\mathrm{Piv}(1)
are tensor products of several objects of the form
+
and
-
and their images under
\omega
took the form
P_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{i_{n}}
, corresponding to lists
i_{j}\in\{-,+\}
. Hence, we wrote the universal morphisms
\psi_{d,X}:\omega(d)\otimes X\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}\rightarrow T_{\omega}(X)
in the form
(\psi_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}})_{X}
. Similarly, it should be clear that by definition
T_{\omega}
is the colimit of a diagram in
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
with functors
F_{d}:=\omega(d)\otimes-\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}
and natural transformations
\omega(f)\otimes-\otimes\omega(d^{\prime})
and
\omega(d)\otimes-\otimes\omega(f)^{\vee}
corresponding to morphisms
f:d\rightarrow d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
. Note that
\omega(\mathtt{1})\otimes-\otimes\omega(\mathtt{1})^{\vee}=F_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
in Section
4.5
since
\omega
was strict monoidal. Since all morphisms in
\mathrm{Piv}(1)
were generated by the quadruple of duality morphisms we only needed to look at four families of parallel pairs to construct
T_{\omega}
in Section
4.5
.

With the above notation
T_{\omega}
obtains a natural comonoidal structure
(T_{2},T_{0})
where
T_{2}(X,Y)
and
T_{0}
are the unique morphisms satisfying

\displaystyle T_{2}(X,Y)\psi_{d,X\otimes Y}=(\psi_{d,X}\otimes\psi_{d,Y})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)\otimes X}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{\omega(d)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}})

(93)

\displaystyle T_{0}\psi_{d,\mathtt{1}}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{\omega(d)}

(94)

for all objects
d\in\mathcal{D}
. In the example of
\omega:\mathrm{Piv}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
the evaluation and coevaluation maps for arbitrary objects
d\in\mathrm{Piv}(1)
took the forms
\mathrm{coev}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
and gave rise to the same comonoidal structure described above.

If we further assume that
\omega
is strong monoidal, then
T
obtains a monad structure
(\mu,\eta)
where
\mu_{X}
and
\eta_{X}
are the unique morphisms satisfying

\displaystyle\mu_{X}\psi_{d,T(X)}(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)}\otimes\psi_{d^{\prime},X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)^{\vee}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{d\otimes d^{\prime},X}(\omega_{2}(d,d^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes(\omega_{2}(d,d^{\prime})^{-1})^{\vee})

(95)

\displaystyle\eta_{X}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathtt{1},X}(\omega_{0}\otimes X\otimes(\omega_{0}^{-1})^{\vee})

(96)

for any pair of objects
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
. In Section
4.5
, (
95
) translated to the universal property of
\theta
and (
96
) simplified since
\omega
was strict monoidal and thereby
\omega_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathtt{1}}
.

The isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{T_{\omega}}\cong\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{r,\mathrm{lax}}
is defined as follows: For any
T
-module
(X,r:T(X)\rightarrow X)
the object
X
obtains a lax right braiding
(r\psi_{d,X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)})(\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)\otimes X}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{coev}}_{\omega(d)})
. In the converse direction for any object
(X,\tau)
in
\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{r,\mathrm{lax}}
, we obtain a
T
-action
r
on
X
as the unique morphism satisfying
r\psi_{d,X}=(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{ev}}_{\omega(d)})(\tau_{d}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega(d)^{\vee}})
. In Section
4.5
, we observed this isomorphism by showing that each object
(X,\sigma)
of
\mathcal{C}(P,Q)
obtains a
T
-action via
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\beta_{\sigma}
and vice versa.

If
\mathcal{C}
is left or right closed then part (III) of Theorem
7.9
follows directly from the isomorphism of
\mathcal{C}^{T}\cong\left({}_{\mathcal{D}}{\omega}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{r,\mathrm{lax}}
and a symmetric version of Theorem
2.3
. However, when
\mathcal{C}
is not closed, the statement is still shown to hold in
[
60
]
and the inverses of the fusion operators of
T
are constructed as in the proof of Theorem
4.15
but with the additional use of the natural isomorphisms
\zeta:\omega(-^{\vee})\rightarrow\omega(-)^{\vee}
. For our choice of
\omega:\mathrm{Piv}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
\zeta
were simply the identity morphisms. We refer the reader to Section 3.8 of
[
60
]
for this description since the details of this construction go beyond the scope of this work.

In
[
60
]
it is also noted that if
\mathcal{C}
admits a braiding then the coend
T_{\omega}
can be written as
-\otimes B
for some braided Hopf algebra
B
. As seen in Section
4.5
, if
\omega
factors through the center of
\mathcal{C}
then one can produce an augmentation on
T_{\omega}
by using the braidings
\omega(d)\otimes X\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}\rightarrow X\otimes\omega(d)\otimes\omega(d)^{\vee}
as in Theorem
4.16
.

Note that when applying Tannaka-Krein reconstruction for ordinary Hopf algebras (
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}
) we obtain an embedding of
\mathcal{D}
into the category of comodules of the reconstructed Hopf algebra
H_{\omega}
. In the monadic setting, the category of comodules over
T_{\omega}
are defined as the left lax dual of the forgetful functor
U_{T_{\omega}}
and this embedding follows trivially. We will briefly discuss the notion of comodules in Section
8
. In the classical setting, we can also use a ‘Recognition Theorem’ to tell if this embedding is an equivalence when restricted to the rigid subcategory of comodule [Theorem 3
[
34
]
]. As far as the author is aware, such a result does not exist in the monadic setting for a general base category
\mathcal{C}
.

Duals and Cenetralisers of Hopf monads:
Before the work of Shimizu in
[
60
]
which we discussed above, Bruguières and Virelizier
[
15
]
studied the question of when the dual of the forgetful functor
U_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of a Hopf monad
T
could be identified as modules over another monad
Z_{T}
. In the Tannaka-Krein terminology, we would obtain
Z_{T}
as
T_{U_{T}}
if suitable coends exist. However, the work in
[
15
]
is presented from a slightly different point of view.

First note that for an ordinary finite dimensional Hopf algebras
H
, the dual of its category of finite dimensional modules can be identified with the category of modules over the dual Hopf algebra
H^{*}
. Hence,
Z_{T}
is generalising the dual Hopf algebra construction and since the latter structure on
H^{*}
only makes sense when
H
is finite dimensional, generalising this theory to Hopf monads only makes sense if we assume
\mathcal{C}
to be rigid. In this setting, an endofunctor
T
on a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
was called
centralizable
in
[
15
]
if for any object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exists an object
Z_{T}(X)
with a universal natural transformation
\phi^{X}_{-}:X\otimes-\rightarrow T(-)\otimes Z_{T}(X)
, such that for any other such pair
(\overline{Z(X)},\overline{\phi^{X}})
there exists a morphism
f:\overline{Z(X)}\rightarrow Z_{T}(X)
satisfying
\phi^{X}=\overline{\phi^{X}}f
. If
Z_{T}
exists,
\phi^{X}_{Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow T(Y)\otimes Z_{T}(X)
becomes a natural transformation in both entries [Lemma 5.2
[
15
]
] and the pair
(Z_{T},\phi)
is called a
centralizer
for
T
.

In principle, the universal property described means that
Z_{T}(X)=\int^{Y\in\mathcal{C}}T(Y)\otimes X\otimes Y^{\vee}
and given the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
we obtain an equality

Z_{T}(X)=\int^{Y\in\mathcal{C}}T(Y)\otimes X\otimes Y^{\vee}=\int^{(M,r)\in\mathcal{C}^{T}}U_{T}(M,r)\otimes X\otimes U_{T}(M,r)^{\vee}=T_{U_{T}}

See Lemma 3.9 of
[
15
]
for more details. Thereby, Theorem 5.6 of
[
15
]
follows as a consequence of Theorem
7.9
and
\mathcal{C}^{Z_{T}}\cong\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
.

Finally, we should note that
Z_{T}
was described in
[
15
]
as the monad whose module category recovers the
relative centre
to
T
, which was denoted by
\mathcal{Z}_{T}(\mathcal{C})
, rather than
\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}
. The relative centre is defined to have pairs
(M,\delta)
as objects, where
M
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
\delta:M\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow T\otimes M
a natural transformation satisfying
(T_{2}(Y,Z)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\delta_{Y\otimes Z}=(\mathrm{id}_{T(Y)}\otimes\delta_{Z})(\delta_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Z})
and
\mathrm{id}_{M}=(T_{0}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\delta_{\mathtt{1}}
. Using the adjunction
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
, we obtain a natural isomorphism between
\mathcal{Z}_{T}(\mathcal{C})
and
\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
: If
\eta
and
\epsilon
denote the unit and counit of
F_{T}\dashv U_{T}
, then we can define a pair of inverse functors
L:\mathcal{Z}_{T}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
and
K:\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{T}(\mathcal{C})
by

\displaystyle L(M,\delta):=

\displaystyle\big(M,(U_{T}\epsilon_{(N,r)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\delta_{N}:M\otimes U_{T}(N,r)\rightarrow U_{T}(N,r)\otimes M\big)

\displaystyle K(M,\tau):=

\displaystyle\big(M,\tau_{F_{T}(N)}(\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\eta_{N}):M\otimes N\rightarrow U_{T}F_{T}(N)\otimes M\big)

Note that since
\mathcal{C}
was assumed to be rigid for constructing
Z_{T}
, there was no difference between the dual and the lax dual of
U_{T}
, as noted in Theorem
2.1
.

8
Other aspects of Hopf monads

In this section, we briefly mention some other aspects of the theory of Hopf monads which we have not covered.

Comodules over bimonads:
For an ordinary bialgebra
H
, it is well-known that the category of
H
-comodules
{}^{H}{\mathcal{M}}
becomes isomorphic to the dual
\left({}_{{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}}{U}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\right)^{\circ}
of the forgetful functor
U:{}_{H}{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}
. The correspondence between
H
-coactions on
M
and braidings
M\otimes U\rightarrow U\otimes M
follows exactly as in the correspondence between
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
and
Z_{l,\mathrm{lax}}({}_{H}{\mathcal{M}})
. An interesting question is finding an appropriate notion of comodules over an arbitrary bimonad.

If we consider the bimonads arising from braided bialgebras
B
, we recover the notion of
B
-comodules by looking at
T(\mathtt{1})
-comodules. Therefore in the main references on Hopf monads
[
14
,
12
]
comodules over
T(\mathtt{1})
are called
T
-comodules, as in Section
7.2
. However, given a bialgebroid
B
over an algebra
A
, there exists a well-define notion of
B
-comodules such that the category of these objects again become isomorphic to the lax left dual of the forgetful functor
{}_{B}{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}

[
58
]
. Therefore, given a bimonad
T
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, it is more natural to define the category of
T
-comodules
as the category
\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
. This is the point of view which is taken in
[
60
]
. However as pointed out in Remark 2.13 of
[
60
]
, this definition is not appropriate for braided bialgebras in arbitrary braided categories.

Since the forgetful functor
\overline{U}:\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
creates colimits by Theorem
2.4
, it is natural to expect that for suitable choices of
\mathcal{C}
, the functor
\overline{U}
admits a right adjoint and that
\left({}_{\mathcal{C}^{T}}{(U_{T})}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{\circ}_{l,\mathrm{lax}}
can indeed be identified with the category of comodules over some bicomonad. For ordinary bialgebras this would be the bicomonad structure on
H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
, while for a bialgebroid
B
over a base algebra
A
it would be the bicomonad structure on the functor
B\times_{A}-
on
{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
. However, the existence of such a right adjoint has to be verified for specific examples using the adjoint functor Theorems and we can not make a general statement on the comonadicity of
\overline{U}
.

Double of a Hopf monad:
Given a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
, the Drinfeld double of
H
usually denoted by
D(H)
, is a Hopf algebra structure on the tensor product
H\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}H^{*}
with a quasitriangular structure and its category of representations recover the center of the category of
H
-modules
Z({}_{H}{\mathcal{M}})\cong{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
. If
T
is a centralizable Hopf monad on a rigid category
\mathcal{C}
with centralizer
Z_{T}
(replacing
H^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}-
from the ordinary case), one can obtain a Hopf monad structure on the endofunctor
Z_{T}T
[Theorem 6.1
[
15
]
]. This is done by first obtaining a comonoidal distributive law
\lambda:TZ_{T}\rightarrow Z_{T}T
using the universal property of
Z_{T}
and then defining the
double
of
T
as
D_{T}:=Z_{T}\circ_{\lambda}T
.

In particular, it follows that
\mathcal{C}^{D_{T}}\cong\left(\mathcal{C}^{T}\right)^{\hat{Z_{T}}}
where
\hat{Z_{T}}
denotes the lift of monad
Z_{T}
onto
\mathcal{C}^{T}
via
\lambda
, as discussed in Section
5.2
. As in the case with the ordinary double, there exists an isomorphism
Z(\mathcal{C}^{T})\cong\mathcal{C}^{D_{T}}
[Theorem 6.5
[
15
]
] and one can also obtain a quasitriangular structure on
D_{T}
. For more details on this we refer the reader to Section 6 of
[
15
]
. In
[
15
]
, this theory was utilised to define the notion of double for a braided Hopf algebra
B
in a rigid category
\mathcal{C}
which admits a coend
C
. The resulting double is a braided Hopf algebra structure on
B\otimes{}^{\vee}{B}\otimes C
rather than
B\otimes{}^{\vee}{B}
. In the case of ordinary Hopf algebras
C=\mathbb{K}
in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathrm{fd}}
, and the usual double is recovered.

Hopf Monads of Mesablishvili & Wisbauer:
In
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
, bimonads as discussed here are referred to as opmonoidal monads and the term bimonad refers to endofunctors
B
on arbitrary categories
\mathcal{C}
(not necessarily monoidal) which have a monad structure as well as a comonad structure along with a distributive law
\lambda:BB\rightarrow BB
satisfying a compatibility condition similar to the bialgebra axioms. An antipode is then a natural transformation
S:B\rightarrow B
and, under suitable conditions, it exists if and only if a version of the Hopf module Theorem holds i.e. the free functor
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{C}^{B}\right)_{\hat{B}}
is an equivalence, where
\hat{B}
is the lift of comonad
B
onto
\mathcal{C}^{B}
via
\lambda
.

Bimonoids & Duoidal Categories:
The theory of bimonoids and duoidal categories is very much parallel to that of bimonads. Duoidal categories, originally introduced as 2-monoidal categories, are categories with two distinct monoidal structures and a compatibility structure between them. A detailed discussion of these categories and
n
-monoidal categories can be found in
[
3
]
. These categories are not to be confused with monoidal 2-categories, which are 2-categories with a monoidal structure.

Explicitly, a
duoidal structure
on a category
\mathcal{C}
consists of two monoidal structures
(\otimes,\mathtt{1}_{\otimes})
and
(\star,\mathtt{1}_{\star})
along with a monoidal structure on the functor
\star:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, given by
\zeta_{X,Y,W,Z}:(X\star Y)\otimes(W\star Z)\rightarrow(X\otimes W)\star(Y\otimes Z)
and
\zeta^{0}:\mathtt{1}_{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathtt{1}_{\otimes}\star\mathtt{1}_{\otimes}
, where
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category with
\otimes
acting in each component. A bimonoids in a duoidal category is then an object with a monoid structure with respect to one monoidal structure
\otimes
and a comonoid structure with respect to the other monoidal structure
\star
satisfying an analogous version of the bialgebra axiom using
\zeta
. Such objects naturally give rise to bimonads on the category, with respect to the second monoidal structure
\star
. Braided monoidal categories and braided bialgebras become examples of this theory with with
\star=\otimes^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\zeta=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\Psi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. For a brief discussion of different notions of the Hopf condition for bimonoids, we refer the reader to Section 7.20 of
[
9
]
.

2-Categorical point of view:
The notions of bimonads and Hopf monads can be defined at a 2-categorical level. Monoidal categories can be viewed as 2-monoids (pseudomonoids) in the monoidal bicategory
\mathsf{Cat}
consisting of categories, functors and natural transformations. From this point of view, one can define a bimonad structure on a 1-morphism (a functor in
\mathsf{Cat}
). In a bicategory, one can defined analogous notions of a monad structure on any 1-morphism with the same source and target as well as that of comonoidal structure on any 1-morphism whose source and target carry pseudomonoid structures. Hence, one can study bimonads on any pseudomonoid in a monoidal bicategory. This was the point of view taken by P. McCrudden in
[
47
]
. The Hopf conditions in Definition
3.10
are particularly useful, since they naturally extend to this setting.

More recently in
[
10
]
, a Frobenius condition for pseudomonoids is discussed so that one can define a notion of antipode in the bicategory setting as well. This theory recovers the antipodes given in Section
3.2
for rigid monoidal categories as an example. Moreover, this point of view allows us to view bimonads as examples of bimonoids in a special duoidal category: For a
map-monoidale
(map pseudomonoid) in a monoidal bicategory, the category of 1-morphisms from the map-monoidale to itself and 2-morphisms between them, comes equipped with a duoidal structure. In particular, if the map-monoidale is a monoidal category viewed in
\mathsf{Cat}
, picking a bimonoid in the mentioned duoidal category is equivalent to picking a bimonad on the monoidal category.

Appendix A
Appendix: Hopf Adjunctions in Topos Theory

In this section, we present some occurrences of Hopf adjunctions in topos theory. Since the definitions of Hopf monads and adjunctions were introduced much later than the examples presented here, these examples are usually stated without reference to Hopf adjunctions. However, we hope that by presenting these examples in this survey, we provide an intersection of interests for topos theorist and Hopf algebraist. We use
[
32
,
33
]
as our main references on topos theory. Here, we will only care about the fact that topoi are cartesian closed categories and recall that the appropriate notion of morphisms between topoi are
geometric morphisms

f:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
which consist of a pair of functors
f^{*}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and
f_{*}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
where
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
and
f^{*}
preserves finite limits.

If
f:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a geometric morphism, then the monad
f_{*}f^{*}
obtains a strong monoidal structure since
f^{*}
preserves the cartesian structure by definition. Consequently, the fusion operator (
3.10
) is invertible if and only if the counit of the adjunction is an isomorphism, or equivalently the monad
f_{*}f^{*}
is an idempotent monad. The geometric morphisms satisfying these equivalent properties are called
geometric embeddings
or
geometric inclusions
. See Lemma A4.2.9 of
[
32
]
for more details. Any topological immersion
f:X\rightarrow Y
gives rise to such an embedding
\text{Sh}(X)\hookrightarrow\text{Sh}(Y)
where
f_{*}
is the direct image functor sending a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
to the sheaf
f_{*}\mathcal{F}
defined by
U\mapsto\mathcal{F}f^{-1}(U)
for open
U\subseteq Y
. See Example A4.2.12(c) in
[
32
]
for more details.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is called
essential
if
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint
f_{!}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Since
f^{*}
preserves finite products, then
f_{!}f^{*}
obtains a bimonad structure on
\mathcal{D}
. As far as the author is aware, geometric morphisms were
f^{*}
is also cartesian closed do not have their own name. However, an important class of geometric morphisms satisfy this condition. Namely,
locally connected
geometric morphisms, which are morphisms where the induced functors
f^{*}/c:\mathcal{C}/c\rightarrow\mathcal{D}/f^{*}(c)
for arbitrary objects
c
in
\mathcal{C}
are cartesian closed. This statement is equivalent to asking
f^{*}/c
to admit left adjoints in a compatible manner (see C3.3.1 in
[
33
]
). Consequently, for any locally connected geometric morphism, we obtain a family of Hopf monads on
\mathcal{D}/f^{*}(c)
. In particular,
f^{*}
preserves finite limits and sends the terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
to a terminal object of
\mathcal{D}
i.e
f^{*}(1_{\mathcal{C}})=1_{\mathcal{D}}
and
f^{*}/1_{\mathcal{C}}=f^{*}:\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{C}/1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}/1_{\mathcal{D}}\simeq\mathcal{D}
is thereby cartesian closed. Therefore, if
f:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a locally connected geometric morphism, then we obtain a Hopf monad
f_{!}f^{*}
on
\mathcal{D}
.

The classical of example for locally connected geometric morphisms are given by locally connected topological spaces. Given such a space
X
, we obtain a locally connected geometric morphism
f:\text{Sh}(X)\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
, where
f^{*}
is the constant sheaf functor,
f_{*}
the global section functor and
f_{!}
the connected components functor which sends a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
to the set of connected components of its associated étale space. See C1.5.9 of
[
33
]
for additional details.

Another class of essential geometric morphisms for which
f^{*}f_{!}
carries a Hopf monad structure are
connected
morphisms where
f_{!}(1)=1
. A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is called connected if
f^{*}
is faithful and full. In this case,
f^{*}f_{!}
again becomes an idempotent monad. See Lemmas C1.5.7 and C3.3.3 in
[
33
]
for further details.
